F^{\sharp}\colon F\Phi_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}F
. A functor of pairs is an
equivalence
if
its underlying functor is an equivalence of categories.

2.1.11
.

Observe that the collection of pairs (as in
Definition

2.1.10
) form
a
2
-category in a natural way. In particular, the notion of equivalence of
pairs is simply the corresponding notion of equivalence in this
2
-category.

2.1.12
.

In the context of
Definition

2.1.10
, we always identify a functor of
pairs with the functor between the corresponding underlying categories.

2.1.13
.

Let
(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
and
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},{\pentagon}^{\prime})
be two
pre-
(d+2)
-angulated categories. A
pre-
(d+2)
-angulated equivalence

between
(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
and
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},{\pentagon}^{\prime})
is an equivalence of
pairs

F\colon(\mathcal{F},\Sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})

such that, for each

(d+2)
-angle

x_{1}\to x_{2}\to\cdots\to x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{\eta}\Sigma(x_{0})

in
{\pentagon}
, the sequence

F(x_{1})\to F(x_{2})\to\cdots\to F(x_{d+2})\xrightarrow{\eta^{\prime}}\Sigma(F(x_{0}))

lies in
{\pentagon}^{\prime}
, where
\eta^{\prime}=F^{\sharp}_{x_{0}}F(\eta)
. If
{\pentagon}
and

{\pentagon}^{\prime}
are in fact
(d+2)
-angulations, we say that
F
is a

(d+2)
-angulated equivalence
or an
equivalence of

(d+2)
-angulated categories
.

2.2.
Constructions of
(d+2)
-angulated categories

We recall the two constructions of
(d+2)
-angulated categories that are most
relevant for the purposes of this article.

2.2.1.
Standard
(d+2)
-angulated categories

The following theorem of Geiss, Keller and Oppermann is one of the main sources
of
(d+2)
-angulated categories; in fact, this theorem was the main motivation
for introducing the notion of
(d+2)
-angulated category, and it also motivated
the definition of a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting subcategory (
Definition

1.3.1
).

2.2.1
[
GKO13
, Thm. 1]
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
be a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
. Then, the
pair
(\mathcal{C},[d])
is endowed with a
(d+2)
-angulation whose
(d+2)
-angles are
those sequences

c_{d+2}\to c_{d+1}\to\cdots\to c_{2}\to c_{1}\to c_{d+2}[d]

in
\mathcal{C}
which fit as the spine of a commutative diagram of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

in which the oriented triangles are exact triangles in
\mathcal{T}
and such that the
connecting morphism
c_{0}\to c_{d+2}[d]
is given by the obvious (shifted)
composite along the bottom row of the diagram.

2.2.2
.

We call a
(d+2)
-angulated category
(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon}_{\mathcal{F}})

standard
if there exists a fully faithful functor of pairs

\iota\colon(\mathcal{F},\Sigma)\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{T},[d])
with the following properties:

(1)

The category
\mathcal{T}
is triangulated with suspension functor
[1]
.

(2)

The essential image
\iota(\mathcal{F})
of
\iota
is a
d
-rigid subcategory of

\mathcal{T}
.

(3)

The image of the
(d+2)
-angulation of
\mathcal{F}
under
\iota
coincides with
the class of sequences considered in
Theorem

2.2.1
. More
precisely, the closure under isomorphisms of the class of sequences

F(x_{1})\to F(x_{2})\to\cdots\to F(x_{d+2})\xrightarrow{F_{x_{1}}^{\sharp}F(\eta)}\Sigma(F(x_{1}))

in
\iota(\mathcal{F})
that are the image of a
(d+2)
-angle

x_{1}\to x_{2}\to\cdots\to x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{\eta}\Sigma(x_{1})

that lies in the class
{\pentagon}_{\mathcal{F}}
coincides with the class of sequences
considered in
Theorem

2.2.1
.

In this case, we say that
\iota(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\mathcal{T}
is a
standard

(d+2)
-angulated subcategory
and call the class
{\pentagon}_{\mathcal{F}}
a

standard
(d+2)
-angulation
.

2.2.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
be a standard
(d+2)
-angulated category with
respect to a fully faithful functor
\iota\colon\mathcal{F}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}
into a
triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
. Since
\iota
is a functor of pairs

\iota(\mathcal{F})[d]=\iota(\mathcal{F})
and since moreover
\iota(\mathcal{F})
is
d
-rigid then

\iota(\mathcal{F})
is actually a
d\mathbb{Z}
-rigid subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
.

Theorems

1.3.9
and
2.2.1
combine into the following
result.

2.2.4
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
has finite-dimensional morphism spaces and split
idempotents. Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
be a subcategory that satisfies

\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}
and
\iota\colon\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}
the inclusion functor.
Then, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The subcategory
\mathcal{C}
is a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster
tilting subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
.

(2)

\operatorname{thick}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{T}
and the functor
\iota

exhibits
\mathcal{C}
as a standard
(d+2)
-angulated category; in other words, the
pair
(\mathcal{C},[d])
admits a
(d+2)
-angulation whose
(d+2)
-angles are given
as in
Theorem

2.2.1
.

Proof.

The implication
(
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
2
) is precisely

Theorem

2.2.1
. We prove the converse implication
(
2
)
\Rightarrow
(
1
). By
assumption,
\mathcal{C}
is a
d\mathbb{Z}
-rigid subcategory such that
\operatorname{thick}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{T}
and

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}[d]
. In view of
Theorem

1.3.9
, it is enough to
prove that the inclusion

(\mathcal{C}[-1]*\mathcal{C})\subseteq(\mathcal{C}*\mathcal{C}[1]*\cdots*\mathcal{C}[d-1])

holds. Indeed, let
x\in\mathcal{C}[-1]*\mathcal{C}
and choose an exact triangle in
\mathcal{T}
of the
form

x\to c_{2}\xrightarrow{f}c_{1}\to x[1]

with
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
. By axioms (

d

-TR1
) and (

d

-TR2
), there exists a

(d+2)
-angle in
\mathcal{C}
of the form

c_{d+2}\to c_{d+1}\to\cdots\to c_{2}\xrightarrow{f}c_{1}\to c_{d+2}[d].

Thus, since
\iota
exhibits
\mathcal{C}
as a standard
(d+2)
-angulated category,
there must exist a commutative diagram of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

where
x=x_{2.5}
. This shows that
x\in\mathcal{C}*\mathcal{C}[1]*\cdots*\mathcal{C}[d-1]
, as required,
see
Example

1.2.4
. This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

2.2.5
.

In the context of
Theorem

2.2.4
, the
condition

\operatorname{thick}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{T}

is not essential: We may replace
\mathcal{T}
by its full
subcategory
\operatorname{thick}(\mathcal{C})
, noticing that statement (
2
)
only depends on the latter subcategory (compare with
Theorem

1.3.9
(
2a
)).

2.2.2.
Amiot–Lin
(d+2)
-angulations

In addition to
Theorem

2.2.1
, an important source of
(d+2)
-angulated
categories is the following ‘higher-dimensional’ version of a theorem of Amiot,
who proved the result in the case
d=1
. We need some preliminaries to state the
result.

Given an algebra
\Lambda
, we let
\Lambda^{e}=\Lambda\otimes\Lambda^{\mathrm{op}}
be its
enveloping algebra and identify the category of
\Lambda
-bimodules with
that of (right)
\Lambda^{e}
-modules in the canonical way. Recall that a
finite-dimensional self-injective algebra
\Lambda
is
twisted

n
-periodic
if there exist an algebra automorphism
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
and an isomorphism

\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{n}(\Lambda)\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}

in the stable category of
\Lambda
-bimodules; here,
{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}

denotes the diagonal
\Lambda
-bimodule with right action twisted by
\sigma
.
Equivalently,
\Lambda
is twisted
n
-periodic if there exists an exact
sequence of
\Lambda
-bimodules

0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to P_{n}\to\cdots\to P_{2}\to P_{1}\to\Lambda\to 0

whose middle terms are projective
\Lambda
-bimodules. Similarly,
\Lambda
is

n
-periodic
if it is twisted
n
-periodic with
\sigma=\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}
.

On the other hand, let
\Lambda
be a basic self-injective algebra. By

[
Bol84
, Prop. 3.8]
, the map

\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Pic}(\Lambda),\qquad[\sigma]\longmapsto[{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}],

yields an isomorphism between the outer automorphism group
\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda)
of

\Lambda
and the Picard group
\operatorname{Pic}(\Lambda)
of invertible

\Lambda
-bimodules. In particular, every autoequivalence of the additive
category
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
is of the form

-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)

for some automorphism
\sigma
of
\Lambda
; notice also that the above
autoequivalence is isomorphic to the restriction of scalars

(-)_{\sigma}\colon P\longmapsto P_{\sigma}

along
\sigma
. In particular,
\Lambda
is twisted
n
-periodic if and only if
the
\Lambda
-bimodule
\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{n}(\Lambda)
is stably isomorphic to
an invertible
\Lambda
-bimodule.

We record the following observation for later use. Recall that the syzygy

\Omega(M)
of a module
M
is the kernel of its projective cover.

2.2.6
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
self-injective algebra that is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic. Suppose that

\Lambda
is connected and non-separable. Then, the following statements hold:

(1)

Every invertible
\Lambda
-bimodule
I
that is isomorphic to

\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)
in
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda^{e})
is already
isomorphic to
\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)
as a
\Lambda
-bimodule.

(2)

The
(d+2)
-syzygy
\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)
is an invertible

\Lambda
-bimodule.

Proof.

The proof of
[
Mur22
, Prop. 9.8]
applies verbatim.
∎

Recall that a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
is
separable
if it is
isomorphic to a finite product of matrix algebras over skew fields, whose centres are
separable field extensions of the ground field
\mathbf{k}
; in particular, if
\mathbf{k}
is
perfect, then
\Lambda
is separable if and only if it is semisimple (see for
example
[
Wei94
, Sec. 9.2.1]
). The following theorem of Green, Snashall and
Solberg (with some simplifications due to Hanihara) gives a convenient
characterisation of the class of twisted periodic algebras, see also the
comments in the proof of
[
CDIM
, Prop. 3.5]
.

2.2.7
[
GSS03
, Thm. 1.4]
and
[
Han20
, Cor. 2.2]
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional self-injective algebra and
n\geq 1
an
integer; suppose, moreover, that
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable. The
following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The algebra
\Lambda
is twisted
n
-periodic.

(2)

There exist an algebra automorphism
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda

and a natural isomorphism

\Omega_{\Lambda}^{n}\cong(-)_{\sigma}

of exact functors
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)
.

2.2.8
.

Suppose that the equivalent conditions in
Theorem

2.2.7

are satisfied and that
\Lambda
is connected and non-separable.

Proposition

2.2.6
shows that
\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)
is an invertible

\Lambda
-bimodule but it need not be isomorphic to
{}_{1}\Lambda_{\sigma}
.
Although these bimodules induce isomorphic functors on
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}\Lambda
, this
only permits us to conclude that

\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)\cong{}_{1}\Lambda_{\sigma\gamma}
where
\gamma

is a
stable
inner automorphism, that is an algebra automorphism such that
the restriction of scalars along
\gamma
is isomorphic to the identity
functor of the stable category
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}\Lambda
(compare
with
[
IV14
, Thm. 1.8]
for example).

Our interest in twisted periodic algebras in the context of
(d+2)
-angulated
categories stems from the following observation, which is essentially a
reformulation of
Proposition

2.1.8
.

2.2.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
be a
(d+2)
-angulated category with
finite-dimensional morphism spaces and split idempotents. Suppose that there
exists a basic object
x\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\operatorname{add}(x)=\mathcal{F}
and set

\Lambda\coloneqq\mathcal{F}(x,x)
. If
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable, then
\Lambda

is self-injective and twisted
(d+2)
-periodic.

Proof.

The proof is an adaptation of the proof of
[
Han20
, Prop. 3.1]
, which
treats the case
d=1
of triangulated categories. By assumption, the functor

\mathcal{F}(x,-)\colon\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\qquad y\longmapsto\mathcal{F}(x,y)

is an equivalence of categories; hence the finite-dimensional algebra

\Lambda
is self-injective by

Proposition

2.1.8
(
1
). In particular, there
are commutative diagrams of equivalences of categories
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
is an
algebra automorphism (compare
[
Bol84
, Prop. 3.8]
) and the functors in the diagram on the right are exact
with respect to the induced triangulated structures.

Proposition

2.1.8
(
3
) yields the existence
of a natural isomorphism
\Sigma^{-1}\cong\Omega_{\mathcal{F}}^{d+2}
of exact functors
on
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\mathcal{F})
and, consequently, there is also a natural isomorphism

(-)_{\sigma}\cong\Omega_{\Lambda}^{d+2}
of exact functors on

\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)
.
Theorem

2.2.7
then implies that

\Lambda
is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic, which is what we needed to prove.
∎

Combining
Theorem

2.2.1
and

Proposition

2.2.9
, we obtain an alternative proof of
the following result that emphasises the role of
(d+2)
-angulations in this
context.

2.2.10
[
Dug12
]
,
[
CDIM
, Prop. 8.5(a)]
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category with finite-dimensional morphism spaces
and split idempotents and such that there exists a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting
object
c\in\mathcal{T}
. Set
\Lambda\coloneqq\mathcal{T}(c,c)
. If
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is
separable, then
\Lambda
is self-injective and twisted
(d+2)
-periodic.

Proof.

Indeed,
Theorem

2.2.1
shows that the additive category

\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\operatorname{add}(c)\subseteq\mathcal{T}
is endowed with the structure of a

(d+2)
-angulated category with suspension functor
[d]\colon\mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{C}
. Both
claims then follows from
Proposition

2.2.9
.
∎

2.2.11
.

In
[
CDIM
, Prop. 8.5(a)]
, the authors assume that the ambient
triangulated category admits a Serre functor in the sense of
[
BK89
]
, but
this fact is not used in their proof.

2.2.12
.

Proposition

2.2.10
shows that the correspondence in

A
is well defined.

2.2.13
.

Let
\Lambda
be a basic finite-dimensional self-injective algebra and

\sigma
an automorphism. Notice that
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\mathrm{op}}
are
Frobenius algebras
[
Zim14
, Prop. 4.5.7]
and therefore so is their tensor
product
\Lambda^{e}
; in particular the projective
\Lambda
-bimodules are also
injective
\Lambda
-bimodules. Consider an exact sequence of

\Lambda
-bimodules

\eta\colon\quad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

with
P_{i}
projective-injective as
\Lambda
-bimodules for
0\leq i<d+1
. In
particular
\eta
, regarded as a complex of left
\Lambda
-modules, is
contractible
2
2

2

It suffices to assume that
P_{i}
is left projective for

0\leq i<d+1
(as a consequence
P_{d+1}
too), so that the tensored
sequence in (
2
) remains exact. This hypothesis is
missing in
[
Mur20b
, Prop. 5.6 and Rmk. 5.7]
. Nevertheless, any
extension has representatives satisfying this assumption, such as the
representative considered in the proof of
[
Mur20b
, Prop. 5.6]
.
. We will be mainly interested in the case that
\Lambda
is
twisted
(d+2)
-periodic with respect to
\sigma
and
P_{d+1}
is also a
projective-injective
\Lambda
-bimodule, so that
\eta
exhibits the existence
of an isomorphism

\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}
in

\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda^{e})
. Let

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)

and

\Sigma^{-1}\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)

its quasi-inverse. We define a class
{\pentagon}_{\eta}
of
exact

(d+2)
-angles
in
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
as follows: A complex of finitely
generated projective-injective
\Lambda
-modules

Q_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f}Q_{d+1}\to\cdots\to Q_{1}\xrightarrow{g}\Sigma Q_{d+2}

lies in
{\pentagon}_{\eta}
if it has the following two properties:

(1)

The extended complex

Q_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f}Q_{d+1}\to\cdots\to Q_{1}\xrightarrow{g}\Sigma Q_{d+2}\xrightarrow{\Sigma f}\Sigma Q_{d+1}

is exact (notice that this condition only depends on the pair

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
).

(2)

Denote the
\Lambda
-module
\coker{g}

by
N
. By construction, there is an exact sequence

0\to\Sigma^{-1}N\xrightarrow{i}Q_{d+1}\to\cdots\to Q_{1}\xrightarrow{g}\Sigma Q_{d+2}\xrightarrow{p}N\to 0

such that
f=i\circ\Sigma^{-1}p
. We require the above exact sequence to be
equivalent to the exact sequence

N\otimes_{\Lambda}(0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0)

in the extension space
\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)
. The latter
sequence is exact because, as we have pointed out above,
\eta
is
contractible as a complex of left
\Lambda
-modules.

2.2.14
.

In connection with
Remark

2.1.6
, given an exact sequence of

\Lambda
-bimodules

\eta\colon\quad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

and a unit
u\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
in the ground field the class

u\cdot[\eta]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)

is represented by the exact sequence of
\Lambda
-bimodules

u\cdot\eta\colon\quad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\xrightarrow{u^{-1}\cdot\iota}P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0.

Therefore, the class
{\pentagon}_{u\cdot\eta}
given by

2.2.13
coincides with
u\cdot{\pentagon}_{\eta}
.

We are ready to state the theorem(s) of Amiot and Lin.

2.2.15
[
Ami07
, Thm. 8.1]
and
[
Lin19
, Thm. 1.3]
.

Let
\Lambda
be a basic finite-dimensional algebra that is twisted

(d+2)
-periodic with respect to an algebra automorphism
\sigma
and let

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda).

Then, the class
{\pentagon}_{\eta}
of exact
(d+2)
-angles from

2.2.13
associated to an extension
\eta
with
projective-injective middle
\Lambda
-bimodules endows the pair

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
with the structure of a
(d+2)
-angulated category.

2.2.16
.

Notice that
Theorem

2.2.15
is
a priori
quite different from

Theorem

2.2.1
. Indeed, the former result does not require the
existence of an ambient triangulated category in which to embed

\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
. However,
A
and

Theorem

4.5.8
show that, if
\mathbf{k}
is perfect,
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)

embeds as a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting subcategory in an essentially unique
algebraic triangulated category in such a way that the
(d+2)
-angulated
structure from
Theorem

2.2.15
coincides with that from

Theorem

2.2.1
.

2.2.17
.

In the context of
Theorem

2.2.15
, we call the class
{\pentagon}_{\eta}
an

Amiot–Lin (AL)
(d+2)
-angulation
of the pair

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
.

The following result shows that the
(d+2)
-angulated structure from

Theorem

2.2.15
is independent of the choice of (truncated) projective
resolution of the diagonal bimodule.

2.2.18
.

Let
\Lambda
be a basic finite-dimensional algebra that is twisted

(d+2)
-periodic with respect to an algebra automorphism
\sigma
and let

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda).

Choose exact sequences

\eta\colon\quad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

and

\eta^{\prime}\colon\quad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to Q_{d+1}\to\cdots\to Q_{1}\to Q_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

of
\Lambda
-bimodules with projective-injective middle terms. Then, there
exists a morphism of exact sequences
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

where
u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
is a unit in the centre of
\Lambda
. In
particular, the
(d+2)
-angulations
{\pentagon}_{\eta}
and
{\pentagon}_{\eta^{\prime}}

of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
are equivalent in the sense of

Definition

2.1.13
.

Proof.

The Comparison Lemma for projective resolutions yields a morphism of complexes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

in which the morphism
\varphi
is an isomorphism in the stable category of

\Lambda
-bimodules. Since the algebra
\Lambda
is finite-dimensional, the
morphism
\varphi
can be replaced by an isomorphism in the category of

\Lambda
-bimodules without modifying the rows and the other vertical
morphisms in the diagram (except for possibly
P_{d+1}\to Q_{d+1}
), see
[
Che21
, Cor. 2.3]
. Also, it is easily
verified that there are isomorphisms of algebras

\operatorname{End}_{\Lambda^{e}}\!\left({}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)=\operatorname{End}_{\Lambda^{e}}\!\left(\Lambda\right)\cong Z(\Lambda),

where the rightmost isomorphism is classical. In particular, the isomorphism

\varphi
is given by right multiplication by a unit
u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
, which
we interpret as a natural isomorphism
u\colon\Sigma\Rightarrow\Sigma
in the
usual way. It readily follows that the pair
(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},u)
yields
an equivalence between the
(d+2)
-angulations
{\pentagon}_{\eta^{\prime}}
and

{\pentagon}_{\eta}
on the pair
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
.
∎

2.2.19
.

We mention that
Proposition

2.2.18
holds, more
generally, for locally bounded categories (or even more general objects) for which
one can lift stable isomorphisms between twists of the diagonal bimodule to
actual bimodule isomorphisms. We thank Martin Herschend for informing us that
the latter property holds for Krull–Schmidt categories whose quiver of
irreducible maps is connected, for such categories have local centre.

The following theorem is a
(d+2)
-angulated analogue of
[
Han20
, Thm. 1.2]
,
which treats the case
d=1
.

2.2.20
.

Let
\Lambda
be a basic finite-dimensional algebra such that

\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable. The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The algebra
\Lambda
is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic.

(2)

The additive category
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
admits the structure of a

(d+2)
-angulated category.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward adaptation of the proof of

[
Han20
, Thm. 1.2]
. If
\Lambda
is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic, then

Theorem

2.2.15
shows that the additive category
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
admits
the structure of a
(d+2)
-angulated category. The converse is precisely

Proposition

2.2.9
.
∎

2.2.3.
Twisted periodic algebras and higher Auslander–Reiten Theory

Although not needed in the sequel, the following discussion provides further
motivation for the study of twisted periodic algebras from the perspective of
Iyama’s higher Auslander–Reiten theory, see the introduction to
[
Iya07b
]

for the analogous discussion in the (
d
-)abelian case.

Let
\Lambda
be a basic Frobenius
algebra; for simplicity, assume that
\Lambda
is connected and not semisimple. The
Nakayama functor

\nu\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}D\Lambda\colon\operatorname{proj}\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{proj}\Lambda

is a Serre functor in the sense that there are natural isomorphisms

\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}\!\left(Y,\nu X\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\!\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}\!\left(X,Y\right),\qquad X,Y\in\operatorname{proj}\Lambda,

where
V\mapsto DV
denotes the passage to the
\mathbf{k}
-linear dual,
see
[
BO01
, Def. 1.2]
.

Suppose now that
\Lambda
is twisted

(d+2)
-periodic with respect to an algebra automorphism
\sigma
and let

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda).

Fix an arbitrary Amiot–Lin
(d+2)
-angulation on the pair

(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda,\Sigma)
. Following
[
IY08
]
, we set

\nu_{d}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-d}\nu\colon\operatorname{proj}\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}\Lambda.

Let
P^{0}
be an indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-module. Extend the map

{f\colon P^{0}\to\nu P^{0}}
that maps the simple top of
P^{0}
to the simple socle of
\nu P^{0}
to a

(d+2)
-angle

\nu_{d}P^{0}\to P^{-d}\to\cdots\to P^{-1}\to P^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}\nu P^{0},

which we may assume is minimal in the sense that all the morphisms lie in the
radical of
\operatorname{proj}\Lambda
. Since
(d+2)
-angles are
exact
[
GKO13
, Prop. 2.5(a)]
, the above
(d+2)
-angle is the first part of a
minimal projective resolution of the simple top
S
of
P^{0}
and,
simultaneously, of a minimal injective co-resolution of the simple socle

\Omega_{\Lambda}^{d+2}(S)\cong\sigma^{*}(S)
of
\nu_{d}P^{0}
.
Following the paradigm of classical Auslander–Reiten
theory
[
AR75
,
AR78
]
, the above
(d+2)
-angle can be regarded as the prototypical example of an

almost-split
(d+2)
-angle
in the sense of
[
IY08
, 3.8]
, see
also
[
Fed19
, Def. 5.1]
. For the equivalence between the existence of a Serre
functor and the existence of almost-split
(d+2)
-angles
see
[
RVdB02
, Thm. I.2.4]
for the case
d=1
and
[
Zho22
, Thm. 1.5]
for
the general case (see
also
[
Ami07
, Prop. 1.2.]
for a related statement).

3.
Enhanced
(d+2)
-angulated categories

3.1.
Pre-triangulated differential graded categories

We begin by recalling the notion of a pre-triangulated differential graded
category in the sense of
[
BK90
]
. We need a few preliminaries.

3.1.1.
Reminder on differential graded categories

We recall basic aspects of the theory of differential graded categories that are
needed in the sequel; we refer the reader to
[
Kel06
]
and the references
therein for details.

A
differential graded (=DG) category
is a category enriched in the
symmetric monoidal category
\operatorname{C}(\mathbf{k})=\operatorname{C}(\operatorname{Mod}(\mathbf{k}))
of cochain complexes of

\mathbf{k}
-modules (endowed with the usual tensor product of cochain complexes).
Thus, the morphisms between two objects in a DG category form a cochain complex
and the composition law satisfies the graded Leibniz rule. A
DG functor

is simply an enriched functor between DG categories. A
graded category
is
a DG category whose cochain complexes of morphisms have vanishing differential,
and a
graded functor
is a DG functor between graded categories. For
example, given a (
\mathbf{k}
-linear) additive category
\mathcal{C}
, there is a DG category

\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{C})
whose objects are the cochain complexes in
\mathcal{C}
and for

X,Y\in\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{C})
we let
\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\!\left(X,Y\right)
be the complex whose
component of degree
i
is the
\mathbf{k}
-module

\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\!\left(X,Y\right)\coloneqq\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}(X^{k},Y^{k+i})

of degree
i
morphisms of graded objects
X\to Y
and differential

\partial\colon f\longmapsto d_{Y}f-(-1)^{i}fd_{X},\qquad f\in\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\!\left(X,Y\right).

Note also that the usual shift of cochain complexes

(X,d_{X})\longmapsto X[1]\coloneqq(X(1),-d_{X}),

where
X\mapsto X(1)
denotes the shift of graded objects, can be promoted to a
DG functor

[1]\colon\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{C}),\qquad X\longmapsto X[1]

whose action on morphisms is given by

[1]\colon f\longmapsto(-1)^{i}f,\qquad f\in\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\!\left(X,Y\right).

A DG category
\mathcal{A}
has two associated categories whose objects are the same as
those of
\mathcal{A}
: the
underlying category
\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\mathcal{A})
and the
0
-th
cohomology category
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
. As the notation suggests, the morphisms in

\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\mathcal{A})
are the
\mathbf{k}
-modules of cocycles

\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\mathcal{A})(x,y)\coloneqq\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\mathcal{A}(x,y)),\qquad x,y\in\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\mathcal{A}),

while the morphisms in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
are the cohomology
\mathbf{k}
-modules

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})(x,y)\coloneqq H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(x,y)),\qquad x,y\in H^{0}(\mathcal{A}).

Similarly,
\mathcal{A}
has an associated graded category
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
whose objects are
the same as those of
\mathcal{A}
and with graded
\mathbf{k}
-modules of morphisms

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})(x,y)\coloneqq H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}(x,y)),\qquad x,y\in H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}).

A DG functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
induces apparent functors

\operatorname{Z}^{0}(F)\colon\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\mathcal{B})\qquad\text{and}\qquad H^{0}(F)\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{B})

as well as a graded functor

H^{\bullet}(F)\colon H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}).

A DG functor
F
is a
quasi-equivalence
if the induced functor
H^{\bullet}(F)

is an equivalence of graded categories.

For example, if
\mathcal{C}
is an additive category then

\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{C}))=\operatorname{C}(\mathcal{C})

while

H^{0}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{C}))=\operatorname{K}(\mathcal{C})

is the homotopy category of cochain complexes in
\mathcal{C}
. Similarly, the graded
category
H^{\bullet}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{C}))
has graded
\mathbf{k}
-modules of morphisms

H^{i}(\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\!\left(X,Y\right))=\operatorname{K}(\mathcal{C})(X,Y[i]),\qquad X,Y\in\operatorname{C}(\mathcal{C}),\ i\in\mathbb{Z}.

Under these identifications, the shift (DG) functor on
\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{C})
induces the
usual shift functors on
\operatorname{C}(\mathcal{C})
and
\operatorname{K}(\mathcal{C})
.

3.1.2.
The homotopy category of small DG categories

As with quasi-isomorphisms between cochain complexes, quasi-equivalences between
DG categories need not admit a quasi-inverse given by a DG functor. However, the
category
\operatorname{dgcat}
of small DG categories admits a cofibrantly generated Quillen
model category structure, called the
Tabuada model structure
, whose weak
equivalences are the quasi-equivalences (we do not need to recall here what are
the classes of (co)fibrations)
[
Tab05
]
. We denote the homotopy category of
the Tabuada model structure by
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
, which is, by the general theory of
Quillen model categories, equivalent to the localisation of
\operatorname{dgcat}
at the
class of quasi-equivalences. In particular, two small DG categories
\mathcal{A}
and

\mathcal{B}
are isomorphic in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
if and only if they are connected by a
finite zig-zag of quasi-equivalences. Furthermore, the set of morphisms

\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
is in bijection with the set of quasi-isomorphism
classes of DG
\mathcal{A}
-
\mathcal{B}
-bimodules
X
such that, for each
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the DG

\mathcal{B}
-module
X(-,a)
is quasi-isomorphic to a representable DG
\mathcal{B}
-module,
see
[
Toë11
, Cor. 4.8]
.

3.1.3.
The derived category of a DG category

We recall the construction of the derived category of a (small) DG category. We
refer the reader
[
Kel94
]
for details. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small DG category, that
is the objects in
\mathcal{A}
form a set. The
opposite DG category
A^{\mathrm{op}}
of

\mathcal{A}
is the DG category with the same objects as
\mathcal{A}
and graded morphism
spaces

\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}(x,y)\coloneqq\mathcal{A}(y,x),\qquad x,y\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}};

the composition law in
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
is given in terms of the composition law in
\mathcal{A}

by the formula

g\circ^{\mathrm{op}}f\coloneqq(-1)^{ij}f\circ g

whenever
f
and
g
are homogeneous of degree
i
and
j
, respectively.
The
DG category of (right) DG

\mathcal{A}
-modules
is the DG category

\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A})\coloneqq\operatorname{Fun}_{\mathrm{dg}}\!\left(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}},\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathbf{k})\right)

of DG functors
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathbf{k})=\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\operatorname{Mod}\mathbf{k})
. We denote the morphism complexes in

\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A})
by

\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\!\left(M,N\right),\qquad M,N\in\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}).

The DG Yoneda embedding

\mathbf{h}\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}),\qquad x\longmapsto\mathcal{A}(-,x).

identifies
\mathcal{A}
with full DG subcategory of
\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A})
.

The underlying category

\operatorname{dgMod}(\mathcal{A})\coloneqq\operatorname{Z}^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))

is a Grothendieck abelian category. Moreover,
\operatorname{dgMod}(\mathcal{A})
is a Frobenius exact
category in which a short exact sequence

0\to L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\to}}M\to N\to 0

of DG
\mathcal{A}
-modules is admissible (=a conflation) if there exists a morphism

p\colon M\to L
of graded (!)
\mathcal{A}
-modules such that
pi=\mathbf{1}_{L}
; a DG

\mathcal{A}
-module is projective-injective in this exact structure if and only if it is

contractible
, that is if it is a zero object in the homotopy category

H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
. In particular, the stable category of the Frobenius
exact category
\operatorname{dgMod}(\mathcal{A})
equals
H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
and is a
triangulated category with suspension functor induced by the shift functor on

\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathbf{k})
by means of the formula

[1]\colon M\longmapsto M[1]\coloneqq[1]\circ M,\qquad M\in\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}),

keeping in mind that a DG
\mathcal{A}
-module is a DG functor
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathbf{k})
. By
construction, there are canonical isomorphisms

H^{i}(\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\!\left(M,N\right))\cong H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))(M,N[i]),\qquad M,N\in\operatorname{dgMod}(\mathcal{A})

and, as a consequence of the DG Yoneda Lemma,

H^{i}(\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\!\left(\mathbf{h}_{x},M\right))\cong H^{i}(M_{x}),\qquad x\in\mathcal{A},\ M\in\operatorname{dgMod}(\mathcal{A}).

A DG
\mathcal{A}
-module
N
is
acyclic
if for each
x\in\mathcal{A}
the cochain complex
of
\mathbf{k}
-modules
N_{x}
is acyclic. The
derived category
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
of

\mathcal{A}
is the full subcategory of the triangulated category

H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
spanned by the DG
\mathcal{A}
-modules that are
DG
projective
, that is the DG
\mathcal{A}
-modules
P
such that every epimorphism
M\to P
with acyclic kernel is split. The derived category of
\mathcal{A}
is a triangulated
subcategory of
H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
that is closed under small coproducts
and retracts and is compactly generated by the
free DG
\mathcal{A}
-modules

\mathbf{h}_{x}=\mathcal{A}(-,x)\colon y\longmapsto\mathcal{A}(y,x),\qquad x\in\mathcal{A}.

The
perfect derived category
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
of
\mathcal{A}
is the full
subcategory of
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
spanned by the compact objects; equivalently,

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
is the thick subcategory of
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
generated by the free
DG
\mathcal{A}
-modules
[
Kel94
, Thm. 5.3]
. In particular,
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
is a
triangulated category in which idempotents split.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a DG functor. The restriction of scalars

F^{*}\colon\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{B})\longrightarrow\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A})

induces an exact functor

F^{*}\colon\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{B})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})

that admits an exact left adjoint

\mathbb{L}F_{!}\colon\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{B})

that preserves small co-products (constructed explicitly as a left derived
functor). Moreover, the functor
\mathbb{L}F_{!}
preserves compact objects (since
it preserves the free DG modules) and hence restricts to an exact functor

\mathbb{L}F_{!}\colon\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{B})

between the corresponding perfect derived categories.

3.1.4.
Pre-triangulated DG categories

The following definitions motivate our definition of DG enhancement of a

(d+2)
-angulated category (see

Definitions

3.2.1
and
3.2.5
).

3.1.1
[
BK90
]
.

A small DG category
\mathcal{A}
is
pre-triangulated
if the fully faithful
functor

H^{0}(\mathbf{h})\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})

identifies
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
with a triangulated subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{A}
is
Karoubian pre-triangulated
if the above functor is
an equivalence of categories.

3.1.2
.

The term
pre-triangulated
is also used for triangulated categories
which need not satisfy the octahedral axiom
[
Nee01
, Def. 1.1.2]
, see also

Definition

2.1.1
and
Remark

2.1.5
. Despite the clash of
terminology, it is always clear from the context which notion is in use. In
particular, notice that the
0
-th cohomology category of a pre-triangulated
DG category is a triangulated category (and therefore satisfies the octahedral
axiom).

3.1.3
[
BK90
]
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category.

(1)

A
(DG) enhancement of
\mathcal{T}
is a pre-triangulated DG category
\mathcal{A}

such that there exists an equivalence of triangulated categories

\mathcal{T}\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{A})

(notice that we only require the existence of such an equivalence, and not
the datum of a preferred such).

(2)

We say that
\mathcal{T}
admits a unique (DG) enhancement
if it admits an
enhancement and any two enhancements of
\mathcal{T}
are quasi-equivalent (that is,
isomorphic in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
).

3.1.5.
Morita equivalences

The category of small DG categories and DG functors between them admits a
cofibrantly generated Quillen model category structure, called the Morita model
structure
[
Tab05
, Thm. 5.3]
, see also

[
Tab06
,
Tab07a
]
,
whose weak equivalences are the
Morita equivalences
, that is the DG
functors
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
such that the induced exact functor

\mathbb{L}F_{!}\colon\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{B})

is an equivalence of triangulated categories. A DG category is fibrant in the
Morita model structure if and only if it is Karoubian pre-triangulated; more
generally, the DG Yoneda embedding

\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A})

is a fibrant replacement (that is a Morita equivalence with fibrant target) for
every small DG category
\mathcal{A}
, where
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A})
is the
perfect
derived DG category of
\mathcal{A}
, that is the full DG subcategory of

\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A})
spanned by the DG
\mathcal{A}
-modules that are DG projective and
compact in
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
. The
Morita category of small DG categories
is
the homotopy category
\operatorname{Hmo}
associated to the Morita model structure. Two DG
categories are
Morita equivalent
if they are isomorphic in
\operatorname{Hmo}
. In
particular, every small DG category is Morita equivalent to its perfect derived
DG category.

3.1.4
.

If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are small DG categories that are Morita equivalent, then the
perfect derived categories
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
and
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{B})
are
equivalent as triangulated categories. The converse, however, is
false
[
DS07
,
Sch02
,
Kaj13
,
RVdB19
]
.

3.1.5
.

Every quasi-equivalence between small DG categories is also a Morita
equivalence. Closely related to this is the fact that
\operatorname{Hmo}
is equivalent to
the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
spanned by the Karoubian
pre-triangulated DG categories. In particular, two Karoubian pre-triangulated
DG categories are quasi-equivalent if and only if they are Morita equivalent.

3.1.6
[
Kel06
, Thm. 3.8]
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a Karoubian pre-triangulated DG category and
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
a
full DG subcategory such that
\operatorname{thick}(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))=H^{0}(\mathcal{X})
. Then, the
inclusion functor
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a Morita equivalence and, in
particular, there are canonical equivalences of triangulated categories

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{X})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{X}).

3.1.7
.

Given a DG algebra
A
, there are canonical morphisms of DG algebras (=DG
functors between DG categories with a single object)

H^{0}(A)\longleftarrow\tau^{\leq 0}A\longrightarrow A,

where

(\tau^{\leq 0}A)^{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}A^{i}&i<0,\\
\operatorname{Z}^{0}(A)&i=0,\\
0&i>0.\end{cases}

By construction, the map
\tau^{\leq 0}A\to A
is a quasi-isomorphism if and
only if the cohomology of
A
is concentrated in non-positive degrees. In particular,
if
H^{\bullet}(A)=H^{0}(A)
is concentrated in degree
0
, then both of the above
maps are quasi-isomorphisms of DG algebras and thus also Morita equivalences.
Combining this observation with
Theorem

3.1.6
, we conclude that the
(compact) derived category of an ordinary algebra admits a unique enhancement
in the sense of
Definition

3.1.3
.

3.1.8
.

In contrast to the situation described in
Example

3.1.7

for the derived category of an ordinary algebra, establishing the uniqueness
of enhancements for more general triangulated categories is a subtle
endeavour. Moreover, the majority of results of this kind that are available
rely on the existence of
t
-structures in a crucial way, see for example

[
LO10
,
CS18
,
CNS22
]
. Similar to the situation in
[
Mur22
]
, in this
article we consider triangulated categories that need not admit any
non-trivial
t
-structures (such as
2
-Calabi–Yau triangulated categories
with a
2
-cluster tilting object, see
Theorem

6.1.5
and compare
with
[
ZZ
, Thm. 4.1]
).

3.2.
Pre-
(d+2)
-angulated differential graded categories

The following are straightforward extensions of

Definitions

3.1.1
and
3.1.3
to the context of

(d+2)
-angulated categories.

3.2.1
.

A small DG category
\mathcal{A}
is
pre-
(d+2)
-angulated
if the fully faithful
functor

H^{0}(\mathbf{h})\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})

identifies identifies
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
with a standard
(d+2)
-angulated
subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
(see
Definition

2.2.2
).
Similarly,
\mathcal{A}
is
Karoubian pre-
(d+2)
-angulated
if it is
pre-
(d+2)
-angulated and idempotents split in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.

3.2.2
.

A (Karoubian) pre-3-angulated DG category is precisely a (Karoubian)
pre-triangulated DG category.

3.2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a pre-
(d+2)
-angulated DG category. Since, by definition,

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to a
d
-rigid subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})

closed under the action of the
d
-fold suspension and its inverse, the
standard isomorphisms

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})}\!\left(\mathbf{h}_{x},\mathbf{h}_{y}{[i]}\right)\cong H^{i}(\mathcal{A}(x,y)),\qquad x,y\in\mathcal{A},

imply that

\forall i\not\in d\mathbb{Z},\qquad H^{i}(\mathcal{A}(x,y))=0.

This is a strong restriction on the cohomology of
\mathcal{A}
(see also

Definition

4.3.2
).

3.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small DG category such that
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is an additive
category with finite-dimensional morphism spaces and split idempotents. The
following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The DG category
\mathcal{A}
is Karoubian pre-
(d+2)
-angulated.

(2)

The fully faithful functor

H^{0}(\mathbf{h})\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})

identifies
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
with a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting
subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

Notice that, since
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
is generated as a triangulated category
with split idempotents by the free DG
\mathcal{A}
-modules, the finiteness assumption
on
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
implies that
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
has finite-dimensional morphism
spaces. The equivalence between the two conditions then follows from

Theorem

2.2.4
.
∎

3.2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
(d+2)
-angulated category.

(1)

A
(DG) enhancement of
\mathcal{F}
is a pre-
(d+2)
-angulated DG category

\mathcal{A}
such that there exists an equivalence of
(d+2)
-angulated categories

\mathcal{F}\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),

where the
(d+2)
-angulation on the right-hand side is induced by the
triangulation on
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
as in
Theorem

2.2.1
. If
\mathcal{F}
has an enhancement we say that it is
algebraic.

(2)

We say that
\mathcal{F}

admits a unique (DG) enhancement
if it admits an enhancement and any two
enhancements of
\mathcal{F}
are quasi-equivalent (that is isomorphic in

\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
).

The following two questions are natural.

3.2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
(d+2)
-angulated category. Does
\mathcal{F}
admit an enhancement? If
it does, under which conditions is the enhancement unique?

3.2.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)
be a pair consisting of an additive category
\mathcal{C}
and an
autoequivalence
\Sigma\colon\mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{C}
. We say that a pre-
(d+2)
-angulated
DG category
\mathcal{A}

induces a (DG) enhanced
(d+2)
-angulated structure on

(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)
if there exists an equivalence
F\colon\mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A})

such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

commutes up to natural isomorphism. In this case, we say that the pair

(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)

admits a (DG) enhanced
(d+2)
-angulated structure
.

3.2.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)
be a pair consisting of an additive category
\mathcal{C}
and an
automorphism
\Sigma\colon\mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{C}
. Does the pair admit an enhanced

(d+2)
-angulated structure? If it does, is the enhancement of the underlying

(d+2)
-angulated category unique?

3.2.9
.

Theorems

4.5.8
and
5.1.3
provide a complete answer to

Questions

3.2.6
and
3.2.8
in the setting of

Theorems

2.2.15
and
2.2.20
.

3.2.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
be a
(d+2)
-angulated category and
n\geq 1
an
integer. Recall from
Example

2.1.9
that the pair
(\mathcal{F},\Sigma^{n})
is
endowed with an
n(d+2)
-angulation
{\pentagon}^{n}
obtained by extending the
sequences in
{\pentagon}
periodically up to iterated applications of
\Sigma
.
In general, we do not know if the existence of an enhancement of

(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
implies the existence of an enhancement of

(\mathcal{F},\Sigma^{n},{\pentagon}^{n})
. Notwithstanding, as explained in the
introduction,
Theorem

5.1.3
provides a
positive answer when
\mathcal{F}=\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
for some twisted
(d+2)
-periodic
algebra with respect to an algebra automorphism

\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
and

{\pentagon}={\pentagon}_{\eta}
is the AL
(d+2)
-angulation associated to an exact
sequence of
\Lambda
-bimodules

\eta\colon\qquad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

with projective-injective middle terms. Indeed, the algebra
\Lambda
is
twisted
n(d+2)
-periodic with respect to
\sigma^{n}
and the class

{\pentagon}^{n}
is precisely the AL
n(d+2)
-angulation associated to the exact
sequence obtained by splicing together the exact sequences

\eta,\ \Sigma(\eta),\dots,\Sigma^{n-1}(\eta).

Therefore, in this case, the
n(d+2)
-angulated category

(\mathcal{F},\Sigma^{n},{\pentagon}^{n})
admits an enhancement by

Theorem

5.1.3
. However, the precise
relationship between enhancements of
(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
and

(\mathcal{F},\Sigma^{n},{\pentagon}^{n})
is not clear (even if the semisimple algebra

\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable, so that we can guarantee that enhancements
are unique). For example, can one construct—explicitly—an enhancement of

(\mathcal{F},\Sigma^{n},{\pentagon}^{n})
from a given enhancement of

(\mathcal{F},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
?

4.
Higher structures and
(d+2)
-angulations

4.1.
Toda brackets and standard
(d+2)
-angulations

4.1.1
.

We fix a triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
with shift functor
[1]
in this
subsection.

We aim to characterise standard
(d+2)
-angles
(
Definition

2.2.2
) in terms of higher Toda brackets. We begin by
recalling the necessary definitions.

4.1.2
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

The
Toda family
\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
is the set of pairs

(\beta,\alpha)
such that there exists a commutative diagram of the form

(4.1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}

in which the horizontal row is an exact triangle in
\mathcal{T}
.

4.1.4
.

In the setting of
Definition

4.1.2
, notice that the Toda family

\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
is non-empty if and only if
f_{1}f_{2}=0
and
f_{2}f_{3}=0
.

4.1.5
.

An alternative (perhaps more suggestive) way to depict the condition for the
pair
(\beta,\alpha)
to lie in
\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
is as a commutative
diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-1}

in which
\beta
is a morphism of degree
-1
. Here, the oriented triangle is
an exact triangle in
\mathcal{T}
and the un-oriented triangles commute. In
particular, the pair
(\beta,\alpha)
yields the well-defined composite
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}

4.1.6
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

The
Toda bracket
\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle
is the subset of

\mathcal{T}(x_{d+2}[d],x_{0})
defined inductively as follows:

(1)

If
d=0
, then
\langle{f_{1},f_{2}}\rangle\coloneqq\left\{f_{1}\circ f_{2}\right\}
.

(2)

If
d\geq 1
, then

\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle\coloneqq\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}\langle{\beta[1],\alpha[1],f_{4}[1],\cdots,f_{d+2}[1]}\rangle

is the union of Toda brackets of length
d+1
.

4.1.7
.

Definition

4.1.6
is equivalent to that in
[
Shi02
]
, which is itself
based on that in
[
Coh68
]
, see
[
CF17
, Prop. 5.8]
. We also note that

Definition

4.1.2
is equivalent to the desuspension of that
in
[
CF17
, Sec. 5]
.

4.1.8
.

The Toda bracket is not quite a
(d+2)
-ary operation as its output is a

set
of morphisms rather than a single map (compare with

Proposition

4.1.31
).

4.1.9
.

The Toda bracket of a triple of composable morphisms

x_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

is the subset of
\mathcal{T}(x_{3}[1],x_{0})
given by

\langle{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}}\rangle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}\langle{\beta[1],\alpha[1]}\rangle=\left\{(\beta\circ\alpha)[1]\right|(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)\}.

4.1.10
.

The Toda bracket of the morphisms in an exact triangle

x_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{3}[1]

satisfies
\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}[1]}\in\langle{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}}\rangle
. Indeed, in view of

Example

4.1.9
, it is enough to observe that the tautological diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}}[1]}

exhibits the membership
(\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}},\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}})\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
.

The following classical result, which is a refinement of
Example

4.1.10
,
illustrates the usefulness of Toda brackets.

4.1.11
[
Hel68
, Thm. 13.2]
, see also
[
CF17
, Thm. B.1]
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{3}[1].

The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The above sequence is an exact triangle in
\mathcal{T}
.

(2)

The following two conditions are satisfied:

(a)

For each
y\in\mathcal{T}
, The induced sequence of
\mathbf{k}
-modules

\mathcal{T}(y,x_{3})\to\mathcal{T}(y,x_{2})\to\mathcal{T}(y,x_{1})\to\mathcal{T}(y,x_{3}[1])\to\mathcal{T}(y,x_{2}[1])

is exact.

(b)

We have
\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}[1]}\in\langle{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}}\rangle
.

The objective of this section is to prove the following
(d+2)
-angulated
analogue of
Theorem

4.1.11
(compare also with

Theorem

2.2.1
). As is customary, given a subset
X
of an abelian
group, we let

-X\coloneqq\left\{-x\right|x\in X\}.

4.1.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
be a
d
-rigid subcategory such that
{\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{add}(c)}
for some basic object
c
,

{\mathcal{C}[d]=\mathcal{C}}
and
\operatorname{thick}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{T}
. Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}

of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}[d],}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

with
d\geq 1
. The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The above sequence fits as the
spine of a commutative diagram of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

in which the oriented triangles are exact triangles in
\mathcal{T}
and such that
the connecting morphism
c_{0}\to c_{d+2}[d]
is given by the obvious
(shifted) composite along the bottom row of the diagram.

(2)

The following two conditions are
satisfied:

(a)

For each
y\in\mathcal{C}
, the induced sequence of vector spaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(y,c_{d+2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(y,c_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(y,c_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(y,c_{d+2}[d])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(y,c_{d+1}[d])}

is exact.

(b)

We have

\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[d]}\in(-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}i}\langle{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle.

Computing higher Toda brackets can be rather intricate. We establish a few
computation rules that are needed for the proof of

Theorem

4.1.12
as well as elsewhere in the article.

4.1.13
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}

and let
\varphi\colon x_{1}\to x_{1}^{\prime}
be an isomorphism. Then, there is an
equality of Toda brackets

\langle{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle=\langle{f_{1}\varphi^{-1},\varphi f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle.

Proof.

The claim is obvious for
d=0
for we have

\langle{f_{1},f_{2}}\rangle=\left\{f_{1}\circ f_{2}\right\}=\left\{(f_{1}\varphi^{-1})\circ(\varphi\circ f_{2})\right\}=\langle{f_{1}\varphi^{-1},\varphi f_{2}}\rangle.

To prove the claim for
d\geq 1
it is enough to show that

\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1}\varphi^{-1},\varphi f_{2},f_{3}\right).

Moreover, it is enough to prove that

\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)\subseteq\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1}\varphi^{-1},\varphi f_{2},f_{3}\right)

for the reverse induction is obtained by replacing
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
by

f_{1}\varphi^{-1}
and
\varphi f_{2}
, respectively, and
\varphi
by its
inverse. Indeed, given a pair
(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
there
is a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{T}
of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\varphi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v\varphi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}

in which the rows of the front face are exact triangles in
\mathcal{T}
(the top layer
exists by assumption). The bottom face of the diagram exhibits the membership

(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1}\varphi^{-1},\varphi f_{2},f_{3}\right)
, which is what we
needed to prove.
∎

4.1.14
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}

and let
S
be a set of exact triangles of the form

c\to x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\to c[1].

We let
\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}\subseteq\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
be the subset
of all pairs
(\beta,\alpha)
that are part of a commutative diagram of the
form (
4.1.3
) in which the bottom horizontal row belongs to the set

S
.

4.1.15
.

Let
d\geq 1
and suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

Let
S
be a non-empty class of triangles with middle morphism
f_{2}
as in

4.1.14
. Then, in the definition of the Toda bracket

\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle
, we can replace
\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
by
its subset
\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}
.

Proof.

Given an arbitrary exact triangle in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

c^{\prime}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}c^{\prime}[1],

there exists an isomorphism of exact triangles
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

where the bottom row is an exact triangle in
S
(chosen arbitrarily). Thus,
given a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{T}
of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}

there exists an extended commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta\varphi^{-1})[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi[1]}

Finally, since by
Lemma

4.1.13
there is an equality of
Toda brackets

\langle{\beta[1],\alpha[1],f_{4}[1],\dots,f_{d+2}[1]}\rangle=\langle{(\beta\varphi^{-1})[1],(\varphi\alpha)[1],f_{4}[1],\dots,f_{d+2}[1]}\rangle,

we see that we may go without of the pair
(\beta,\alpha)\in\mathcal{T}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})

by considering the pair

((\beta\varphi^{-1})[1],(\varphi\alpha)[1])\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}

instead. This finishes the proof.
∎

We now describe the action of the shift functor on the Toda brackets.

4.1.16
[
CF17
, p. 2709]
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

The
negative Toda family
-\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
is the set

-\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)\coloneqq\left\{(\beta,-\alpha)\right|(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)\}

(notice the unusual convention for the negative of a set).

4.1.17
.

In the setting of
Definition

4.1.16
, notice that

-\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},-f_{2},f_{3}\right).

Indeed, given a diagram of the form (
4.1.3
) exhibiting the
membership relation
(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
, there is a
commutative diagram in
\mathcal{T}
of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}

that exhibits the pair
(\beta,-\alpha)\in-\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
as a member
of
\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},-f_{2},f_{3}\right)
. The converse is then also clear.

4.1.18
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

Then, there is an equality of Toda brackets

-\langle{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle=\langle{f_{1},-f_{2},f_{3},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the number
n=2
of morphisms in the sequence. If

n=2
, then the claim is obvious. Suppose then that the claim holds for all
sequences of
n
composable morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
for some
n\geq 2
. Let

f_{1},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}
be a sequence of composable morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
. Then,
in view of
Remark

4.1.17
and the inductive hypothesis,

\displaystyle\langle{f_{1},-f_{2},f_{3},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\gamma)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},-f_{2},f_{3}\right)}\langle{\beta[1],\gamma[1],f_{4}[1],\dots,f_{n}[1],f_{n+1}[1]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\gamma)\in-\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}\langle{\beta[1],\gamma[1],f_{4}[1],\dots,f_{n}[1],f_{n+1}[1]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}\langle{\beta[1],-\alpha[1],f_{4}[1],\dots,f_{n}[1],f_{n+1}[1]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}-\langle{\beta[1],\alpha[1],f_{4}[1],\dots,f_{n}[1],f_{n+1}[1]}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}}\rangle.

The claim follows.
∎

4.1.19
.

Given a set
X
of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
, we let

X[1]\coloneqq\left\{f[1]\right|f\in X\},

and similarly for sets of pairs of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
(we are thinking of Toda
families as in
Definition

4.1.2
).

4.1.20
[
CF17
, Lemma 5.12]
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

Then, there is an equality

\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1}[1],f_{2}[1],f_{3}[1]\right)=-\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)[1].

4.1.21
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

Then, the following relation between Toda brackets holds:

\langle{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle[1]=(-1)^{d}\langle{f_{1}[1],f_{2}[1],f_{3}[1],\dots,f_{d+2}[1]}\rangle.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the number
n
of morphisms in the sequence. If

n=2
, then the claim follows from the functoriality of the shift:

\langle{f_{1},f_{2}}\rangle[1]=\left\{f_{1}\circ f_{2}\right\}{[1]}=\left\{(f_{1}\circ f_{2})[1]\right\}=\left\{f_{1}[1]\circ f_{2}[1]\right\}=\langle{f_{1}[1],f_{2}[1]}\rangle.

Suppose that the claim holds for all sequences of
n
composable morphisms in

\mathcal{T}
for some
n\geq 2
. Let
f_{1},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}
be a sequence of
composable morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
. Then, in view of

Lemmas

4.1.18
and
4.1.20
and the inductive
hypothesis, the Toda bracket

\langle{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}}\rangle[1]

equals

\displaystyle\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}\langle{\beta[1],\alpha[1],f_{4}[1],\dots,f_{n}[1],f_{n+1}[1]}\rangle[1]

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}(-1)^{n-2}\langle{\beta[2],\alpha[2],f_{4}[2],\dots,f_{n}[2],f_{n+1}[2]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}(-1)^{n-1}\langle{\beta[2],-\alpha[2],f_{4}[2],\dots,f_{n}[2],f_{n+1}[2]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,-\alpha)\in-\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}(-1)^{n-1}\langle{\beta[2],-\alpha[2],f_{4}[2],\dots,f_{n}[2],f_{n+1}[2]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta[1],-\alpha[1])\in-\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)[1]}(-1)^{n-1}\langle{\beta[2],-\alpha[2],f_{4}[2],\dots,f_{n}[2],f_{n+1}[2]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta[1],-\alpha[1])\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1}[1],f_{2}[1],f_{3}[1]\right)}(-1)^{n-1}\langle{\beta[2],-\alpha[2],f_{4}[2],\dots,f_{n}[2],f_{n+1}[2]}\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1}\langle{f_{1}[1],\dots,f_{n}[1],f_{n+1}[1]}\rangle.

The claim follows.
∎

We combine the above results into the following equivalent way to compute the
Toda bracket of a sequence of morphisms.

4.1.22
.

Let
d\geq 1
and suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}.

Then, there is an equality

\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}(-1)^{d-1}\langle{\beta,\alpha,f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle[1].

Proof.

According to
Definition

4.1.6
and
Lemma

4.1.21
, there
are equalities

\displaystyle\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}\langle{\beta[1],\alpha[1],f_{4}[1],\cdots,f_{d+2}[1]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}(-1)^{d-1}\langle{\beta,\alpha,f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle[1].\qed

4.1.23
.

Let
d\geq 1
and suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+2}[d].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

Suppose that the above sequence fits as the spine of a commutative diagram of
the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

in which the oriented triangles are exact triangles in
\mathcal{T}
and the morphism

f_{1}
is the apparent (shifted) composite along the bottom row of the diagram.
Then,

\mathbf{1}_{x_{d+2}[d]}\in(-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}i}\langle{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
d
. The case
d=1
follows from

Theorem

4.1.11
. Suppose then that the claim holds for

d\geq 1
and consider a sequence of morphisms
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2},f_{d+3}

that satisfies the conditions in the statement of the proposition. Thus, there
are exact triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i+1.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i+1.5}[1],\qquad{i=1,\dots,d+1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}

such that the triangles

\hbox to123.34pt{\vbox to50.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.67094pt\lower-22.54169pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{27.33824pt}{-7.21385pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\qquad i=2,\dots,d+1,

commute, where we set
x_{1.5}\coloneqq x_{1}
and
x_{d+2.5}\coloneqq x_{d+3}
,
so that also
g_{2}=f_{2}
and
h_{d+2}=f_{d+3}
. We also set

f_{1}^{(d)}=(u_{d+1}[d-1])\circ\cdots\circ(u_{3}[1])\circ u_{2}

and notice that
f_{1}^{(d)}\colon x_{2.5}\to x_{d+3}[d]
and

f_{1}=(f_{1}^{(d)}[1])\circ u_{1}
. Having fixed the necessary notation, the
induction hypothesis implies that

\mathbf{1}_{x_{d+3}[d]}\in(-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}i}\langle{f_{1}^{(d)},g_{3},f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2},f_{d+3}}\rangle.

We claim that
(f_{1}^{(d)},g_{3})\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
and therefore,
according to
Proposition

4.1.22
,

\displaystyle\langle{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2},f_{d+3}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}(-1)^{d}\langle{\beta,\alpha,f_{3},\dots,f_{d+2},f_{d+3}}\rangle[1]

\displaystyle\supseteq(-1)^{d}\langle{f_{1}^{(d)},g_{3},f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2},f_{d+3}}\rangle[1]

\displaystyle\ni(-1)^{d+2}((-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}i}\mathbf{1}_{x_{d+3}[d]})[1]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d+2}i}\mathbf{1}_{x_{d+3}[d+1]}.

To prove the claim, it suffices observe that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+3}[d+1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}[1].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
