\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{(d)}[1]}

This finishes the proof.
∎

We also need the following lemma.

4.1.24
.

Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{T}
of the form

x_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}x_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{3}[1].

The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

We have
\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}[1]}\in\langle{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}}\rangle
.

(2)

There exist an object
y\in\mathcal{T}
and morphisms
g\colon y\to x_{2}
and

h\colon x_{1}\to y[1]
such that the sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}\oplus{y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{3}\oplus{y})[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{3}&g\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{1}\\
h\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

is an exact triangle in
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathbf{1}_{x_{2}[1]}\in\langle{f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}}\rangle
if and only if there
exists a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{T}
of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta[1]}

in which the bottom row is an exact triangle in
\mathcal{T}
and

(\beta\alpha)[1]=\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}[1]}
or, equivalently
\beta\alpha=\mathbf{1}_{x_{3}}
. In
particular,
c\cong x_{3}\oplus y
for some object
y\in\mathcal{T}
. If we identify
c

with
x_{3}\oplus y
we see that the morphism
u
has components
f_{3}\colon x_{3}\to x_{2}
and
g\colon y\to x_{2}
for some morphism
g
. Similarly, under the
identification
c[1]=(x_{3}\oplus y)[1]=x_{3}[1]\oplus y[1]
the morphism
v
has
components
f_{1}\colon x_{1}\to x_{3}[1]
and
h\colon x_{1}\to y[1]
for some
morphism
h
. The claim follows.
∎

In the proof of
Theorem

4.1.12
we will use elementary properties of Poincaré polynomials of periodic graded modules.

4.1.25
.

Let
A
be a graded algebra with a degree
d
unit. The
Poincaré polynomial
of a degree-wise finite-dimensional
A
-module
M
is

p(M)=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\dim_{\mathbf{k}}M^{i}\cdot t^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[t]/(t^{d}-1).

We now show some elementary properties of these Poincaré polynomials. All proofs are simple, albeit somewhat tedious, exercises, so we only sketch them.

4.1.26
.

Given a graded algebra
A
with a degree
d
unit
u\in A^{d}
, the Poincaré polynomials of degree-wise finite-dimensional
A
-modules satisfy the following properties:

(1)

p(M\oplus N)=p(M)+p(N)
.

(2)

Given a short exact sequence
0\to M\to N\to P\to 0
,
p(N)=p(M)+p(P)
.

(3)

p(M)=0\Leftrightarrow M=0
.

(4)

p(M)\in\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
M^{i}=0
for
i\notin d\mathbb{Z}
.

(5)

If
p(M)\in\mathbb{Z}
and
N\subset M
then
p(N)\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(6)

p(M(-1))=t\cdot p(M)
.

(7)

p(M(d))=p(M)
.

(8)

Given an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

if
K=\ker g=\operatorname{im}f
then

p(M)+tp(N)-tp(P)=(t+1)p(K).

Proof.

The first five properties are obvious. For (
6
), we use the following three facts:

•

M(-1)^{i}=M^{i-1}
,

•

multiplication by
u
induces an isomorphism
M(-1)^{0}=M^{-1}\cong M^{d-1}
,

•

t^{d}=1
in
\mathbb{Z}[t]/(t^{d}-1)
.

Property (
7
) is a direct consequence of (
6
).
For (
8
), we break the exact sequence

0\to K(-1)\to N(-1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}P(-1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\to}}M\to K\to 0

into three short exact sequences and apply (
2
) and (
6
).
∎

4.1.27
.

Let
d\geq 1
. Given a graded algebra
A
with a degree
d
unit and an exact sequence of degree-wise finite-dimensional
A
-modules,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}

if
K=\ker f_{2}
then the following relation between Poincaré polynomials holds,

(-1)^{d}\left(p(C_{2})-p(C_{1})\right)+\sum_{i=3}^{d+2}(-1)^{i}p(C_{i})=\left((-1)^{d}-1\right)p(K).

Proof.

Consider
K_{i}=\ker f_{i}
and the short exact sequences

\begin{array}[]{rr}0\to K_{i}\to C_{i}\to K_{i-1}\to 0,&2\leq i\leq d+2,\\
0\to K_{1}\to C_{1}\to K_{d+2}(d)\to 0.\end{array}

Now it is just a matter of applying (
2
) and (
7
) from
Proposition

4.1.26
.
∎

4.1.28
.

Let
d\geq 1
. Given a graded algebra
A
with a degree
d
unit and exact sequences of degree-wise finite-dimensional
A
-modules,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{d.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{d-1.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

if
K_{i}=\ker g_{i}
then

p(M_{2})+p(M_{d+2})-p(M_{1})+\sum_{i=3}^{d+1}p(M_{i})t^{d+2-i}=\\
p(K_{2})+p(K_{d+1})+\sum_{i=3}^{d+1}\left(p(K_{i})+p(K_{i-1})\right)t^{d+2-i}

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

4.1.26
(
8
).
∎

4.1.29
.

In the setting of
Proposition

4.1.28
, if the
M_{i}
are concentrated in degrees
d\mathbb{Z}
then

(-1)^{d}\left(p(M_{2})-p(M_{1})\right)+\sum_{i=3}^{d+2}(-1)^{i}p(M_{i})=\left((-1)^{d}-1\right)p(K_{2}).

Proof.

By
Proposition

4.1.26
(
4
) and (
5
),
p(M_{i}),p(K_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}
. In the polynomial equation of
Proposition

4.1.28
, both polynomials must have the same coefficients in the same degrees. This translates into
d
equations involving
p(M_{i}),p(K_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}
. From these equations, it is easy to deduce the equation in the statement.
∎

We are ready to prove
Theorem

4.1.12
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
be a
d
-rigid subcategory such that
\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}
and

\mathcal{C}[d]=\mathcal{C}
, and

(4.1.30)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}[d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
with
d\geq 1
.

(
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
2
)
Suppose that the sequence (
4.1.30
) satisfies the conditions in

Theorem

4.1.12
(
1
). The
fact, for each
y\in\mathcal{C}
, the induced sequence of vector spaces

\mathcal{T}(y,c_{d+2})\to\cdots\to\mathcal{T}(y,c_{2})\to\mathcal{T}(y,c_{1})\to\mathcal{T}(y,c_{d+2}[d])\to\mathcal{T}(y,c_{d+1}[d])

is exact follows from standard arguments using that
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
is a

d
-rigid subcategory, see for example the first paragraph in the proof of

[
Lin19
, Lemma 4.6]
. That

\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[d]}\in(-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}}\langle{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle

follows from
Proposition

4.1.23
.

(
2
)
\Rightarrow
(
1
)
If
d=1
, then we are precisely in the setting of

Theorem

4.1.11
; hence, we may assume that
d>1
. Inductively,
we shall construct a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

with the following properties:

•

The triangles

x_{k.5}\xrightarrow{h_{k}}c_{k}\xrightarrow{g_{k}}x_{k-1.5}\xrightarrow{u_{k-1}}x_{k.5}[1],\qquad 2\leq k\leq d,

are exact, where
x_{1.5}=c_{1}
and
p_{2}=f_{2}
;

•

We have
\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[1]}\in\langle{u_{d},g_{d+1},f_{d+2}}\rangle
, although the
triangle

c_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}c_{d+1}\xrightarrow{g_{d+1}}x_{d.5}\xrightarrow{u_{d}}c_{d+2}[1]

is not (yet) known to be exact.

•

There are equalities
f_{k}=h_{k-1}\circ g_{k}
, for all
3\leq k\leq d+1
.

•

There is an equality
f_{1}=u_{d}[d-1]\circ\cdots\circ u_{2}[1]\circ u_{1}
.

In the first step we notice that, since the morphism

(-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}i}\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[d]}
lies in

\langle{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)}(-1)^{d-1}\langle{\beta,\alpha,f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle[1],

there exist a pair of morphisms
(f_{1}^{(d)},g_{3})\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
such
that

(-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}i}\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[d]}\in(-1)^{d-1}\langle{f_{1}^{(d)},g_{3},f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle[1]

or, equivalently,

(-1)^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{d}i}\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[d-1]}\in\langle{f_{1}^{(d)},g_{3},f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle.

In particular, there exists a diagram of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}[d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-1}

in which the oriented triangle is exact. Thus, we obtain a diagram of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+(d-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

We may then continue the inductive procedure with the sequence

f_{1}^{(d)},g_{3},f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}

(-1)^{1+(1+2)}\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[1]}=\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[1]}\in\langle{u_{d},g_{d+1},f_{d+2}}\rangle,

as required). It remains to prove that the sequence

c_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}c_{d+1}\xrightarrow{g_{d+1}}x_{d.5}\xrightarrow{u_{d}}c_{d+2}[1]

is an exact triangle. Since
\mathbf{1}_{c_{d+2}[1]}\in\langle{u_{d},g_{d+1},f_{d+2}}\rangle
,

Lemma

4.1.24
shows that there exists an object
y\in\mathcal{T}
and an
exact triangle of the form

y\oplus c_{d+2}\xrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{d+2}&g\end{smallmatrix}\right)}c_{d+1}\xrightarrow{g_{d+1}}x_{d.5}\xrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{d}\\
h\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(y\oplus c_{d+2})[1].

We need to prove that
y=0
; since
\operatorname{thick}(c)=\operatorname{thick}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{T}
it suffices
to prove that
\mathcal{T}(c[i],y)=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{C}[d]=\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{add}(c)
and
c
is basic,
c[d]\cong c
and the graded endomorphism algebra
A
of
c
in
\mathcal{T}
is of the form
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
in
Section

4.5.1
, so it has a degree
d
unit. Moreover,
M_{x}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{T}(c[i],x)
is a degree-wise finite-dimensional
A
-module for any
x\in\mathcal{T}
. We have to show that
M_{y}=0
. We will denote
p(x)=p(M_{x})
, and we will show that
p(y)=0
, which suffices by
Proposition

4.1.26
(
3
).

Applying
\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{T}(c[i],-)
to
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}

and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\oplus c_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d-1.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{d+2}&g\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{d+2}\\
h\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}

we obtain diagrams of
A
-modules as in
Proposition

4.1.27
and
Proposition

4.1.28
. By
Proposition

4.1.27
, if
K=\ker\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{T}(c[i],f_{2})
,

(-1)^{d}\left(p(c_{2})-p(c_{1})\right)+\sum_{i=3}^{d+2}(-1)^{i}p(c_{i})=\left((-1)^{d}-1\right)p(K).

Moreover, since
c_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}
is
d
-rigid,
M_{c_{i}}
is concentrated in
d\mathbb{Z}
. Hence, by
Corollary

4.1.29
,

(-1)^{d}\left(p(c_{2})-p(c_{1})\right)+\sum_{i=3}^{d+1}(-1)^{i}p(c_{i})+p(y)+p(c_{d+2})=\left((-1)^{d}-1\right)p(K).

Both equations are identical, except for the fact that the second one has an extra term,
p(y)
. Therefore,
p(y)=0
, as we wanted to prove.
∎

We conclude this section with the following result.

4.1.31
[
Sag08
, Prop. 4.10]
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
a
d
-rigid subcategory such that
\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}
and

\mathcal{C}[d]=\mathcal{C}
. Suppose given a sequence of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
of the form

c_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}c_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}c_{0}

Then, the Toda bracket
\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle
is an element of the
quotient

\operatorname{Toda}\left(f_{d+2},f_{1}\right)\coloneqq\frac{\mathcal{T}(c_{d+2}[d],c_{0})}{f_{1}\cdot\mathcal{T}(c_{d+2}[d],c_{1})+\mathcal{T}(c_{d+1}[d],c_{0})\cdot f_{d+2}[d]}.

4.2.
Massey products versus Toda brackets

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a DG category. We recall that, similarly to the cohomology of a
space, the graded category
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
is endowed with so-called Massey
products
[
BK90
, Sec. 5.A]
. Massey products are closely related to Toda
brackets. In fact, it is stated in
loc. cit.
that

“It can be verified that for an enhanced triangulated category the Massey
products coincide with the Toda brackets.”

As it turns out, this statement indeed holds, but only up to a suitable sign.
Since this agreement is crucial to our main results, we include a proof for the
convenience of the reader (see also
[
Bod14
]
for an alternative proof that
involves the language of twisted complexes).

We begin by recalling the definition of the Massey products, see for example

[
May69
]
.

4.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a DG category and suppose given a sequence in
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
of the
form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}

such that
f_{i}
is homogeneous of degree
|f_{i}|
, where
d\geq 0
.

(1)

A
defining system (for the above sequence)
is a set

\left\{g_{ij}\colon x_{j}\to x_{i}\right|0\leq i<j\leq d+2,\ j-i<d+2\}

of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
that are homogeneous of degree

|g_{ij}|=i-j+1+\sum_{i<k\leq j}|f_{k}|

with the following two properties:

(a)

The equality

\partial(g_{ij})=\sum_{i<k<j}(-1)^{|g_{ik}|-1}g_{ik}g_{kj}

holds (notice that
|g_{ik}|-1=i-k+\sum_{i<\ell\leq k}|f_{\ell}|
). In
particular,

\partial(g_{i-1,i})=0

so that
g_{i-1,i}
is a cocycle of degree

|f_{i}|
.

(b)

The morphism
g_{i-1,i}
represents
f_{i}
in
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
.

(2)

The
Massey product
is the subset of

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle\subseteq H^{-d+\sum_{i=1}^{d+2}|f_{i}|}(\mathcal{A}(x_{d+2},x_{0}))

consisting of the classes of the cocycles

\sum_{0<k<d+2}(-1)^{|g_{0k}|-1}g_{0k}g_{k,d+2}=\sum_{0<k<d+2}(-1)^{-k+\sum_{0<\ell\leq k}|f_{\ell}|}g_{0k}g_{k,d+2}

for all possible defining systems.

4.2.2
.

In the setting of
Definition

4.2.1
, the degree of the Massey product

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
is more recognisable by letting
n=d+2
so
that
\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}}\rrangle
is a subset of the cohomology of

\mathcal{A}(x_{n},x_{0})
in degree
2-n+\sum_{i}|f_{i}|
.

4.2.3
.

The Massey product
\llangle{f_{1},f_{2}}\rrangle
of a pair of (homogeneous)
composable morphisms in
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
is always defined and is given by the
singleton

\llangle{f_{1},f_{2}}\rrangle=\left\{(-1)^{|f_{1}|-1}f_{1}\circ f_{2}\right\}.

We see that, if
|f_{1}|=0
, the Massey product of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
is the
sign-twisted composite
-f_{1}\circ f_{2}
(compare with
Definition

4.1.6

and
Theorem

4.2.6
).

4.2.4
.

In the setting of
Definition

4.2.1
, if
d\geq 1
, then the Massey
product is defined (i.e. non-empty) if and only if

\forall 0\leq i<j\leq d+2,\;j-i<d+2,\qquad 0\in\llangle{f_{i},\dots,f_{j}}\rrangle.

4.2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small DG category. Suppose given a sequence of degree
0

morphisms

M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}M_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}M_{0}

in the triangulated category
H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
. By definition, their
Massey product satisfies

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle\subseteq H^{-d}(\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\!\left(M_{d+2},M_{0}\right))\cong H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))(M_{d+2},M_{0}[-d]),

while their Toda bracket satisfies

\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle\subseteq H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))(M_{d+2}[d],M_{0}).

Of course, we may compare both brackets by means of the isomorphism

H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))(M_{d+2},M_{0}[-d])\cong H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))(M_{d+2}[d],M_{0})

induced by the
d
-fold shift functor.

We shall prove the following agreement result for Massey products and Toda
brackets.

4.2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small DG category. Suppose given a sequence of degree
0

morphisms

M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}M_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}M_{0}

in the triangulated category
H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
. Then, their Massey
product and their Toda bracket are related by the formula

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle[d]=(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}i}\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle.

4.2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small DG category. Since

\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
is a full triangulated
subcategory,
Theorem

4.2.6
also establishes a relationship between
Massey products and Toda brackets in
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
.

For the proof of
Theorem

4.2.6
, we need the following observation. Given
a small DG category
\mathcal{A}
and a morphism
f\colon M\to N
in
\operatorname{dgMod}(\mathcal{A})
,
recall that the
cone of
f
, denoted by
\operatorname{cone}\left(f\right)
, is the DG
\mathcal{A}
-module
whose underlying graded
\mathcal{A}
-module is
M(1)\oplus N
equipped with the
differential

d_{\operatorname{cone}\left(f\right)}\coloneqq d_{M[1]}\oplus d_{N}+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
f&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-d_{M}&0\\
f&d_{N}\end{smallmatrix}\right);

above,
M\mapsto M(1)
denotes the shift of the underlying graded
\mathcal{A}
-module of
M
.
The definition above ensures that the canonical split short exact sequence of
graded
\mathcal{A}
-modules
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cone}\left(f\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

is in fact a (not necessarily split) short exact sequence of DG
\mathcal{A}
-modules.
The above sequence yields an exact triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cone}\left(f\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

in the triangulated category
H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
.

4.2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small DG category. Suppose given a sequence of degree
0

morphisms

M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}M_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}M_{0}

in the triangulated category
H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
, with
d\geq 1
. Let
S

be the set consisting of the exact triangles of the precise form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cone}\left(g_{12}\right)[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cone}\left(g_{12}\right),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi[-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\iota}

where
g_{12}
ranges over all degree
0
cocycles
M_{2}\to M_{1}
in

\operatorname{dgMod}(\mathcal{A})
that represent the morphism
f_{2}
. Then, the Massey product

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
satisfies the formula

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}}\llangle{\beta,\alpha,f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle.

Proof.

Let

\left\{g_{ij}\colon M_{j}\to M_{i}\right|0\leq i<j\leq d+2,\ j-i<d+2\}

be a defining system for the Massey product

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
. Define the morphisms in
\operatorname{dgMod}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}g_{23}\\
g_{13}\end{pmatrix}\colon M_{3}\longrightarrow\operatorname{cone}\left(g_{12}\right)[-1],

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}g_{02}&-g_{01}\end{pmatrix}\colon\operatorname{cone}\left(g_{12}\right)[-1]\longrightarrow M_{0}[-1].

Notice that
|\alpha|=0=|\beta|
. We claim that the pair formed by the
cohomology classes of these morphisms

(\left\{\beta\right\},\left\{\alpha\right\})\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}
. Notice first that

\alpha
and
\beta
are indeed cocycles since, in view of the equations
satisfied by a defining system, we have

\displaystyle\partial(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}d_{M_{2}}&0\\
-g_{12}&-d_{M_{1}}\end{pmatrix}\circ\begin{pmatrix}g_{23}\\
g_{13}\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}g_{23}\\
g_{13}\end{pmatrix}\circ d_{M_{3}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}d_{M_{2}}\circ g_{23}-g_{23}\circ d_{M_{3}}\\
-g_{12}g_{23}-(d_{M_{1}}\circ g_{13}+g_{13}\circ d_{M_{3}})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\partial(g_{23})\\
\partial(g_{13})-\partial(g_{13})\end{pmatrix}=0

and

\displaystyle\partial(\beta)

\displaystyle=-d_{M_{0}}\circ\begin{pmatrix}g_{02}&-g_{01}\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}g_{02}&-g_{01}\end{pmatrix}\circ\begin{pmatrix}d_{M_{2}}&0\\
-g_{12}&-d_{M_{1}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-(d_{M_{0}}\circ g_{02}+g_{02}\circ d_{M_{2}})-g_{01}g_{12}&d_{M_{0}}\circ g_{01}-g_{01}\circ d_{M_{1}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\partial(g_{02})+\partial(g_{02})&\partial(g_{01})\end{pmatrix}=0.

Here we use that
d_{M_{0}[-1]}=-d_{M_{0}}
. Thus, to exhibit the membership
relation
(\left\{\beta\right\},\left\{\alpha\right\})\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)
, it suffices
to observe that the diagram (see
Remark

4.1.5
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cone}\left(g_{12}\right)[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\{\alpha\right\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\{\beta\right\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi[-1]}

commutes in
H^{\bullet}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
, for
g_{01}
and
g_{23}
represent the
morphisms
f_{1}
and
f_{3}
and
\pi[-1]
and
\iota
are the apparent
projection and inclusion into the corresponding direct summands.

We wish to use the given defining system for the Massey product

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
to construct a defining system for the
Massey product

\llangle{\left\{\beta\right\},\left\{\alpha\right\},f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
. We claim
that the collection of morphisms

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{01}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\beta=\begin{pmatrix}g_{02}&-g_{01}\end{pmatrix}\colon\operatorname{cone}\left(g_{12}\right)[-1]\longrightarrow M_{0};

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{0j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq g_{0,j+1}\colon M_{j+1}\longrightarrow M_{0}[-1],\qquad d+2>j\geq 2;

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{1j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}g_{2,j+1}\\
g_{1,j+1}\end{pmatrix}\colon M_{j+1}\longrightarrow\operatorname{cone}\left(g_{12}\right)[-1],\qquad d+2>j\geq 2;

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{ij}

\displaystyle\coloneqq g_{i+1,j+1}\colon M_{j+1}\longrightarrow M_{i+1},\qquad d+2>j>i\geq 2;

is such a defining system. Notice that

•

\overline{g}_{01}=\beta
represents
\left\{\beta\right\}
;

•

\overline{g}_{12}=\alpha
represents
\left\{\alpha\right\}
;

•

\overline{g}_{i-1,i}=g_{i,i+1}\colon M_{i+1}\to M_{i}
represents
f_{i+1}
.

We now analyse the differentials
\partial(\overline{g}_{i,j})
in the four cases:

•

We have already shown that
\partial(\overline{g}_{01})=\partial(\beta)=0
.

•

If
j\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle\partial(\overline{g}_{0j})

\displaystyle=-\partial(g_{0,j+1})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{0<\ell<j+1}(-1)^{-\ell}g_{0\ell}g_{\ell,j+1}

\displaystyle=-\begin{pmatrix}g_{02}&-g_{01}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}g_{2,j+1}\\
g_{1,j+1}\end{pmatrix}+\sum_{2<\ell<j+1}(-1)^{1-\ell}g_{0\ell}g_{\ell,j+1}

\displaystyle=-\overline{g}_{01}\overline{g}_{1j}+\sum_{2<\ell<j+1}(-1)^{1-\ell}\overline{g}_{0,\ell-1}\overline{g}_{\ell-1,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0<k<j}(-1)^{-k}\overline{g}_{0k}\overline{g}_{kj},

where the equality
\partial(\overline{g}_{0j})=-\partial(g_{0,j+1})
stems from the
fact that the target of
\overline{g}_{0j}
is the DG
\mathcal{A}
-module
M_{0}[-1]
, which
has the differential
-d_{M_{0}}
.

•

If
i=1
and
j\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle\partial(\overline{g}_{1j})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}d_{M_{2}}&0\\
-g_{12}&-d_{M_{1}}\end{pmatrix}\circ\begin{pmatrix}g_{2,j+1}\\
g_{1,j+1}\end{pmatrix}-(-1)^{|\overline{g}_{1j}|}\begin{pmatrix}g_{2,j+1}\\
g_{1,j+1}\end{pmatrix}\circ d_{M_{j+1}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}d_{M_{2}}\circ g_{2,j+1}-(-1)^{|g_{2,j+1}|}g_{2,j+1}\circ d_{M_{j+1}}\\
-g_{12}g_{2,j+1}-(d_{M_{1}}\circ g_{1,j+1}-(-1)^{|g_{1,j+1}|}g_{1,j+1}\circ d_{M_{j+1}})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\partial(g_{2,j+1})\\
-g_{12}g_{2,j+1}-\partial(g_{1,j+1})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{2<\ell<j+1}(-1)^{2-\ell}g_{2\ell}g_{\ell,j+1}\\
-\sum_{2<\ell<j+1}(-1)^{1-\ell}g_{1\ell}g_{\ell,j+1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\sum_{2<\ell<j+1}(-1)^{2-\ell}\begin{pmatrix}g_{2\ell}\\
g_{1\ell}\end{pmatrix}g_{\ell,j+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{2<\ell<j+1}(-1)^{2-\ell}\overline{g}_{1,\ell-1}\overline{g}_{\ell-1,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1<k<j}(-1)^{1-k}\overline{g}_{1k}\overline{g}_{kj}.

•

If
i\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle\partial(\overline{g}_{ij})=\partial(g_{i+1,j+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i+1<k+1<j+1}(-1)^{(i+1)-(k+1)}g_{i+1,k+1}g_{k+1,j+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<k<j}(-1)^{i-k}\overline{g}_{ik}\overline{g}_{kj}.

This shows that the collection of morphisms
\left\{\overline{g}_{ij}\right\}
is indeed a
defining system for the Massey product

\llangle{\left\{\beta\right\},\left\{\alpha\right\},f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
.

Finally, the above process is clearly reversible: Given

(\left\{\beta\right\},\left\{\alpha\right\})\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}
and a defining
system for the Massey product

\llangle{\left\{\beta\right\},\left\{\alpha\right\},f_{4},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
, one obtains
a defining system for the Massey product
\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle

by means of the above formulas. This finishes the proof.
∎

We are ready to prove
Theorem

4.2.6
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.2.6
.

Given a sequence of degree
0
morphisms

M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}M_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}M_{0}

in the triangulated category
H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
, for
\mathcal{A}
a small DG
category, we need to prove that

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle[d]=(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}i}\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle.

If
d=0
, then the claim follows from the discussion in

Remark

4.2.3
. For
d\geq 1
, the claim follows from a
straightforward induction using

Propositions

4.1.22
and
4.2.8
and

Lemma

4.1.15
. Indeed, suppose that the claim holds for all
sequences of
n
composable morphisms for some
n\geq 2
. Let

f_{1},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}
be a sequence of composable homogeneous morphisms in

H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathcal{A}))
. Then, the
(n-1)
-fold shifted Massey product

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}}\rrangle[n-1]

equals

\displaystyle\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}}\llangle{\beta,\alpha,f_{4},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}}\rrangle[n-1]

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}}(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i}\langle{\beta,\alpha,f_{4},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}}\rangle[1]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i}(-1)^{n}\bigcup_{(\beta,\alpha)\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\right)_{S}}(-1)^{n-2}\langle{\beta,\alpha,f_{4},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}}\rangle[1]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}i}\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{n},f_{n+1}}\rangle,

where
S
is as in
Proposition

4.2.8
. The claim follows.
∎

4.3.
Massey products and minimal
A_{\infty}
-structures

In this subsection we relate (under some conditions) higher operations in a
minimal
A_{\infty}
-category and Massey products. We begin by recalling the
necessary definitions.

4.3.1.
Reminder on
A_{\infty}
-categories

We recall the most basic aspects of the theory of
A_{\infty}
-categories
and its relationship to that of DG categories. We refer the reader to

[
Kel01
,
LH
]
for details.

An
A_{\infty}
-category
is a categorical structure
\mathcal{A}
consisting of a
class of objects and graded vector spaces of morphisms

\mathcal{A}(x,y),\qquad x,y\in\mathcal{A}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is equipped with higher composition operations

m_{n}\colon\mathcal{A}(x_{1},x_{0})\otimes\mathcal{A}(x_{2},x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}(x_{n},x_{n-1})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(x_{n},x_{0}),\qquad n\geq 1

given by graded morphisms of degree
2-n
that must satisfy a certain infinite
system of equations that we do not recall here. We only mention that, in
particular, these equations imply the following:

•

For each
x,y\in\mathcal{A}
, the pair
(\mathcal{A}(x,y),m_{1})
is a cochain complex, that
is
m_{1}\circ m_{1}=0
.

•

The differential
m_{1}
is a graded derivation with respect to the
composition operation

m_{2}\colon\mathcal{A}(y,z)\otimes\mathcal{A}(x,y)\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(x,z),

(that is, it satisfies the graded Leibniz rule).

•

Although the composition operation
m_{2}
is not necessarily associative,
the failure of the associativity is controlled by the ternary operation
m_{3}

in a precise sense. In particular, as in the case of DG algebras,
\mathcal{A}
has an
associated graded category
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
as well as an ordinary category

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
, whose (associative) composition laws are induced by
m_{2}
.

We also note that DG categories can be identified with the
A_{\infty}
-categories
with
m_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 3
; in particular, the composition operation
m_{2}
is
associative in this case. An
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{A}
is
minimal
if

m_{1}=0
; in this case the composition operation is also associative.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be
A_{\infty}
-categories. An
A_{\infty}
-functor

F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
consists of the following data:

•

A map
F_{0}\colon\operatorname{ob}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\to\operatorname{ob}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)
between the classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}

and
\mathcal{B}
.

•

Graded morphisms

F_{n}\colon\mathcal{A}(x_{1},x_{0})\otimes\mathcal{A}(x_{2},x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}(x_{n},x_{n-1})\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}(F_{0}(x_{n}),F_{0}(x_{0})),

for
n\geq 1
, of degree
1-n
.

Moreover, the above morphisms are required to satisfy a further infinite system
of equations that imply that the morphisms

F_{1}\colon\mathcal{A}(x,y)\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}(F_{0}(x),F_{0}(y)),\qquad x,y\in\mathcal{A},

are cochain maps and, moreover,
F
induces a graded functor

H^{\bullet}(F)\colon H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})

via the apparent formulas

x\longmapsto F_{0}(x),\quad x\in\operatorname{ob}\left(\mathcal{A}\right),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left\{f\right\}\longmapsto\left\{F_{1}(f)\right\},\quad f\in\operatorname{Z}(\mathcal{A}(x,y)),

and in particular an ordinary functor

H^{0}(F)\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{B}).

An
A_{\infty}
-functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a
quasi-equivalence
if the
induced graded functor

H^{\bullet}(F)\colon H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})

is an equivalence of graded categories.

4.3.1
.

There are different notions of ‘unitality’ for the composition operation in an

A_{\infty}
-category. First of all, the existence of units (identities) may not
be required, in which case the corresponding
A_{\infty}
-categories are termed

non-unital
. Second, one may require that the graded category
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
is
unital, in which case one speaks of
cohomologically unital

A_{\infty}
-categories. Finally, one may require that the (higher) composition
operations
m_{n}
are
strictly unital
in a precise sense. All these
variants of unitality extend to
A_{\infty}
-functors. We do not wish to
elaborate on how the various notions of unitality relate to each other, but
only remark that strict unitality implies cohomological unitality, and that
every cohomologically unital
A_{\infty}
-category is quasi-equivalent to a
strictly unital
A_{\infty}
-category, see for example
[
Sei08
, Sec. I.2a]
.
The
A_{\infty}
-categories in this article are cohomologically unital.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a DG category. By the Homotopy Transfer Theorem
[
Kad80
]
, the
graded category
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
admits a (non-unique) minimal
A_{\infty}
-structure

(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{3},m_{4},\dots)

whose composition operation
m_{2}
is the composition law in the graded category

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
(and is implicit in the notation), and such that there is a
quasi-equivalence of
A_{\infty}
-categories

F\colon(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{3},m_{4},\dots)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}

that is the identity on the objects:
F_{0}=\mathbf{1}
. In this case,

(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{3},m_{4},\dots)
is called a
minimal
A_{\infty}
-model of
\mathcal{A}
.
Up to quasi-equivalence of
A_{\infty}
-categories, the DG category structure on

\mathcal{A}
is determined by the additional structure on its cohomology
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
.
This is the main motivation for the use of
A_{\infty}
-categories in this article
(see the proof of
Theorem

5.1.3
to see why this
is important for us).

4.3.2.
Massey products in DG categories with sparse cohomology

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a DG category. It was a longstanding belief that the higher Massey
products in the graded category
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
were related to the higher operations
in a minimal model for
\mathcal{A}
via the formula

\pm m_{n}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\in\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}}\rrangle.

However, counter-examples were found recently by Buijs, Moreno-Fernández and
Murillo in
[
BMFM20
]
. There, the authors give sufficient conditions for the
above formula to hold; however, these conditions are not suitable for our
purposes. Below we show that the above relationship between Massey products and
higher operations holds under a different sufficient condition that is better
adapted to our framework.

The following definition is motivated by
Remark

3.2.3
.

4.3.2
.

A graded category
\mathcal{C}
is
d
-sparse
if

\forall x,y\in\mathcal{C},\ \forall i\not\in d\mathbb{Z},\qquad\mathcal{C}(x,y)^{i}=0.

Similarly, a DG category
\mathcal{A}
has
d
-sparse cohomology
or is

cohomologically
d
-sparse
if the graded category
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
is

d
-sparse. Explicitly,
\mathcal{A}
is cohomologically
d
-sparse if

\forall x,y\in\mathcal{A},\qquad\forall i\not\in d\mathbb{Z},\qquad H^{i}(\mathcal{A}(x,y))=0.

4.3.3
.

For
d=1
, the sparseness condition on a (DG) category is, of course, vacuous.

4.3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be DG category with
d
-sparse cohomology, for some
d\geq 1
. Suppose
given a sequence of composable morphisms in
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
of the form

x_{k+2}\xrightarrow{f_{k+2}}x_{k+1}\xrightarrow{f_{k+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}

with
k\not\in d\mathbb{Z}
. Let
(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{3},m_{4},\dots)
be a minimal

A_{\infty}
-model of
\mathcal{A}
. Then,

m_{k+2}(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k+2})=0.

In other words, the higher operation
m_{k+2}
is identically
0
.

Proof.

By definition,
m_{k+2}
is a homogeneous morphism
of degree
2-(k+2)=-k
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is cohomologically
d
-sparse by assumption
and

|m_{k+2}(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k+2})|=-k+\sum_{i=1}^{k+2}|f_{i}|,

we must have
m_{k+2}(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k+2})=0
, because either some

|f_{i}|\notin d\mathbb{Z}
and hence
f_{i}=0
or all
|f_{i}|\in d\mathbb{Z}
and then

-k+\sum_{i=1}^{k+2}|f_{i}|\not\in d\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

4.3.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},m_{3},m_{4},\dots)
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-category whose underlying
graded category
\mathcal{A}=H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
is
d
-sparse. In view of

Proposition

4.3.4
, we write

(\mathcal{A},m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)=(\mathcal{A},m_{3},m_{4},\dots)

since the higher operations
m_{i+2}
with
i\not\in d\mathbb{Z}
must vanish.

The following proposition shows that, in a DG category with
d
-sparse
cohomology, the lowest non-trivial higher operation of any minimal

A_{\infty}
-model, that is
m_{d+2}
, determines the Massey products of the
corresponding length.

4.3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be DG category with
d
-sparse cohomology, for some
d\geq 1
, and
suppose given a sequence of composable morphisms in
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
of the form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}

and such that
f_{i}f_{i+1}=0
for each
0<i<d+2
. Then, for every minimal

A_{\infty}
-model
(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)
of
\mathcal{A}
, we have

-(-1)^{\sum_{0<k\leq d+2}(d+2-k)|f_{k}|}m_{d+2}(f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2})\in\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle.

Proof.

Let

(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-model of
\mathcal{A}
and
choose a quasi-equivalence

F\colon(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}

with
F_{0}=\mathbf{1}
. For
0\leq i<j\leq d+2
and
j-i<d+2
, we define

g_{ij}\coloneqq(-1)^{\sum_{i<k\leq j}(j-k)|f_{k}|}F_{j-i}(f_{i+1},\dots,f_{j}).

We claim that the
g_{ij}
’s form a defining system for computing the Massey
product
\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
. Indeed, in view of the equations
satisfied by the
A_{\infty}
-functor
F

[
Kel01
, Sec. 3.4]
, the morphism

d(F_{j-i}(f_{i+1},\dots,f_{j}))
equals

-\sum_{i<k<j}(-1)^{(k-i-1)+(j-k-1)\sum_{i<\ell\leq k}|f_{\ell}|}F_{k-i}(f_{i+1},\dots,f_{k})F_{j-k}(f_{k+1},\dots,f_{j})

and the claim follows by multiplying by the sign
(-1)^{\sum_{i<k\leq j}(j-k)|f_{k}|}
in the definition of
g_{ij}
.

Finally, the morphism
d(F_{d+2}(f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}))
equals

\displaystyle F_{1}(m_{d+2}(f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}))

\displaystyle-\sum_{0<k<d+2}(-1)^{(k-1)+(d+1-k)\sum_{0<\ell\leq k}|f_{\ell}|}F_{k}(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})F_{d+2-k}(f_{k+1},\dots,f_{d+2}).

From this, it follows that the morphism

-(-1)^{\sum_{0<k\leq d+2}(d+2-k)|f_{k}|}m_{d+2}(f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2})

represents the same cohomology class in
\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
as
the one induced by the above defining system.
∎

4.4.
The universal Massey product

In this subsection we introduce the universal Massey product of length
d+2

associated to a cohomologically
d
-sparse DG category
(
Definition

4.4.5
).

4.4.1.
Reminder on Hochschild cohomology

We refer the reader to
[
Mur22
, Sec. 1]
for more details on the material in
this section. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
\mathcal{C}^{e}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
its
enveloping category. As usual, we identify the abelian category of

\mathcal{C}
-bimodules with that of (right)
\mathcal{C}^{e}
-modules; in particular, the category

\mathcal{C}
yields the
diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule

(x,y)\longmapsto\mathcal{C}(x,y),\qquad x,y\in\mathcal{C},

that, with some abuse of notation, we also denote by
\mathcal{C}
. Recall that the

Hochschild cohomology of
\mathcal{C}
with coefficients in a
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule
M
,
also known as the
Hochschild–Mitchell cohomology

[
Mit72
]
, is the
graded vector space

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)\coloneqq\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}^{e}}^{\bullet}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right).

In particular, the Hochschild cohomology
\operatorname{HH}^{n}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)
vanishes if
n
is
negative. If
M=\mathcal{C}
the diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule, we write

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\coloneqq\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}\!\left(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}\right).

If
\mathcal{C}
is a graded category, then the category of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules is also graded
and the Hochschild cohomology is then a bi-graded vector space
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)
and

\operatorname{HH}^{p}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)=\prod_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right),\qquad p\geq 0.

Here,
p
is the
Hochschild (=horizontal) degree
and
q
is the

internal (=vertical) degree
. Hochschild cohomology is Morita invariant
and suitably functorial with respect to graded functors in the first variable
and bimodule morphisms in the second variable, see for example
[
Mur06
]
.

The Hochschild cohomology of a graded category can be computed by means of the

bar complex
, which is the projective resolution of the diagonal bimodule
whose component in degree
n
is

B_{n}(\mathcal{C})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}}\mathcal{C}(x_{0},-)\otimes\mathcal{C}(x_{1},x_{0})\otimes\mathcal{C}(x_{2},x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(x_{n},x_{n-1})\otimes\mathcal{C}(-,x_{n})

and whose differential
d\colon B_{n}(\mathcal{C})\to B_{n-1}(\mathcal{C})
and augmentation

d\colon B_{0}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
act on an elementary tensor by the formula

d(f_{0}\otimes f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n}\otimes f_{n+1})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}f_{0}\otimes f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i}f_{i+1}\otimes\dots\otimes f_{n}\otimes f_{n+1}

that uses the composition operation in
\mathcal{C}
. The
normalised bar complex

\bar{B}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
, consisting of replacing
\mathcal{C}(x,x)
with
\mathcal{C}(x,x)/\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{x}
above whenenever
x_{i+1}=x_{i}=x\in\mathcal{C}
, is also a projective resolution of the diagonal bimodule.
The
Hochschild cochain
complex of
\mathcal{C}
is the the cochain complex

\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{e}}^{*}\!\left(B_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}),M\right).

Explicitly, in Hochschild degree
n
,

\operatorname{C}^{n,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)=\prod_{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}\!\left(\mathcal{C}(x_{1},x_{0})\otimes\mathcal{C}(x_{2},x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(x_{n},x_{n-1}),M(x_{n},x_{0})\right)

and the differential
\varphi\mapsto d(\varphi)
, of bidegree
(1,0)
, is given
by the formula

\displaystyle d(\varphi)(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n+1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\varphi|_{v}|f_{1}|}f_{1}\cdot\varphi(f_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n+1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}\varphi(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i}f_{i+1}\otimes\dots\otimes f_{n}\otimes f_{n+1})

\displaystyle+(-1)^{n}\varphi(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n})\cdot f_{n+1},

where
|\varphi|_{v}
denotes the vertical degree.
We can alternatively use the
normalised Hochschild cochain complex,

\bar{\operatorname{C}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{e}}^{*}\!\left(\bar{B}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}),M\right),

which is the subcomplex formed by the Hochschild cochains which vanish when at least one entry is an identity morphism in
\mathcal{C}
.
If
M=\mathcal{C}
the diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule, we
also write

\operatorname{C}^{\bullet}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\coloneqq\operatorname{C}^{\bullet}\!\left(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}\right).

Essentially by definition, the Hochschild cohomology

\operatorname{HH}^{0,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{e}}^{*}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)

is isomorphic to the so-called
end
of the bimodule
M
,
see
[
Kel05b
, Sec. 2.1]
for the definition. In particular,

\operatorname{HH}^{0,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{e}}^{*}\!\left(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}\right)\cong Z(\mathcal{C})

is isomorphic to the end of the diagonal bimodule, that is the
graded centre
of

\mathcal{C}
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is an ungraded category (=graded category concentrated in degree
0
),
then the Hochschild cohomology of
\mathcal{C}
with coefficients in a graded (!)

\mathcal{C}
-bimodule can be computed in terms of ungraded Hochschild cohomology by
means of the formula

\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M\right)\cong\operatorname{HH}^{p}\!\left(\mathcal{C},M^{q}\right),\qquad p\geq 0,\quad q\in\mathbb{Z}.

The Hochschild cohomology

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}\right)

of a graded category has the structure of a
Gerstenhaber algebra

[
Ger63
]
and
[
Mur20a
, Sec. 1]
. Thus,
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)
is equipped with a
graded commutative product

\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\otimes\operatorname{HH}^{r,s}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{p+r,q+s}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right),\qquad a\otimes b\longmapsto a\cdot b,

called the
cup product
; up to sign, it agrees with the Yoneda product on the
corresponding extension spaces. Thus, the cup product satisfies the identities

\displaystyle(a\cdot b)\cdot c

\displaystyle=a\cdot(b\cdot c),

\displaystyle a\cdot b

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|a||b|}b\cdot a.

Here
|x|=p+q
is the
total degree
of an element
x\in\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)
.
Moreover, there is a degree
-1
Lie bracket

\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\otimes\operatorname{HH}^{r,s}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{p+r-1,q+s}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right),\qquad a\otimes b\longmapsto[a,b],

that satisfies the following graded variants of the usual Lie algebra
identities:

\displaystyle[a,a]

\displaystyle=0,\qquad|a|\text{ is odd},

\displaystyle[a,b]

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{(|a|-1)(|b|-1)}[b,a],

\displaystyle[a,[b,c]]

\displaystyle=[[a,b],c]+(-1)^{(|a|-1)(|b|-1)}[b,[a,c]],

\displaystyle[a,[a,a]]

\displaystyle=0,\qquad|a|\text{ is even}.

The cup product and the Lie bracket are compatible in the sense that

[a,b\cdot c]=[a,b]\cdot c+(-1)^{(|a|-1)|b|}b\cdot[a,c].

Finally, there is a
Gerstenhaber squaring operation
or
Gerstenhaber
square

\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{2p-1,2q}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right),\qquad a\longmapsto\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(a\right),

defined whenever
p+q
is even or
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})=2
. The Gerstenhaber square
satisfies the following compatibility relations with respect to the cup product
and the Lie bracket:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(a+b\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(a\right)+\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(b\right)+[a,b],

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(a\cdot b\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(a\right)\cdot b^{2}+a\cdot[a,b]\cdot b+a^{2}\cdot\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(b\right),

\displaystyle[\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(a\right),b]

\displaystyle=[a,[a,b]].

If
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})\neq 2
, then the Gerstenhaber square is in fact determined by the
Lie bracket according to the formula

\operatorname{Sq}(a)=\frac{1}{2}[a,a],

which follows from the previous relations. In particular, if
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})\neq 2
,
then the relations that involve the Gerstenhaber square are redundant.

4.4.1
.

For a graded algebra
A
(that we can regard as a graded category with only one object), the automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
acts on the right of the cochain complex
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
by conjugation,

\displaystyle\varphi^{f}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=f^{-1}\varphi(f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n})),\qquad\varphi\in\operatorname{C}^{n,*}\!\left(A\right),\quad f\in\operatorname{Aut}(A).

This induces a right action of
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
which is determined by

x^{f}=(f^{-1})_{*}f^{*}(x).

Here
f^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,A\right)\to\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,{}_{f}A_{f}\right)
is induced by the algebra automorphism
f
in the first variable and
f^{-1}_{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,{}_{f}A_{f}\right)\to\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,A\right)
is induced by the
A
-bimodule morphism
f^{-1}\colon{}_{f}A_{f}\to A
in the second variable.

The following result is probably well known but we have not found any reference.

4.4.2
.

The right action of
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
(
Remark

4.4.1
) factors through the outer automorphism group
\operatorname{Out}(A)
.

Proof.

We have to show that the inner automorphism
f_{u}\colon A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}A
,
f_{u}(x)\coloneqq(-1)^{|u||x|}uxu^{-1}
, induced by a unit
u\in A
acts trivially on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
. The sign in the definition of the outer automorphism comes from the fact that
\mathbf{1}_{A}
and
f_{u}
can be regarded as endofunctors of the graded category with one object
A
and multiplication by
u
can then be regarded as a degree
|u|
natural isomorphism
u\colon\mathbf{1}_{A}\Rightarrow f_{u}
. We will produce a null-homotopy for the map

\displaystyle\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right),

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\varphi^{f_{u}}.

The null-homotopy is the map

\displaystyle h\colon\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}^{\bullet-1,*}\!\left(A\right)

defined by

\displaystyle h(\varphi)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i+|u||\varphi|_{v}}u^{-1}\varphi(ux_{1}u^{-1},\dots,ux_{i}u^{-1},u,x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n-1}).

A tedious but straightforward computation shows that

dh+hd=\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)}-(-)^{f_{u}}.\qed

4.4.3
.

Proposition

4.4.2
can be generalized from algebras
A
to categories
\mathcal{C}
. In theory, the role of
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
should be replaced by the self-equivalences of
\mathcal{C}
, but these do not form a group (taking pseudo-inverses is not well defined). Nevertheless,
\operatorname{Out}(A)
can be replaced by the group of natural isomorphism classes of self-equivalences of
\mathcal{C}
, which happens to act on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)
by the same formulas as above. This can be checked by using the functoriality properties of Hochschild cohomology of categories, compare
[
Mur06
]
.

4.4.2.
The universal Massey product of lenght
d+2

Let
(\mathcal{A},m_{3},m_{4},\dots)
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-category. We are interested in
the Hochschild cohomology of the graded category
\mathcal{A}=H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
(recall that

m_{1}=0
by definition and
m_{2}
is the composition law in
\mathcal{A}
). Firstly, notice
that by definition the higher operations are Hochschild cochains:

m_{n}\in\operatorname{C}^{n,2-n}\!\left(\mathcal{A}\right),\qquad n\geq 3.

Similarly, if

F\colon(\mathcal{A},m_{3},m_{4},\dots)\longrightarrow(\mathcal{B},m_{3},m_{4},\dots)

is an
A_{\infty}
-functor between minimal
A_{\infty}
-categories, then
F_{0}
and

F_{1}
define a graded functor

F_{0,1}\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}.

between the underlying graded categories, and the rest of components are
Hochschild cochains:

F_{n}\in\operatorname{C}^{n,1-n}\!\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}(F_{0,1},F_{0,1})\right),\qquad n\geq 2.

The following result is a straightforward consequence of results of
Lefèvre-Hasegawa
[
LH
]
.

4.4.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-category whose
underlying graded category
\mathcal{A}
is
d
-sparse. The following statements hold:

(1)

The first non-trivial higher operation (see

Proposition

4.3.4
)

m_{d+2}\in\operatorname{C}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

is a Hochschild cocycle.

(2)

Let

(\mathcal{A},m_{d+2}^{\prime},m_{2d+2}^{\prime},\dots)
be another minimal
A_{\infty}
-category with the same
underlying graded category
\mathcal{A}
and

F\colon(\mathcal{A},m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A},m_{d+2}^{\prime},m_{2d+2}^{\prime},\dots)

an
A_{\infty}
-functor with
F_{0}=\mathbf{1}
and
F_{1}=\mathbf{1}
. Then,

\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}=\left\{m_{d+2}^{\prime}\right\}\quad\text{in}\quad\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\mathcal{A}\right).

Proof.

(
1
) Since
\mathcal{A}
is
d
-sparse, the higher operations

m_{i+2}
and
m_{i+2}^{\prime}
vanish whenever
i\not\in d\mathbb{Z}

(
Proposition

4.3.4
). The claim follows from

[
LH
, Lemma B.4.1]
, which shows that the differential
d(m_{d+2})
of

m_{d+2}
in the Hochschild complex
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
equals an expression of the form

\sum\pm m_{i+2}(\cdots),

where in particular
0<i<d
, and hence the above expression must vanish.

(
2
) Similarly as in the previous statement, since

\mathcal{A}
is
d
-sparse, the higher components
F_{i+1}
of the
A_{\infty}
-functor

F
must vanish whenever
i\not\in d\mathbb{Z}
as, by definition,
|F_{i+1}|=-i
.
The claim follows from
[
LH
, Lemma B.4.2]
, which shows that the
differential
d(F_{d+1})
of
F_{d+1}
in the Hochschild complex

\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
equals an expression of the form

m_{d+2}-m_{d+2}^{\prime}+\sum\pm F_{i+1}(\cdots)-\sum\pm m_{i+2}^{\prime}(\cdots),

where in particular
0<i<d
, and hence the two right-most terms in the
expression must vanish. This finishes the proof.
∎

4.4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a cohomologically
d
-sparse DG category and
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
is graded
cohomology category. The
universal
Massey product of length
d+2
is the Hochschild cohomology class

\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)

represented by the operation
m_{d+2}
in any minimal
A_{\infty}
-model of

\mathcal{A}
.

4.4.6
.

Given a cohomologically
d
-sparse DG category,

Proposition

4.4.4
shows that the universal Massey product
of length
d+2
is well defined and, moreover, is natural with respect to
quasi-equivalences: if
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a quasi-equivalence between
cohomologically
d
-sparse DG categories, then the induced isomorphism

\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)\cong\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})\right)

takes the universal Massey product of length
d+2
of
\mathcal{A}
to that of
\mathcal{B}
.
The universal Massey product of length
d+2
is also considered in
[
Sei15
, Ch. 3]
.
For
d=1
, it has been investigated for example in

[
BKS04
,
Kad82
]
and plays a crucial role in
[
Mur22
]
as well.

4.4.7
.

The formula in
Proposition

4.3.6
still holds if we replace
m_{d+2}
by any other Hochschild cochain
m
of bidegree
(d+2,-d)
representing the universal
Massey product of length
d+2
of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e. given a sequence of composable morphisms in
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
of the form

x_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}x_{d+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}x_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}x_{0}

and such that
f_{i}f_{i+1}=0
for each
0<i<d+2
, then

-(-1)^{\sum_{0<k\leq d+2}(d+2-k)|f_{k}|}m(f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2})\in\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle.

This is straightforward to check, see
[
BKS04
, Lem. 5.14]
for the case
d=1
. Compare also
[
BD89
, Thm. 3.3]
in topology.

4.4.8
.

Occasionally, we consider, more generally, the universal Massey product

\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

associated to a minimal
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{A}=H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
whose underlying
graded category is
d
-sparse (here,
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
denotes the Hochschild
cohomology of the underlying graded category of
\mathcal{A}
).

4.5.
The restricted universal Massey product

In this subsection we relate universal Massey products to standard and
Amiot–Lin
(d+2)
-angulations (
Theorem

4.5.8
). The key ingredient
for establishing the desired relationship is the restricted universal Massey
product (
Definition

4.5.4
).

4.5.1
.

We fix a small DG category
\mathcal{A}
with the following properties:

(1)

The DG category
\mathcal{A}
is cohomologically
d
-sparse. We fix a minimal

A_{\infty}
-model

(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)

of
\mathcal{A}
(see
4.3.5
).

(2)

The essential image of
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
under the canonical fully faithful
functor

H^{0}(\mathbf{h})\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})

is closed under the action of the
d
-fold shift and its inverse. In
particular, there is a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[d]}

where
[d]\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is an
autoequivalence such that

\forall x,y\in\mathcal{A},\qquad H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(x,y[d]))\cong H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(x,y)[d])\cong H^{d}(\mathcal{A}(x,y))

as vector spaces (functorially on
x
and
y
).

(3)

The category
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is additive and has split idempotents and
finite-dimensional morphism spaces.

(4)

There exists a basic object
c\in H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
such that

\operatorname{add}(c)=H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
. In particular, the Yoneda functor

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda),\qquad x\longmapsto H^{0}(\mathcal{A})(c,x)

restricts to an equivalence of categories

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),

where
\Lambda\coloneqq H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(c,c))
.

4.5.2
.

Since the finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
is basic, there exists an
algebra automorphism
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
(unique
up to inner automorphisms) such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes up to natural isomorphism. Explicitly, we fix an isomorphism

\varphi\colon c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}c[d]
in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
(which exists since

c
is a basic additive generator of
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
, which is closed under
[d]
). The algebra automorphism

\sigma=\sigma_{\varphi}\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})(c,c)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A})(c,c),\qquad a\longmapsto\varphi^{-1}(a[d])\varphi,

has the desired property. In particular, the autoequivalence

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)

can be identified with the restriction of scalars
P\longmapsto P_{\sigma^{-1}}
along
\sigma^{-1}
.

4.5.1.
The
d
-sparse graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)

The isomorphism
\varphi\colon c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}c[d]
(
4.5.2
)
can be interpreted as an invertible cohomology class

\varphi\in H^{0}(\mathcal{A})(c,c[d])\cong H^{d}(\mathcal{A}(c,c));

under this identification, the algebra automorphism
\sigma=\sigma_{\varphi}
of

\Lambda
acts by conjugation
a\mapsto\varphi^{-1}a\varphi
. Notice also that

\varphi
induces isomorphisms of vector spaces

H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(c,c))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{di}(\mathcal{A}(c,c)),\qquad b\longmapsto\varphi^{i}b,

for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, as a bimodule over
\Lambda=H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(c,c))
, the
cohomology space
H^{di}(\mathcal{A}(c,c))
identifies with the twisted

\Lambda
-bimodule
{}_{\sigma^{i}}{\Lambda}_{1}
. Indeed, for

a\in H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(c,c))
and

b\in H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(c,c))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{di}(\mathcal{A}(c,c))

we have

\displaystyle b\cdot a=\varphi^{-i}((\varphi^{i}b)a)=ba\qquad\text{and}\qquad a\cdot b=\varphi^{-i}(a(\varphi^{i}b))=\sigma^{i}(a)b.

More generally, we introduce the
d
-sparse graded algebra with underlying
graded vector space

\Lambda(\sigma,d)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{di\in d\mathbb{Z}}{}_{\sigma^{i}}{\Lambda}_{1}

and multiplication law

a\cdot b\coloneqq\sigma^{j}(a)b\in\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{d(i+j)},\qquad a\in{}_{\sigma^{i}}{\Lambda}_{1},\ b\in{}_{\sigma^{j}}{\Lambda}_{1}.

It is straightforward to verify that the linear map

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})(c,c)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda(\sigma,d),\qquad a\longmapsto\varphi^{-i}a,\qquad a\in H^{di}(\mathcal{A})(c,c)

is an isomorphism of graded algebras and, therefore, we obtain an induced
isomorphism of Gerstenhaber algebras

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})(c,c)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),

which we treat as an identification in what follows. Finally, since the object

c\in H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is an additive generator there is also a canonical isomorphism
of Gerstenhaber algebras

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})(c,c)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)

and, consequently, a further isomorphism of Gerstenhaber algebras

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right).

4.5.3
.

There is a further isomorphism of (
d
-sparse) graded algebras

\Lambda(\sigma,d)\cong\frac{\Lambda\langle\imath^{\pm 1}\rangle}{(\imath a-\sigma(a)\imath)_{a\in\Lambda}},\qquad|\imath|=-d,

induced by the
\Lambda
-linear map

\Lambda\langle\imath^{\pm 1}\rangle\longrightarrow\Lambda(\sigma,d),\qquad\imath\longmapsto 1\in\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{-d}={}_{\sigma^{-1}}{\Lambda}_{1}.

Indeed, for
a\in\Lambda=\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{0}
and
1\in\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{-d}
we
have

\sigma(a)\cdot 1=\sigma^{-1}(\sigma(a))1=a=1a=\sigma^{0}(1)a=1\cdot a.

We treat this isomorphism as an identification simply for the purpose of
removing the ambiguities that arise when considering homogeneous elements of
the graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
(whose non-zero components are all equal to

\Lambda
as vector spaces).

4.5.2.
The restricted universal Massey product

The inclusion
j\colon\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Lambda(\sigma,d)
of the degree
0
part
induces a restriction morphism

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

that, in bidegree
(d+2,-d)
, yields a morphism

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\cong\operatorname{HH}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right),

since

\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{-d}={}_{\sigma^{-1}}{\Lambda}_{1}\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}

is the degree
-d
part of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
and

\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\operatorname{HH}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{-d}\right)=\operatorname{HH}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{\sigma^{-1}}{\Lambda}_{1}\right);

notice also that the target of
j^{*}
is (isomorphic to) the extension space

\operatorname{HH}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right).

Using the graded Morita invariance of Hochschild cohomology, we may identify

j^{*}
with the restriction morphism

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{d+2}\!\left(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),H^{-d}(\mathcal{A})\right),

where
H^{-d}(\mathcal{A})
is the ungraded
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
-bimodule

H^{-d}(\mathcal{A})\colon(x,y)\longmapsto H^{-d}(\mathcal{A}(x,y)).

4.5.4
.

The
restricted universal Massey product of length
d+2
is the class

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2}\!\left(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),H^{-d}(\mathcal{A})\right),

where

\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)

is the universal Massey product of length
d+2
associated to the minimal

A_{\infty}
-model

(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)

of the
d
-sparse DG category
\mathcal{A}
.

4.5.5
.

In view of the isomorphism

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),

the universal Massey product
\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
can be identified with a class

\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

and therefore

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)

can be represented by an exact sequence of
\Lambda
-bimodules.

4.5.6
.

Occasionally, we consider, more generally, the restricted universal Massey product

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\mathcal{A}^{0},\mathcal{A}\right)=\operatorname{HH}^{d+2}\!\left(\mathcal{A}^{0},\mathcal{A}^{-d}\right)

associated to a minimal
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{A}=H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
whose underlying
graded category is
d
-sparse, where
\mathcal{A}^{0}
is the degree
0
part of
\mathcal{A}
(in
particular,
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\mathcal{A}^{0},\mathcal{A}\right)
denotes the Hochschild cohomology of the
ungraded category
\mathcal{A}^{0}
with coefficients in the graded
\mathcal{A}^{0}
-bimodule

\mathcal{A}
).

The cohomology class investigated in
[
BD89
]
is a topological version of the restricted universal Massey products considered here in the case
d=1
. It computes Toda brackets, which are the topological counterpart of Massey products. The connection is explained in
[
Mur20b
]
.

4.5.3.
Standard
(d+2)
-angles versus Amiot–Lin
(d+2)
-angulations

4.5.7
.

Choose an exact sequence

\eta\colon\qquad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

that represents
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
in

\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)
; we may and we will
assume that
P_{0},P_{1},\dots,P_{d}
are projective
\Lambda
-bimodules. We let

{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}\coloneqq{\pentagon}_{\eta}

be the class of
(d+2)
-angles in
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
obtained as in

2.2.13
. Note, however, that we do not claim that

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
can be represented by a extension in which
all

middle terms are projective
\Lambda
-bimodules, and therefore

a priori
the class
{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}
is not known to be
a
(d+2)
-angulation of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
.

We aim to prove the following theorem. We remind the reader of our standing
assumptions (
4.5.1
).

4.5.8
.

Suppose that the DG category
\mathcal{A}
is pre-
(d+2)
-angulated. Then, the
following statements hold:

(1)

The class of standard
(d+2)
-angles on

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)\simeq(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),[d])

coincides with the class

(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}
from

4.5.7
(see also
Remark

2.1.6
). In
particular, the class

(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}
does not depend
on the choice of an exact sequence representing
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
in

\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)
.

(2)

Suppose that
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable.
Let

0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

be an exact sequence that represents
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
in

\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)
such that the

\Lambda
-bimodules
P_{i}
,
0\leq i<d+1
, are projective-injective

\Lambda
-bimodules. Then,
P_{d+1}
is also a projective-injective

\Lambda
-bimodule.

In particular, if
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable, the standard

(d+2)
-angulation and the AL
(d+2)
-angulation of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)

coincide.

4.5.9
.

To alleviate the notation, we denote by
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
be the
essential image of
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
under the canonical fully faithful functor

H^{0}(\mathbf{h})\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}).

Under the induced equivalence of categories
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\simeq\mathcal{C}
, the

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
-bimodule

H^{di}(\mathcal{A})\colon(x,y)\longmapsto H^{di}(\mathcal{A}(x,y))\cong H^{0}(\mathcal{A})(x,y[di]),\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z},

identifies with the twisted
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule

{}_{[di]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\colon(M,N)\longmapsto\mathcal{C}(M,N[di]),\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

Similarly, the autoequivalence

[d]\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
induces the
autoequivalence

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}{}_{[d]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\colon\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{C})

with quasi-inverse

\Sigma^{-1}\coloneqq-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}{}_{[-d]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\colon\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{C}).

Working towards the proof of
Theorem

4.5.8
, we first investigate the
relationship between the restricted universal Massey product and extensions. The
reader might find it useful to compare the discussion below with

2.2.13
. We begin with a general observation. Let
\mathcal{X}
be a
category and consider an exact sequence of
\mathcal{X}
-bimodules of the form

0\to M\to E_{d+1}\to\cdots\to E_{1}\to E_{0}\to\mathcal{X}\to 0,

where
E_{0},E_{1},\dots,E_{d}
are projective
\mathcal{X}
-bimodules. Notice that for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
the sequence of
left

\mathcal{X}
-modules

0\to M(x,-)\to E_{d+1}(x,-)\to\cdots\to E_{1}(x,-)\to E_{0}(x,-)\to\mathcal{X}(x,-)\to 0

is split-exact (=contractible as a complex), for the representable left

\mathcal{X}
-module
\mathcal{X}(x,-)
as well as
E_{i}(x,-)
,
i=0,1,\dots,d
, are projective as left
\mathcal{X}
-modules. From
this follows that, for every (right)
\mathcal{X}
-module
N
, the sequence of (right)

\mathcal{X}
-modules

N\otimes_{\mathcal{X}}(0\to M\to E_{d+1}\to\cdots\to E_{1}\to E_{0}\to\mathcal{X}\to 0)

is exact. Thus, in view of the canonical isomorphism
N\otimes_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{X}\cong N
,
there is a well-defined morphism

\Phi_{N}\colon\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{X}^{e}}^{\bullet}\!\left(\mathcal{X},M\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\bullet}\!\left(N,N\otimes_{\mathcal{X}}M\right),

which is an algebra morphism with respect to the Yoneda product if
M=\mathcal{X}
the
diagonal
\mathcal{X}
-bimodule.

Returning to the context of
4.5.1
, if we let

\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{C}
and
M={}_{[q]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
in the previous discussion, for each
\mathcal{C}
-module

N
we obtain a morphism

\Phi_{N}\colon\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\mathcal{C},{}_{[q]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d+2}\!\left(N,N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}{}_{[q]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\right).

In particular, if
q=-d
, we may consider the class

\Phi_{N}(j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d+2}\!\left(N,N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}{}_{[-d]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1})\right)\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)

induced by the restricted universal Massey product of length
d+2
. Thus, given
an exact sequence of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules

\eta\colon\qquad 0\to{}_{[-d]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\mathcal{C}\to 0

that represents the restricted universal Massey product
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
, we
see that the class
\Phi_{N}(j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})
is represented by the exact
sequence

N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(0\to{}_{[-d]}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\mathcal{C}\to 0).

Suppose given a sequence of composable morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
of the form

M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}M_{f+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}M_{0}

and such that
f_{i}f_{i+1}=0
for all
0\leq i<d+2
, so that the Massey product

\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
in
H^{\bullet}(A)
is non-empty (see

Remark

4.2.4
). Let

N\coloneqq\coker\mathcal{C}(-,f_{1})\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{mod}\mathbf{k}.

In view of
Proposition

4.1.31
and
Theorem

4.2.6
,
the Massey product is an element of the vector space
\operatorname{Massey}\left(f_{d+2},f_{1}\right)

defined as the quotient

\frac{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})}\!\left(M_{d+2},M_{0}[-d]\right)}{f_{1}[-d]\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})}\!\left(M_{d+2},M_{1}[-d]\right)+\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})}\!\left(M_{d+1},M_{0}\right)\cdot f_{d+2}}.

We wish to construct a further morphism

\Psi\colon\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Massey}\left(f_{d+2},f_{1}\right).

For this, let
B_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
be the bar complex of
\mathcal{C}
, so that
N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
is a projective resolution of
N
that we use to compute the
extension space
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)
as

\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)=H^{d+2}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\!\left(N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}),\Sigma^{-1}N\right)).

Following essentially the same arguments as in
[
Mur20b
, Sec. 5]
, one can
check that under the above isomorphism the class
\Phi_{N}(j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})
is
represented by the class of the morphism

N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B_{d+2}(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\Sigma^{-1}N

that corresponds, under the Yoneda embedding, to the morphism

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{L_{0},L_{1},\dots,L_{d+2}}N(L_{0})\otimes\mathcal{C}(L_{1},L_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(L_{d+2},L_{d+1})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Sigma^{-1}N(L_{d+2})

given by

(4.5.10)

[h]\otimes g_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes g_{d+2}\longmapsto[(h[-d])\circ m_{d+2}(g_{1},\dots,g_{d+2})],

where we use that

m_{d+2}(g_{1},\dots,g_{d+2})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})}\!\left(L_{d+2},L_{0}[-d]\right)

and therefore the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N[-d]=\Sigma^{-1}N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{d+2}(g_{1},\dots,g_{d+2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h[-d]}

indeed represents an element in
\Sigma^{-1}N(L_{d+2})
. We refer the reader to
the last paragraph of the proof of
[
Mur20b
, Prop. 5.2]
as well
as
[
Mur20b
, Prop. 5.6 and Rmks. 5.4 and 5.7]
for details.

Continuing with the construction of the morphism
\Psi
, consider the morphism
of augmented chain complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(-,M_{d+2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(-,M_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(-,M_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B_{d+3}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B_{d+2}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B_{1}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B_{0}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
