that corresponds, under the Yoneda embedding, to the elements

(4.5.11)

(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{i}j}p\otimes f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{M_{i}}\in(N\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B_{i}(\mathcal{C}))(M_{i}),

compare with the proof of
[
Mur20b
, Prop. 5.9]
. Here
p
is the natural projection onto the cokernel. The morphism
\Psi
is
obtained by applying the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\!\left(-,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)
to the above
morphism of chain complexes and passing to cohomology in degree
d+2
, while
keeping in mind the isomorphisms

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\!\left(\mathcal{C}(-,M_{d+2}),\Sigma^{-1}N\right)\cong\Sigma^{-1}N(M_{d+2})\cong\frac{\mathcal{C}(M_{d+2},M_{0}[-d])}{(f_{1}[-d])\cdot\mathcal{C}(M_{d+2},M_{1}[-d])}.

As a consequence of the above discussion, we obtain the following relationship
between the restricted universal Massey product
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
and the
Massey product
\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
.

4.5.12
.

Suppose given a sequence of composable morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
of the form

M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{f_{d+2}}M_{f+1}\xrightarrow{f_{d+1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}M_{0}

and such that
f_{i}f_{i+1}=0
for all
0\leq i<d+2
. Set
N=\coker\mathcal{C}(-,f_{1})
.
Then,

(\Psi\circ\Phi_{N})(j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})=-(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{d+2}i}\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle

in
\operatorname{Massey}\left(f_{d+2},f_{1}\right)
.

Proof.

In view of (
4.5.10
) and (
4.5.11
),

\displaystyle(\Psi\circ\Phi_{N})(j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{d+2}i}\left\{m_{d+2}(f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2})\right\}\in\operatorname{Massey}\left(f_{d+2},f_{1}\right).

The claim then follows from
Proposition

4.3.6
, which in this
case says that

-m_{d+2}(f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2})\in\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle.\qed

4.5.13
.

In view of
Theorem

4.2.6
, under the assumptions of

Proposition

4.5.12
,

(\Psi\circ\Phi_{N})(j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})[d]=(-1)^{d+1}\langle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rangle\in\operatorname{Toda}\left(f_{d+2},f_{1}\right).

The following proposition shows that the standard
(d+2)
-angles in
\mathcal{C}
are
detected by the restricted universal Massey product of length
d+2
. In the statement we do not assume
\mathcal{A}
to be pre-
(d+2)
-angulated.

4.5.14
.

Let
(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-model
of
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose given a sequence of composable morphisms

(4.5.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma M_{d+2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

in
(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)\simeq(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),[d])
. Then, the following statements are
equivalent:

(1)

The above sequence satisfies the conditions in

Theorem

4.1.12
.

(2)

The above sequence lies in

(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}
(see

4.5.7
and
Remark

2.2.14
).

Proof.

For simplicity, we identify (
4.5.15
) with a sequence of projective

\Lambda
-modules. In view of
Theorem

4.2.6
, statement
(
1
) is equivalent to the following two conditions:

•

The sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma M_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma M_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma f_{d+2}}

is exact.

•

We have
-\mathbf{1}_{M_{d+2}}\in\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
.

The first condition is precisely the first condition that a sequence must
satisfy to be a member of the class

(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}
.

On the other hand, the second (and last) condition that a sequence must
satisfy to be a member of the class

(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}
says the following:
Let
N=\coker{f_{1}}
, so that there is an exact sequence

0\to\Sigma^{-1}N\xrightarrow{i}M_{d+1}\to\cdots\to M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\Sigma M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{p}N\to 0

with
f_{d+2}=i\circ\Sigma^{-1}p
. Then, the above sequence must represent the
class

\Phi_{N}((-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)

that, by definition, is represented by the exact sequence

N\otimes_{\Lambda}(0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}\iota}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0)

(the
\Lambda
-bimodule extension on the right of the tensor product
represents the class
(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
, see

4.5.7
and
Remark

2.2.14
).
The extension space can be computed by means of the projective resolution

\cdots\to\Sigma^{-1}M_{1}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{-1}f_{1}}M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{}M_{d+1}\to\cdots\to M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\Sigma M_{d+2}\xrightarrow{p}N\to 0

and, in these terms, we see that the former sequence is represented by the
class

[\Sigma^{-1}p]\in\frac{\ker(?\circ\Sigma^{-1}f_{1})}{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}\!\left(M_{d+1},\Sigma^{-1}N\right)\cdot f_{d+2}}\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right).

Here

?\circ\Sigma^{-1}f_{1}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}\!\left(M_{d+2},\Sigma^{-1}N\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}\!\left(\Sigma^{-1}M_{1},\Sigma^{-1}N\right)

is the
morphism given by pre-composition with
\Sigma^{-1}f_{1}
. Thus, to summarise,
the second condition for membership in the class

(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}
says that

\left[\Sigma^{-1}p\right]=\Phi_{N}((-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})

in
\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)
.

Now, the morphism
\Psi
is injective by construction and identifies the
extension space
\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda}^{d+2}\!\left(N,\Sigma^{-1}N\right)
with the subspace

\frac{\ker(?\circ\Sigma^{-1}f_{1})}{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}\!\left(M_{d+1},\Sigma^{-1}N\right)\cdot f_{d+2}}\subseteq\frac{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}\!\left(M_{d+2},\Sigma^{-1}N\right)}{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}\!\left(M_{d+1},\Sigma^{-1}N\right)\cdot f_{d+2}}\cong\operatorname{Massey}\left(f_{d+2},f_{1}\right).

Thus, to conclude the proof it is enough to prove that

\Psi(\Phi_{N}((-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}))=\Psi(\left[\Sigma^{-1}p\right])

if and only if
-\mathbf{1}_{M_{d+2}}\in\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle
. Indeed,

\Psi\colon\left[\Sigma^{-1}p\right]\longmapsto\left[\mathbf{1}_{M_{d+2}}\right]\in\operatorname{Massey}\left(f_{d+2},f_{1}\right).

and

\Psi\colon\Phi_{N}((-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})\longmapsto-\llangle{f_{1},\dots,f_{d+2}}\rrangle

by
Proposition

4.5.12
. The claim follows.
∎

The graded algebra structure on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
endows the Hochschild cohomology

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{\bullet}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

with the structure of a bigraded algebra, where the internal grading is induced
by the grading of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
. In particular,

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,0}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{\bullet}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{0}\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{\bullet}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda\right)

is the Yoneda algebra of the diagonal
\Lambda
-bimodule. Similarly, since

\Lambda^{e}
is also self-injective, we may consider the
Hochschild–Tate
cohomology

\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\coloneqq\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),

which is again a bigraded algebra, and note that there are isomorphisms

\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{p,q}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\cong\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{p}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{\sigma^{q}}{\Lambda}_{1}\right),\qquad p>0,\quad q\in\mathbb{Z}.

We refer the reader to
[
Mur22
, Sec. 5]
for details.

4.5.16
.

An extension

0\to{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

of
\Lambda
-bimodules such that
P_{i}
,
0\leq i<d+1
, is a
projective-injective
\Lambda
-bimodule represents a unit in the
Hochschild–Tate cohomology
\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
if and only if

P_{d+1}
is also a projective-injective
\Lambda
-bimodule, see

[
Mur22
, Rmk. 5.9]
.

We are finally ready to prove
Theorem

4.5.8
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.5.8
.

Statement (
1
) follows from
Proposition

4.5.14
. We prove
statement (
2
). Choose an exact sequence

\eta\colon\qquad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

that represents the restricted universal Massey product

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)

and such that
P_{i}
,
0\leq i<d+1
, is a projective-injective

\Lambda
-bimodule (such a representative always exists). We need to prove
that the
\Lambda
-bimodule
P_{d+1}
is also projective-injective. It is
enough to prove that
N\otimes P_{d+1}
is projective for every
finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-module
N
, see
[
AR91
, Thm. 3.1]
and notice
that the proof remains valid under the assumption of
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
being
separable, since in this case the finite-dimensional algebra

(\Lambda/J_{\Lambda})\otimes(\Lambda/J_{\Lambda})^{\mathrm{op}}
is semisimple

[
ERZ57
, Cor. 18]
.

Thus, let
N
be a finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-module and choose a projective
presentation

Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma Q_{d+2}\to N\to 0.

Using axioms (

d

-TR1
) and (

d

-TR2
), complete the morphism
f_{1}
to a
standard
(d+2)
-angle

Q_{d+2}\to Q_{d+1}\to\cdots\to Q_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\Sigma Q_{d+2}.

In view of the validity of statement (
1
), the above

(d+2)
-angle lies in the class
\pm{\pentagon}_{j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}}
. In
particular, the exact sequence

N\otimes_{\Lambda}(0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pm\iota}}{{\to}}P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0)

is equivalent to an exact sequence of the form

0\to\Sigma^{-1}N\xrightarrow{}Q_{d+1}\to\cdots\to Q_{1}\xrightarrow{}\Sigma Q_{d+2}\xrightarrow{}N\to 0.

Notice that all of the middle terms in the latter exact sequence are
projective-injective
\Lambda
-bimodules and, therefore, the class of this
sequence in the Hochschild–Tate cohomology
\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
is a
unit. Consequently, the former (equivalent) sequence also represents a unit in

\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
, and hence the
\Lambda
-module

{N\otimes_{\Lambda}P_{d+1}}
must be projective-injective, which is what we
needed to prove. This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Theorem

4.5.8
and
Proposition

4.5.14
yield the following important corollaries.

4.5.17
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small DG category satisfying the assumptions in

4.5.1
. Consider the following statements:

(1)

The DG category
\mathcal{A}
is pre-
(d+2)
-angulated.

(2)

The canonical fully faithful functor

H^{0}(\mathbf{h})\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})

identifies
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
with a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting subcategory of

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
.

(3)

The restricted universal Massey product

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)

is represented by an extension all of whose middle terms are
projective-injective
\Lambda
-bimodules.

In general (
1
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
2
) and
(
1
)
\Leftarrow
(
3
). If

\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable, then
(
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
).

Proof.

The equivalence (
1
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
2
)
is
Proposition

3.2.4
.

(
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
) This is
precisely
Theorem

4.5.8
(
2
), which requires the
algebra
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
to be separable.

(
3
)
\Rightarrow
(
1
) By

Proposition

4.5.14
and
Remark

2.2.14
, the Amiot–Lin
(d+2)
-angulation associated to a representative of
(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
coincides with
the class of sequences that satisfy the conditions in

Theorem

4.1.12
, which therefore forms a
(d+2)
-angulation.
Consequently, the canonical fully faithful functor

H^{0}(\mathbf{h})\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})

identifies the left-hand side with a standard
(d+2)
-angulated subcategory of
the right-hand side, which is precisely the definition of
\mathcal{A}
being
pre-
(d+2)
-angulated.
∎

4.5.18
.

Suppose for simplicity that the field
k
is perfect. In the context of

Corollary

4.5.17
, it follows from
[
Dug12
, Thm. 3.2]
that
there is a stable bimodule isomorphism

\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)\cong{}_{1}\Lambda_{\sigma}
. An important aspect of

Corollary

4.5.17
is that this stable bimodule isomorphism is
witnessed by the restricted universal Massey product of
\mathcal{A}
.

Recall that the Nakayama automorphism
\eta
of a Frobenius algebra
\Lambda
is
characterised (up to inner automorphisms) by the existence of an isomorphism of

\Lambda
-bimodules
D\Lambda\cong{}_{1}\Lambda_{\eta}
.

4.5.19
[
Dug12
, Prop. 3.3]
,
[
GKO13
, Sec. 5.4]
,
[
IO13
, Cor. 4.6]
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small DG category satisfying the assumptions in

4.5.1
and such that the canonical fully faithful functor

H^{0}(\mathbf{h})\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})

identifies
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
with a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting subcategory of

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
. If
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is
ds
-Calabi–Yau,
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, in the sense that there
are functorial isomorphisms

H^{0}(A)(y,x[d\ell])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}DH^{0}(A)(x,y),\qquad x,y\in H^{0}(A),

then
\sigma^{-\ell}
is a Nakayama automorphism for
\Lambda
. In particular,
the stable module category
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}\Lambda
is
((d+2)\ell-1)
-Calabi–Yau.

Proof.

The argument is standard; we reproduce it for the
convenience of the reader. The functorial isomorphism

H^{0}(A)(c,c[d\ell])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}DH^{0}(A)(c,c)

can be interpreted as an isomorphism of
\Lambda
-bimodules

{}_{1}\Lambda_{\sigma^{-\ell}}\cong D\Lambda
that exhibits
\sigma^{-\ell}
as a
Nakayama automorphism for
\Lambda
. This proves the first claim. To prove
the second claim, observe that
Corollary

4.5.17
yields the
existence of an isomorphism

\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)\cong{}_{1}\Lambda_{\sigma},

in
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}\Lambda^{e}
that in turn induces an isomorphism of functors

\Omega_{\Lambda}^{(d+2)\ell}\cong\nu^{-1}

on
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}\Lambda
, where
\nu=-\otimes_{\Lambda}D\Lambda
is the Nakayama
functor. The claim follows from the fact that
\nu\Omega_{\Lambda}
is a Serre
functor on
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}\Lambda
, see
[
ES06
, Prop. 1.2 and Cor. 1.3]
and
compare also with
[
IV14
, Thm. 1.8]
. This finishes the proof.
∎

4.5.20
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional self-injective algebra that is twisted

(d+2)
-periodic with respect to an algebra automorphism
\sigma
and let

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda).

If
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable, then, up to equivalence, the pair

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
admits at most one algebraic
(d+2)
-angulation.

Proof.

Indeed,
Theorem

4.5.8
shows that any two algebraic

(d+2)
-angulations of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
coincide with the AL

(d+2)
-angulations given by any choice of exact sequences with
projective-middle terms in

\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)
that represent the
corresponding restricted universal Massey products of the enhancements, up to
a sign depending on
d
, see also

Remark

2.2.14
. But such AL
(d+2)
-angulations
are unique up to equivalence by
Proposition

2.2.18
.
∎

4.5.21
.

Notice that
Corollary

4.5.20

does not
prove that any two
enhancements are equivalent, provided at least one exists. This is part of the
content of
A
.

4.6.
Example of a universal Massey product

In this subsection we compute the universal Massey product of a specific
Karoubian pre-
(d+2)
-angulated DG category
\mathcal{A}
or, rather, the first non-zero
higher operation in a minimal
A_{\infty}
-model of
\mathcal{A}
. Let
A=A_{3}
be the path
algebra of the quiver
1\to 2\to 3
and consider its compact derived category

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)
. It is well known and easy to verify that
A
is DG Morita
equivalent to the factor algebra
B=A/J_{A}^{2}
(an explicit tilting object in

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)
with endomorphism algebra isomorphic to
B
is given by the
direct sum of the simple
A
-modules at the vertices
1
and
3
together with
the unique indecomposable projective-injective
A
-module). Thus, we may and we
will identify minimal
A_{\infty}
-models of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)
with those of

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(B)
(we do this to minimise the number of projective resolutions
needed in the computation below).

The algebra
B
has global dimension
2
and
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(B)
admits a

2\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting subcategory given by

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}(B)\coloneqq\operatorname{add}\left\{(B\oplus S_{1})[2i]\right|i\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subset\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(B),

where
B
is the regular representation and
S_{1}
is the simple
B
-module
concentrated at the vertex
1
, see
[
Iya11
, Cor. 1.15 and Thm. 1.21]
(the
claim can also be verified directly using the explicit description of the
Auslander–Reiten quiver of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)\simeq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(B)
available
in
[
Hap88
, Sec. I.5]
for example). By
Theorem

2.2.1
the
pair
(\mathcal{U},[2])
has an induced standard
4
-angulation; it is also easy to
verify that all
4
-angles in
\mathcal{U}
are
finite direct sums of rotations of trivial
4
-angles as in (

d

-TR1b
) and the apparent
4
-angle

P_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\to}}P_{1}\to S_{1}\to P_{3}[2],

where
P_{i}
denotes the indecomposable projective
B
-module at the
vertex
i
and
the connecting morphism
S_{1}\to P_{3}[2]
classifies the (essentially unique)
non-zero class in
\operatorname{Ext}_{B}^{2}\!\left(S_{1},P_{3}\right)
; thus, the above
4
-angle consists of
the minimal projective resolution of
S_{1}
together with its canonical augmentation.

Theorems

4.1.12
and
4.2.6
imply that the Toda
bracket/Massey product of the morphisms in the above
4
-angle contains

\pm\mathbf{1}_{P_{3}}
. By
Proposition

4.1.31
, this Massey product is an element of the quotient

\frac{\hom_{B}(P_{3},P_{3})}{\hom_{B}(P_{2},P_{3})\cdot a},

hence it cannot contain
0
since it contains
\pm\mathbf{1}_{P_{3}}
and the morphism
a

is not a split monomorphism. Therefore, any minimal
A_{\infty}
-model of the
canonical DG enhancement of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(B)
must have non-vanishing
m_{4}
by

Proposition

4.3.6
. For the convenience of the reader, below we
provide an explicit computation of the action of
m_{4}
on the above
4
-angle by
means of Kadeishvili’s Homotopy Transfer Theorem.

The minimal projective resolution
Q
of
S_{1}
is the apparent
complex

Q\colon\quad P_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\to}}P_{1}.

As a graded quiver with relations, the full DG subcategory of
\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\operatorname{Mod}B)

spanned by
Q
and the
P_{i}
’s the following:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{\prime}}

The bottom row is the complex
Q
and the degrees of the arrows and the relations are as
follows:

\displaystyle|a|

\displaystyle=|b|=|w|=|w^{\prime}|=0

\displaystyle-|v|

\displaystyle=|v^{\prime}|=1

\displaystyle-|u|=|u^{\prime}|=2

\displaystyle u^{\prime}u

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{P_{3}}

\displaystyle v^{\prime}v

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{P_{2}}

\displaystyle w^{\prime}w

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{P_{1}}

\displaystyle uu^{\prime}+vv^{\prime}+ww^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{Q}

\displaystyle ba

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle u^{\prime}v

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle v^{\prime}u

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle v^{\prime}w

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle w^{\prime}v

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle u^{\prime}w

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle w^{\prime}u

\displaystyle=0.

The non-zero differentials of the generating morphisms are given by

\displaystyle\partial(u)

\displaystyle=va

\displaystyle\partial(v)

\displaystyle=wb

\displaystyle\partial(w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-bv^{\prime}

\displaystyle\partial(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-au^{\prime};

the remaining differentials are uniquely determined by the graded Leibniz rule.
Similarly, as a graded quiver with relations, the graded cohomology category is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{u}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

The latter quiver is subject to the relation that the composite of any two
consecutive arrows vanishes.

We claim that
3
3

3

This computation goes back to Kontsevich,
see
[
Sei08
, Rmk. 3.11]
and compare with
[
HKK17
, Eq. 3.18]
.

m_{4}(\underline{u}^{\prime},\underline{w},\underline{b},\underline{a})=\pm\mathbf{1}_{P_{3}}.

The part of the necessary homotopy retract that is relevant to our computation is
the following:

\displaystyle i(\underline{a})

\displaystyle=a

\displaystyle i(\underline{b})

\displaystyle=b

\displaystyle i(\underline{w})

\displaystyle=w

\displaystyle i(\underline{u}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=u^{\prime}

\displaystyle p(u^{\prime}u)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{P_{3}}

\displaystyle h(va)

\displaystyle=u

\displaystyle h(wb)

\displaystyle=v.

Recall that
m_{4}
is defined combinatorially in terms of planar binary rooted
trees by interpolating the original composition operation with the homotopy
h
,
see for example
[
Val14
, p. 233]
. Since the composites
u^{\prime}w
and
ba
vanish
on the nose, the summands of

m_{4}(\underline{u}^{\prime},\underline{w},\underline{b},\underline{a})
that involve
first composing (after applying
i
) either the leftmost two or the rightmost
two morphisms vanish. This leaves only the two summands that involve first
composing
w
with
b
and then applying
h
. Finally, given that the composite

u^{\prime}h(wb)=u^{\prime}v=0
vanishes,
u^{\prime}=i(\underline{u}^{\prime})
w=i(\underline{w})
b=i(\underline{b})
a=i(\underline{a})
v=h(wb)
u=h(va)
\mathbf{1}_{P_{3}}=p(u^{\prime}u)

is the only non-zero summand of

m_{4}(\underline{u}^{\prime},\underline{w},\underline{b},\underline{a})
, which is what
we had to prove.

4.6.1
.

The above example shows that
[
Her16
, Thm. D]
cannot hold as stated.
Indeed,
[
Her16
, Thm. D]
claims that the compact derived category of an
acyclic quiver has a minimal
A_{\infty}
-model with only one non-zero higher
operation, namely
m_{3}
; the previous example shows that there are higher
non-vanishing operations in general. Notice also that
[
Her16
]
would
contradict our
Corollary

4.5.17
, as the latter implies that the
Hochschild class of
m_{4}
in the above example must be non-zero. The error in
the proof of
[
Her16
, Thm. D]
occurs in the proof of Prop. 3.8.1 therein,
where it is claimed that the higher operations
m_{n}
,
n>3
, vanish for the
minimal
A_{\infty}
-models of bounded derived categories of hereditary abelian categories
with enough projectives. There, the author argues that the higher composite of
a sequence of
n
morphisms in the graded cohomology category always
represents a morphism in the
(n-2)
-nd extension space between two complexes
that lie in the heart of the derived category, and these spaces indeed vanish
for
n>3
due to the hereditary assumption. However, as the example above
shows, the sequence

P_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\underline{a}}}{{\to}}P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\underline{b}}}{{\to}}P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\underline{w}}}{{\to}}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\underline{u}^{\prime}}}{{\to}}P_{3}

has total degree
2
and its higher composite yields a non-zero morphism

P_{3}\to P_{3}
of degree
0
(notice that
m_{4}
is a morphism of degree
-2
).

4.7.
Hochschild cohomology of sparse graded algebras

4.7.1
.

We fix a finite-dimensional self-injective algebra
\Lambda
and an algebra
automorphism
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
.

In this technical subsection we study the Hochschild cohomology of the

d
-sparse graded algebra

\Lambda(\sigma,d)\coloneqq\frac{\Lambda\langle\imath^{\pm 1}\rangle}{(\imath x-\sigma(x)\imath)_{x\in\Lambda}},\qquad|\imath|=-d,

(compare with
Remark

4.5.3
). The results in this section extend
results obtained in
[
Mur22
]
in the case
d=1
.

We begin with some general observations that hold for an arbitrary sparse graded
algebra.

4.7.2
.

Let
A
be a
d
-sparse graded algebra. The
fractional Euler derivation

\delta_{/d}\colon A\to A
is the degree
0
map

\delta_{/d}(a)=\frac{|a|}{d}\cdot a,

where the coefficient is indeed an integer in view of the sparseness of
A
.

4.7.3
.

Let
A
be a
d
-sparse graded algebra. The (usual) Euler derivation is

\delta=\delta_{/1}
. If
d
is invertible in the ground field, then

\delta_{/d}=\frac{1}{d}\delta
.

4.7.4
.

Let
A
be a
d
-sparse graded algebra and
\varphi\in\operatorname{C}^{p,q}\!\left(A\right)
a
Hochschild cochain. Then,

[\delta_{/d},\varphi]=\frac{q}{d}\cdot\varphi.

Moreover,
\delta_{/d}
is a Hochschild cocycle.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to those of
[
Mur22
, Prop. 3.2 and Cor. 3.3]

and is left to the reader.
∎

4.7.5
.

Let
A
be a
d
-sparse graded algebra. The Hochschild cohomology class of the
fractional Euler derivation, called
fractional Euler class
, is denoted

\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{1,0}\!\left(A\right).

4.7.6
.

The square of the fractional Euler class vanishes:

\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}^{2}=0\in\operatorname{HH}^{2,0}\!\left(A\right).

Proof.

The proof of
[
Mur22
, Prop. 3.8]
applies almost verbatim, only that one
needs to replace the cochain
\beta\in\operatorname{C}^{1,0}\!\left(A\right)
in
loc. cit.
by the cochain

\beta\colon x\longmapsto\frac{\frac{|x|}{d}\left(1-\frac{|x|}{d}\right)}{2}\cdot x.\qed

4.7.7
.

If
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})=2
, then
\operatorname{Sq}(\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\})=\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}
.

Proof.

Indeed, the claim follows from the fact that every integer its congruent to
its square modulo
2
(compare with
[
Mur22
, Prop. 3.5]
).
∎

Straightforward computations that combine the above propositions with the laws
of a Gerstenhaber algebra yield the following identities (compare with

[
Mur22
, Props. 3.6, 3.9, 3.10]
). We leave the proof to the reader.

4.7.8
.

Let
A
be a
d
-sparse graded algebra. The following identities hold for

x\in\operatorname{HH}^{p,dq}\!\left(A\right)
and
y\in\operatorname{HH}^{s,dt}\!\left(A\right)
:

\displaystyle[\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x,\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot y]

\displaystyle=(t-q)\cdot\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x\cdot y.

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sq}(\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x)

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{if $\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})\neq 2$ and $p+dq$ is odd.}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sq}(\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x)

\displaystyle=(q+1)\cdot\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x^{2}\quad\text{if $\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})=2$.}

\displaystyle y\cdot x

\displaystyle=[y,\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x]+\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot[y,x]\quad\text{if $t=-1$ and $d-s$ is even.}

We return to our object of interest in this subsection, which is the Hochschild
cohomology of the
d
-sparse graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
(see

4.7.1
).

4.7.9
.

Recall that, by definition, the following identity
holds in
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
:

\imath x\imath^{-1}=\sigma(x),\qquad x\in\Lambda.

Now, the automorphism
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
extends to
\Lambda(\sigma,d)

via the formula

\sigma(\imath)\coloneqq(-1)^{|\imath|}\imath=(-1)^{-d}\imath.

Since
\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{dk}=\left\{\imath^{-k}x\right|x\in\Lambda\}
, we see that the equality

\imath x\imath^{-1}=(-1)^{|x|}\sigma(x)

holds for all homogeneous elements
x\in\Lambda(\sigma,d)
.

4.7.10
.

Observe that the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
of graded algebra automorphisms of

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
acts naturally on the right by conjugation on the bigraded algebra

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
as in
Remark

4.4.1
,
since
\Lambda=\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{0}
is the degree
0
part. It also acts on

\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
since Hochschild–Tate cohomology satisfies the
same functoriality properties as Hochschild cohomology. In particular the
latter action preserves units. The canonical comparison map

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

is
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
-equivariant. Hence the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
preserves edge units.

A unit
u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
in the centre of
\Lambda
induces a
graded algebra automorphism
g_{u}\colon\Lambda(\sigma,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda(\sigma,d)
that is uniquely
determined by the requirements

g_{u}(x)=x,\quad x\in\Lambda=\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{0},\quad\text{and}\quad g_{u}(\imath)=\imath u^{-1}.

Indeed, these formulas define
g_{u}
on the graded algebra generators of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
. In order to check compatibility with the defining relations of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
in
4.7.1
, we observe that
g_{u}(\imath)=\imath u^{-1}
is a unit with inverse
u\imath^{-1}
and, moreover, for
x\in\Lambda=\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{0}

\displaystyle g_{u}(\imath)g_{u}(x)-g_{u}(\sigma(x))g_{u}(\imath)

\displaystyle=\imath u^{-1}x-\sigma(x)\imath u^{-1}=\imath(xu^{-1})-\imath(xu^{-1})=0.

Here we use that
u^{-1}x=xu^{-1}
since
u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
and
\sigma(x)\imath=\imath x
by the defining relations of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
.
This construction defines a group homomorphism

\displaystyle\gamma\colon Z(\Lambda)^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)),

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\longmapsto g_{u}.

Since
g_{u}
is the identity in degree
0
, the right action of
g_{u}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
on
x\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
consists of applying the morphism induced by
g_{u}^{-1}
, regarded as an automorphism of the coefficient graded
\Lambda
-bimodule, on Hochschild cohomology,

x^{g_{u}}=(g_{u}^{-1})_{*}(x)=(g_{u^{-1}})_{*}(x).

Furthermore, given
x\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

x^{g_{u}}=(g_{u^{-1}})_{*}(x)=x\cdot u=u\cdot x

since
g_{u^{-1}}
is given in degree
-d
by right multiplication with
u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}\subset Z(\Lambda)=\operatorname{HH}^{0,0}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
and the product in Hochschild cohomology is graded commutative. Therefore, the right action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
on
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
restricts along the group homomorphism
\gamma
to the multiplication action of
Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
on
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
.

4.7.11
.

Let
j\colon\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Lambda(\sigma,d)
be the inclusion of
\Lambda
as
the degree
0
part, and
\langle\sigma\rangle\subset\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
the
subgroup generated by the extension of the automorphism
\sigma
to

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
, see
Remark

4.7.9
. Then, the following statements hold:

(1)

There is a long exact sequence in Hochschild
cohomology

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n+1,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}-\sigma_{*}^{-1}\sigma^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

(2)

The maps

\begin{split}j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)&\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),\\
\sigma_{*}^{-1}\sigma^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)&\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),\end{split}

are morphisms of graded algebras.

(3)

The map

\partial\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet-1,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

is a morphism of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
-bimodules with square-zero image.

(4)

The
map

\partial\circ j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet+1,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

is given by
x\mapsto\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x
.

Proof.

This proof is similar to the union of the proofs of
[
Mur20b
, Prop. 2.3]
and
[
Mur22
, Prop. 4.3]
. The statement here is simpler because we work with graded algebras instead of categories. However, for algebras, this proposition is more general. It is actually a generalization of the case
d=1
. Therefore, since this result plays a crucial role in this paper, we include the proof with some straightforward computations left to the reader.

We have a short exact sequence of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-bimodules

\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}{}-\Gamma}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}\Lambda(\sigma,d)

where
\mu
is the product in
\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)
and
\Gamma
is defined as

\Gamma(a\otimes b)=a\imath^{-1}\otimes\imath b.

Indeed, using the isomorphisms of
\Lambda
-bimodules

\displaystyle{}_{\sigma^{i}}\Lambda_{1}\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma^{j}}\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}{}_{\sigma^{i+j}}\Lambda_{1},

\displaystyle a\otimes b

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;\sigma^{j}(a)b,

we see that, as
\Lambda
-bidmoules,

(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d))^{k}\cong\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}{}_{\sigma^{k}}\Lambda_{1}.

Through this decomposition,
\mu
maps all copies of
{}_{\sigma^{k}}\Lambda_{1}
to itself identically, and

\Gamma
maps the
i^{\text{th}}
direct summand to the
(i+1)^{\text{st}}
direct summand identically. Hence, the previous sequence is degree-wise a well-known short exact sequence.

The
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-bimodule
\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is the extension of scalars of
\Lambda
along
j^{\otimes^{2}}\colon\Lambda^{e}\to\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}
. The long exact sequence in the statement will be the one associated to the previous short exact sequence and the derived functors of
\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(-,\Lambda(\sigma,d))
. We just have to check that the following square is commutative
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom^{*}_{\Lambda^{e}}(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom^{*}_{\Lambda^{e}}(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(\Gamma,\Lambda(\sigma,d))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{-1}_{*}\sigma^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Here, the horizontal isomorphisms are the adjunction isomorphisms. Let us check this. Let
f\colon\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)\to\Lambda(\sigma,d)
be a
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-bimodule morphism. Since source and target are
d
-sparse, we can suppose that
f
has degree
kd
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. The image of
f
through the upper right corner is the
\Lambda
-bimodule morphism
g\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda(\sigma,d)
given by
g(x)=\sigma^{-1}f(\sigma(x)\otimes 1)
. The image through the lower left corner is the
\Lambda
-bimodule morphism
h\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda(\sigma,d)
given by

\displaystyle h(x)

\displaystyle=f\Gamma(x\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=f(x\imath^{-1}\otimes\imath)

\displaystyle=f(\imath^{-1}\imath x\imath^{-1}\otimes\imath)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{kd^{2}}\imath^{-1}f(\imath x\imath^{-1}\otimes 1)\imath

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-1}f(\sigma(x)\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=g(x).

Here we use that
f
is a
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-bimodule morphism and that
d^{2}\equiv d(\mod 2)
.

The morphisms
j^{*}
and
\sigma^{-1}_{*}\sigma^{*}
in the statement are algebra maps by functoriality properties of Hochschild cohomology. The multiplicative properties of
\partial
require a deeper analysis.

The
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-bimodule endomorphism
\Gamma
lifts to the projective resolution
\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}B_{\bullet}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)
in the following way,

\displaystyle\Gamma\colon\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}B_{k}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}B_{k}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d),

\displaystyle a\otimes x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}\otimes b

\displaystyle\longmapsto a\imath^{-1}\otimes\sigma(x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma(x_{k})\otimes\imath b.

Therefore, the mapping cone of the endomorphism
\mathbf{1}{}-\Gamma
of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}B_{\bullet}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is a projective resolution of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
as a
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-bimodule. This mapping cone is

C_{\mathbf{1}{}-\Gamma}=\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}B_{\bullet}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)\oplus\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}B_{\bullet-1}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)

with differential

\begin{pmatrix}d_{B}&0\\
\mathbf{1}{}-\Gamma&-d_{B}\end{pmatrix}.

Here
d_{B}
stands for the bar complex differential.

Consider the maps

\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}B_{\bullet}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}{}-\Gamma}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda(\sigma,d)\otimes_{\Lambda}B_{\bullet}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\sigma,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}B_{\bullet}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))

where
j_{*}
is defined by

j_{*}(a\otimes x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{j}\otimes b)=a\otimes j(x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes j(x_{j})\otimes b.

We could omit the
j
’s on the right because it is just the inclusion
\Lambda\subset\Lambda(\sigma,d)
.
The composite is null-homotopic, with explicit homotopy

h(a\otimes x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{j}\otimes b)=\\
\sum_{k=0}^{j}(-1)^{k}a\imath^{-1}\otimes\sigma(x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma(x_{k})\otimes\imath\otimes x_{k+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{j}\otimes b.

Indeed, it is easy to check that

j_{*}(\mathbf{1}{}-\Gamma)=d_{B}h+hd_{B}.

This gives rise to an explicit map of resolutions

\displaystyle C_{\mathbf{1}{}-\Gamma}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B_{\bullet}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)),

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\longmapsto j_{*}(x)+h(y).

Taking
\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(-,\Lambda(\sigma,d))
, we obtain a quasi-isomorphism

\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(C_{\mathbf{1}-\Gamma},\Lambda(\sigma,d)).

The Hochschild complex (source) is a differential graded algebra. We can endow the target with a differential graded algebra structure turning this map into an algebra map. It is the following square-zero extension

\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(C_{\mathbf{1}-\Gamma},\Lambda(\sigma,d))=\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\oplus{}_{\sigma^{-1}_{*}\sigma^{*}}\operatorname{C}^{\bullet-1,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)_{1}

of the differential graded algebra
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
by the twisted and then shifted bimodule
{}_{\sigma^{-1}_{*}\sigma^{*}}\operatorname{C}^{\bullet-1,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)_{1}
.
It is straightforward to check that the differential satisfies the Leibniz rule with respect to the product. Moreover, the quasi-isomorphism is an algebra map because, given
f,g\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
,

h^{*}(f\cdot g)=h^{*}(f)\cdot j^{*}(g)+(-1)^{|f|}\sigma^{-1}_{*}\sigma^{*}j^{*}(f)\cdot h^{*}(g).

This is tedious but straightforward to check.

The long exact sequence in the statement is induced by the square-zero extension

{}_{\sigma^{-1}_{*}\sigma^{*}}\operatorname{C}^{\bullet-1,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{2}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(C_{\mathbf{1}-\Gamma},\Lambda(\sigma,d))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

where
p_{1}
is an algebra map and
i_{2}
is a
\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(C_{\mathbf{1}-\Gamma},\Lambda(\sigma,d))
-bimodule morphism.
This proves that
\partial
, which is induced by
-i_{2}
, is a bimodule morphism. In cohomology, we can remove the left twisting by
\sigma^{-1}_{*}\sigma^{*}
since
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
maps to the kernel of
\mathbf{1}-\sigma^{-1}_{*}\sigma^{*}
.

The coordinates of the image of the fractional Euler derivation under the quasi-isomorphism are

j^{*}(\delta_{/d})=0,

because
\Lambda
is ungraded, and

h^{*}(\delta_{/d})=\imath^{-1}\delta_{/d}(\imath)=\frac{|\imath|}{d}\imath^{-1}\imath=-1,

hence it corresponds to
(0,-1)\in\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(C_{\mathbf{1}-\Gamma},\Lambda(\sigma,d))
.
The composite
\partial j^{*}
is induced by
-i_{2}p_{1}
and, in
\hom^{*}_{\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{e}}(C_{\mathbf{1}-\Gamma},\Lambda(\sigma,d))
,

(0,-1)\cdot(f,g)=(0,-f)=-i_{2}p_{1}(f,g).

This proves the last claim.
∎

We record the following immediate corollary of
Proposition

4.7.11
. Below,
we denote by
\langle\sigma\rangle\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}\Lambda(\sigma,d)
be the subgroup
generated by the extension of
\sigma
to
\Lambda(\sigma,d)

4.7.12
.

In the setting of
Proposition

4.7.11
, the inclusion

{j:\Lambda\to\Lambda(\sigma,d)}
induces a square-zero extension of bigraded algebras

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet-1,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)^{\langle\sigma\rangle},

where
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
and

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet-1,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
denote the bigraded algebra of
invariants and the shifted bimodule of coinvariants of the action of

{\langle\sigma\rangle\subset\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))}
on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
,
respectively (see
Remark

4.7.10
).

4.7.13
.

Corollary

4.7.12
implies that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
is graded
commutative with respect to the total degree as is

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
. Moreover

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
is a symmetric

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
-bimodule, that is a module in
the commutative sense. Furthermore, when the obvious map

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)^{\langle\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)_{\langle\sigma\rangle}

is an isomorphism,
Proposition

4.7.11
identifies the kernel of the
square-zero extension with the ideal generated by
\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}
. In
particular, in this case, multiplication by
\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}
in

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
induces a splitting of the square-zero extension. A
sufficient condition is that the order of
\sigma
as an automorphism of

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is finite (compare with the Periodicity
Conjecture of Erdmann and Skoworński
[
ES08
]
) and invertible in the ground field
\mathbf{k}
. Notice that the
order of
\sigma
as an automorphism of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is twice the order of

\sigma
as an automorphism of
\Lambda
if
d
is odd and
\mathbf{k}
has odd
characteristic, and they coincide otherwise, see
Remark

4.7.9
.

Recall that we have a canonical comparison map

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

from Hochschild cohomology to Hochschild–Tate cohomology, that is an
isomorphism in positive horizontal degree and an epimorphism in horizontal
degree
0
. In particular, we may identify
\operatorname{HH}^{>0,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
with
the (horizontal) positive part of
\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
.

We introduce the following auxiliary definition, see
[
Mur22
, Sec. 6]
for
justification for the terminology (in particular
[
Mur22
, Prop. 6.5 and
Cor. 6.7]
).

4.7.14
.

Let
p>0
.

(1)

A class

x\in\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{p}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{\sigma^{q}}{\Lambda}_{1}\right)

is an
edge unit
if it is a unit
\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
.

(2)

Let
j\colon\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Lambda(\sigma,d)
be the inclusion of

\Lambda
as the degree
0
part. A class

x\in\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
is an
edge unit
if the image of

x
under the map

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{p,q}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

is an edge unit.

The following property of edge units is key for later computations.

4.7.15
.

Let
x\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
be an edge unit. Then, the
left-multiplication map

\operatorname{HH}^{p,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{p+d+2,*-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),\qquad y\longmapsto x\cdot y,

is bijective for
p\geq 2
and surjective for
p=1
.

Proof.

Using
Proposition

4.7.11
, both claims can be proved with the same
argument used in the proof of
[
Mur22
, Lemma 6.8]
.
∎

The following result—a direct consequence of

Proposition

2.2.18
—is responsible for the

uniqueness
of enhancements in
Theorem

5.1.2
(compare with

[
Mur22
, Prop. 9.4]
4
4

4

The formula for
g_{u}
in the proof
of
[
Mur22
, Prop. 9.4]
(called
g(x)
therein for
x=u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
), where the case
d=1
is considered, is wrong in degrees

\neq 0,-1
unless
\sigma(u)=u
, since otherwise
u
is not a central
element in
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
and hence
g(x)
does not preserve products. The correct definition is the one in
Remark

4.7.10
in terms of generators and relations, which yields different degree-wise formulas. The rest of the argument in the proof of
[
Mur22
, Prop. 9.4]
is nevertheless valid since it only uses the explicit formulas in degrees
0
and
-1
.
). In particular, it is here that it is crucial for us
to work with finite-dimensional algebras and not with more general objects
such as locally-finite categories (see the proof of

Proposition

2.2.18
, in particular the use of

[
Che21
, Cor. 2.3]
and compare with
Remark

2.2.19
).

4.7.16
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional self-injective algebra that is twisted

(d+2)
-periodic with respect to an algebra automorphism

\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
,
d\geq 1
. Then, the set formed by the
edge units in
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
is an orbit for
the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
.

Proof.

By
Remark

4.7.10
, the orbit of an edge unit consists of edge units.
Let
x
and
x^{\prime}
be edge units in

\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)
and choose exact sequences

\eta\colon\quad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

and

\eta^{\prime}\colon\quad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to Q_{d+1}\to\cdots\to Q_{1}\to Q_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

of
\Lambda
-bimodules with projective-injective middle terms that represent

x
and
y
, respectively (see
Remark

4.5.16
).

Proposition

2.2.18
yields a morphism of exact sequences
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

where
u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
, which implies that

x^{\prime}=u\cdot x=x^{g_{u}},

see again
Remark

4.7.10
.
∎

5.
Existence and uniqueness of enhancements

5.0.1
.

We fix a finite-dimensional basic self-injective algebra
\Lambda
that is
twisted
{(d+2)}
-periodic for some
d\geq 1
with respect to an algebra automorphism

\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\Lambda}
and let

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda).

As in
Remark

4.5.3
, we consider the
d
-sparse graded algebra

\Lambda(\sigma,d)\coloneqq\frac{\Lambda\langle\imath^{\pm}\rangle}{(\imath x-\sigma(x)\imath)_{x\in\Lambda}},\qquad|\imath|=-d.

5.1.
Proof
A

In this subsection we give the proof
A
,
modulo the proof of the following main result (
Theorem

5.1.2
) that
we postpone until
Section

5.3
.

5.1.1
.

Let
g\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
be a graded algebra automorphism and

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},m_{3d+2},\dots)

a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
. We let

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)*g\coloneqq(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2}^{g},m_{2d+2}^{g},\dots),

where
m_{i}^{g}\coloneqq g^{-1}m_{i}g^{\otimes i}
, and note that this is also a
minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the
d
-sparse graded algebra

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
.

5.1.2
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

There exists a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)

on the
d
-sparse graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
whose restricted universal
Massey product

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)

is a unit in the Hochschild–Tate cohomology
\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

(equivalently, the class
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
is represented by an extension
all of whose middle terms are projective-injective
\Lambda
-bimodules).

(2)

Let

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)\quad\text{and}\quad(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2}^{\prime},m_{2d+2}^{\prime},\dots)

be minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
whose corresponding
restricted universal Massey products
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
and

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}^{\prime}\right\}
are units in the Hochsschild–Tate cohomology

\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
. Then, there exist a graded algebra
automorphism
g\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
and a quasi-isomorphism of minimal

A_{\infty}
-algebras

F\colon(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2}^{\prime},m_{2d+2}^{\prime},\dots)*g

such that
F_{0}=\mathbf{1}
and
F_{1}=\mathbf{1}
.

We need some recollections on the derived category of an
A_{\infty}
-algebra.
Given an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
, we denote by
A_{\infty}\operatorname{-Mod}(A)
the DG (!) category
of
(right)
A_{\infty}
-modules over
A
, that is the DG category of

A_{\infty}
-functors
A^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{C}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\mathbf{k})
from the opposite
A_{\infty}
-algebra of

A
(viewed as an
A_{\infty}
-category with a single object
*
) to the DG
category of cochain complexes of vector spaces, see for
example
[
Sei08
, Sec. I.1j]
. The
A_{\infty}
-categorical Yoneda embedding

A\longrightarrow A_{\infty}\operatorname{-Mod}(A),\qquad*\longmapsto A_{A}

yields a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras

A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{A}\coloneqq\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\!\left(A_{A},A_{A}\right)

where
\overline{A}
is the DG (!) algebra of endomorphisms of the free

A_{\infty}
-module
A_{A}
, see for example
[
Sei08
, Sec. I.2g]
(to our
knowledge, this important fact was first observed by Kontsevich and
Soibelman
[
KS06
, Sec. 6.2]
, but see also
[
LH
, Sec. 7.5]
). In particular, there is a canonical
isomorphism of graded algebras

H^{\bullet}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{\bullet}(\overline{A}).

Finally, the derived category of
A
is defined as

\operatorname{D}(A)\coloneqq H^{0}(A_{\infty}\operatorname{-Mod}(A));

this is a sensible definition since quasi-isomorphisms of
A_{\infty}
-modules are
also homotopy equivalences, see
[
Kel01
, Sec. 4]

or
[
LH
, Thm. 4.1.3.1]
while keeping in mind that we work over a field.
By construction, there is a canonical equivalence of triangulated categories

\operatorname{D}(\overline{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{D}(A),

which is in fact induced by the quasi-equivalence of DG categories

A_{\infty}\operatorname{-Mod}(\overline{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{\infty}\operatorname{-Mod}(A)

given by restriction of scalars along the quasi-isomorphism

A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\overline{A}
, see for example
[
Sei08
, Lemma I.2.10]
.
The following result can be regarded as a
(d+2)
-angulated analogue of one of
the main theorems in
[
Mur22
]
.

5.1.3
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional self-injective algebra that is twisted

(d+2)
-periodic with respect to an algebra automorphism
\sigma
and let

\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\colon\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda).

Then, the AL
(d+2)
-angulation
{\pentagon}_{\eta}
of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)

induced by any exact sequence
of
\Lambda
-bimodules

\eta\colon\qquad 0\to{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\to P_{d+1}\to\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\Lambda\to 0

with projective-injective middle terms (
Theorem

2.2.15
) admits an
enhancement. Moreover, if
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable, then the
enhancement is unique.

Proof.

Recall from
Proposition

2.2.18
that, up to equivalence, the AL

(d+2)
-angulation is independent of the choice of the exact sequence
\eta

(as long as it satisfies the conditions in the statement of the theorem).

We prove the first claim, that the AL
(d+2)
-angulation on

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
admits an enhancement. Firstly,

Theorem

5.1.2
(
1
) yields a minimal

A_{\infty}
-algebra structure

A\coloneqq(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)

on the
d
-sparse graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
whose restricted universal Massey
product

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)

is represented by an exact sequence all of whose middle terms are
projective-injective
\Lambda
-bimodules. Secondly, let

\overline{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\operatorname{D}(A)
be the smallest subcategory containing
A

that is closed under finite direct sums and direct summands, and

\mathcal{A}\subseteq A_{\infty}\operatorname{-Mod}(A)
the full DG subcategory spanned by the objects of

\overline{\mathcal{A}}
. By construction,

•

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
and
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
are graded Morita equivalent (indeed, the
graded category
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
identifies with that of projective graded

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-modules) and

•

the induced diagram of equivalences of categories
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes up to natural isomorphism. It follows that there is a commutative
diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}

where the vertical maps are isomorphisms of bigraded algebras and the
horizontal maps are induced by the corresponding inclusion of the degree
0

part. In particular, the restricted universal Massey product

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\right)

is also unit. Consequently, the DG category
\mathcal{A}
is Karoubian
pre-
(d+2)
-angulated (
Corollary

4.5.17

(
1
)
\Leftarrow
(
3
)) and the
standard
(d+2)
-angulation on
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),[d])
coincides with the AL

(d+2)
-angulation induced by any representative of
(-1)^{\Sigma_{i=1}^{d+2}i}j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
with projective-injective middle terms
(
Theorem

4.5.8
(
1
)). Finally,
by construction,
\mathcal{A}
is an enhancement of the AL
(d+2)
-angulation of the
pair
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
, which proves the first claim.

We prove the second claim, that any two enhancements of the AL

(d+2)
-angulation on
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
are equivalent under the
separability hypothesis. Indeed, suppose that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are two such
enhancements. By assumption, we may identify the graded endomorphism algebras
of basic additive generators
c\in H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
and
c^{\prime}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B})
with the

d
-sparse graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
. It follows that any choice of minimal

A_{\infty}
-models on the graded categories
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
and

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})
yield minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structures

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)\quad\text{and}\quad(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2}^{\prime},m_{2d+2}^{\prime},\dots)

on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
. Moreover, as a consequence of
Corollary

4.5.17

(
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
), which
requires the separability hypothesis on
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
, we see
that the restricted universal Massey products
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}
and

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}^{\prime}\right\}
are units in
\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
. Thus, by

Theorem

5.1.2
(
2
), there exist a graded algebra
automorphism
g\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
and a quasi-isomorphism of

A_{\infty}
-algebras

F\colon(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2}^{\prime},m_{2d+2}^{\prime},\dots)*g

such that
F_{1}=\mathbf{1}
. It is straightforward to verify that the tuple

(gF_{1},gF_{2},gF_{3},\dots)\colon(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2}^{\prime},m_{2d+2}^{\prime},\dots)

is also quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras. By well-known rectification
results for
A_{\infty}
-algebras
[
LV12
, Sec. 11.4.3]
, the existence of the
latter quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras implies that the DG algebras

A=\mathcal{A}(c,c)
and
B=\mathcal{B}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime})
are quasi-isomorphic. However the
quasi-isomorphism might not be direct, in the sense that it might be given by
zig-zag through intermediate DG algebras,

A=A^{(0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}A^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}A^{(n-1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}A^{(n)}=B.

Moreover, the quasi-isomorphisms
in the zig-zag might not preserve the unit, and the intermediate DG algebras
might not even be unital. Notwithstanding, since both
A
and
B
are unital
DG algebras, everything can be made unital using the results in

[
Mur14
, Prop. 6.2]
. Finally, any
unital quasi-isomorphism between
A
and
B
can be extended to a
quasi-equivalence between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in an essentially unique way (this is a well-know property of the
homotopy Karoubi envelope of a DG algebra, see for example

[
GHW21
, Sec. 4]
),

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{(0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(n-1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(n)}=\mathcal{B}.

This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.1.4
.

Theorems

4.5.8
and
5.1.3
show that if
\Lambda
is a finite-dimensional self-injective algebra that its twisted
(d+2)
-periodic and such that
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable, then the algebraic and the AL
(d+2)
-angulated structures on
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
coincide, see also
Remark

2.2.14
.

5.1.5
.

The use of derived categories of
A_{\infty}
-algebras in the proof of

Theorem

5.1.3
is not essential; these are
only used for constructing an explicit DG algebra that is quasi-isomorphic
to a given
A_{\infty}
-algebra, and any other choice of such a DG algebra would
suffice.

5.1.6
.

Let
d\geq 1
. For
i=1,2
, let
\mathcal{T}_{i}
be an algebraic triangulated category
with finite-dimensional morphism spaces and split idempotents, and
c_{i}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}

a basic
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object. Assume that the algebras
\mathcal{T}(c_{1},c_{1})
and

\mathcal{T}(c_{2},c_{2})
are isomorphic and write
\Lambda
for a representative of their isomorphism
class. Suppose, moreover, that the algebra
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable and
that there exists a
\Lambda
-bimodule
I
such
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{i}\supset\operatorname{add}(c_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{i}\supset\operatorname{add}(c_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(c_{i},-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{\Lambda}I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(c_{i},-)}

commutes up to natural isomorphism, for
i=1,2
. Then, there exists an equivalence of triangulated categories

F\colon\mathcal{T}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{T}_{2}

that restricts to an equivalence

F\colon\operatorname{add}(c_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{add}(c_{2}).

Proof.

The algebra
\Lambda
is finite-dimensional, self-injective and twisted
(d+2)
-periodic by
Proposition

2.2.10
, and the
\Lambda
-bimodule
I
is invertible because the left vertical and the horizontal arrows in the commutative square of the statement are equivalences. Moreover,
\Lambda
is basic since the
c_{i}
are, so
I\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}
for a some automorphism
\sigma
of
\Lambda
by
[
Bol84
, Prop. 3.8]
. Furthermore,
\sigma
realises the
(d+2)
-periodicity of
\Lambda
by
Theorem

2.2.1
and
Proposition

2.1.8
(
3
). Compare the hypotheses of
Theorem

5.1.3
.

Let

\mathcal{B}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, be pre-triangulated enhancements of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, so
that there are equivalences of triangulated categories

\mathcal{T}_{i}\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i})\qquad i=1,2.

Set
\mathcal{C}_{i}\coloneqq\operatorname{add}(c_{i})
,
i=1,2
, and let
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i}
,
i=1,2
,
be the full DG subcategories spanned by the objects in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
i=1,2
. In
particular, since
\operatorname{thick}(\mathcal{C}_{i})=\mathcal{T}_{i}
(see
Theorem

1.3.7
), the
inclusions
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, are Morita equivalences of DG
categories and, consequently, there are canonical equivalences of triangulated
categories

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{B}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i})\qquad i=1,2,

see
Theorem

3.1.6
. Next, by construction,
\mathcal{A}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, yields a
pre-
(d+2)
-angulated enhancements of

(\mathcal{C}_{i},[d]_{\mathcal{T}_{i}})\simeq(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),-\otimes_{\Lambda}I^{-1}),\qquad i=1,2,

where the pair of the left-hand side is equipped with the standard

(d+2)
-angulation as in
Theorem

2.2.1
. We may apply

Theorem

5.1.3
(see also
Remark

5.1.4
) to deduce that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and

\mathcal{A}_{2}
are isomorphic as objects of the homotopy category
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
. Thus,

\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are connected by a finite zig-zag of quasi-equivalences of
DG categories

(5.1.7)

\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{A}^{(0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(n-1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(n)}=\mathcal{A}_{2}

that extends to a finite zig-zag of equivalences of triangulated categories
(
Theorem

3.1.6
)

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}_{1})=\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}^{(0)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}^{(1)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}^{(n-1)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)})=\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}_{2}).

Choosing quasi-inverses of the left-pointing functors in the latter zig-zag
yields an equivalence of triangulated categories

H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{1})\simeq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A}_{2})\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{2})

which restricts to an equivalence

\mathcal{C}_{1}\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A}_{2})\simeq\mathcal{C}_{2},

which is what we needed to prove.
∎

5.1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an algebraic triangulated category with finite-dimensional
morphism spaces and split idempotents, and
c\in\mathcal{T}
a basic
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster
tilting object. Set
\Lambda=\mathcal{T}(c,c)
and suppose that the algebra
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable. Then
\mathcal{T}
has a unique enhancement.

Proof.

The argument is identical to the proof of
Theorem

5.1.6
, but choosing enhancements

\mathcal{B}_{i}
of
\mathcal{T}_{i}=\mathcal{T}
,
i=1,2
and with
c_{1}=c_{2}=c
. Indeed, letting

\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, as above, by

Remark

3.1.5
the zig-zag of
quasi-equivalences (
5.1.7
) shows that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are Morita
equivalent DG categories, but then so are the pre-triangulated DG categories

\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
. This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.1.9
.

Let
d\geq 1
. Let
\Lambda
be a
basic finite-dimensional algebra that is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic with respect
to an algebra automorphism
\sigma
. Then, there exists an algebraic triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
with finite-dimensional morphism spaces, split idempotents, and a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object such that
\mathcal{T}(c,c)\cong\Lambda
and the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\supset\operatorname{add}(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\supset\operatorname{add}(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(c,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(c,-)}

commutes up to natural isomorphism.

Proof.

Theorem

2.2.15
shows that the pair

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1})
can be endowed with an AL
(d+2)
-angulation, and
the latter admits an enhancement
\mathcal{A}
by

Theorem

5.1.3
. Let
{\mathcal{T}=\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})}
, which is an algebraic triangulated category with split idempotents, and
let
c\in\mathcal{T}
be the image of
\Lambda
under the identification

\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),

so that
\mathcal{T}(c,c)\cong\Lambda
. Since the canonical inclusion functor
identifies
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
with a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting subcategory

\mathcal{C}\subseteq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
(
Proposition

3.2.4
), it follows that
\mathcal{T}
has finite-dimensional morphism spaces and
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to the standard
(d+2)
-angulated category
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})
, so the square in the statement commutes.
∎

We now use
Theorems

5.1.6
,
5.1.8
and
5.1.9
to prove

A
.

Proof of
A
.

We analyse each
correspondence separately.

The map
(\mathcal{T},c)\mapsto(\mathcal{T}(c,c),\mathcal{T}(c,c[-d]))
is well defined.

Let
(\mathcal{T},c)
be a pair satisfying the conditions in

A
(
2
), so that
c\in\mathcal{T}
is a

d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object with finite-dimensional endomorphism algebra.
Then,
Proposition

2.2.10
shows that
\Lambda=\mathcal{T}(c,c)
is
self-injective and twisted
(d+2)
-periodic (here we use the assumption of the
ground field
\mathbf{k}
being perfect). In particular, as noted in

Remark

2.2.12
, the map

(\mathcal{T},c)\longmapsto(\mathcal{T}(c,c),\mathcal{T}(c,c[-d]))

in
A
is well defined since the
\Lambda
-bimodule
I=\mathcal{T}(c,c[-d])
renders the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(c,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{\Lambda}I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(c,-)}

commutative up to natural isomorphism (compare with the proof of
Proposition

2.2.9
).

The map
(\mathcal{T},c)\mapsto(\mathcal{T}(c,c),\mathcal{T}(c,c[-d]))
is surjective.
Let
\Lambda
be a basic finite-dimensional algebra that is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic and
I
an invertible
\Lambda
-bimodule isomorphic to
\Omega^{d+2}_{\Lambda^{e}}(\Lambda)
in the stable category of
\Lambda
-bimodules. Then
I\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}
for a certain automorphism
\sigma
of
\Lambda
by
[
Bol84
, Prop. 3.8]
. Furthermore,
\sigma
realises the
(d+2)
-periodicity of
\Lambda
by the stable isomorphism
I\cong\Omega^{d+2}_{\Lambda^{e}}(\Lambda)
. Hence
Theorem

5.1.9
applies.

The map
(\mathcal{T},c)\mapsto(\mathcal{T}(c,c),\mathcal{T}(c,c[-d]))
is injective.
This follows from
Theorem

5.1.6
. Here
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable for
\Lambda=\mathcal{T}(c,c)
because the ground field
\mathbf{k}
is perfect.

The map
A\mapsto(\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A),A)
is
surjective.
Indeed,
given an algebraic triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
with a basic
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster
tilting object
c\in\mathcal{T}
, any choice of enhancement
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{T}
yields a DG
algebra
A
together with an equivalence of triangulated categories

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{T},\qquad A\longmapsto c.

Explicitly, by assumption there exists an equivalence of
triangulated categories
\mathcal{T}\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
; identify
\mathcal{T}
with

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
along this equivalence and let
A\coloneqq\mathcal{A}(c,c)
be the DG
algebra of endomorphisms of
c
viewed as an object of
\mathcal{A}
. Since

d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting objects are in particular classical generators of the
ambient triangulated category
(
Theorem

1.3.7
),
Theorem

3.1.6
yields an equivalence of
triangulated categories

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\qquad A\longmapsto c,

and the claim follows.

The map
A\mapsto(\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A),A)
is
injective.
The injectivity of the map is a direct consequence of

Theorem

5.1.2
(
2
). Indeed, let
A
and
B
be DG
algebras satisfying the conditions in (
1
) and suppose that
the pairs
(\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A),A)
and
(\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(B),B)
are equivalent; explicitly, this means
that there exists an equivalence of triangulated categories

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(B),\qquad A\longmapsto B;

here we use that
A
and
B
are assumed to be basic objects of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)
.
In particular, we may and we will identify the graded algebras
H^{\bullet}(A)
and
H^{\bullet}(B)
via
this equivalence. Choose minimal

A_{\infty}
-models

(H^{\bullet}(A),m_{d+2}^{A},m_{2d+2}^{A},\dots)\text{ and }(H^{\bullet}(B),m_{d+2}^{B},m_{2d+2}^{B},\dots).

of
A
and
B
, respectively. By
Corollary

4.5.17
, that the restricted universal Massey
products
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}^{A}\right\}
and
j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}^{B}\right\}
are edge units, and

Theorem

5.1.2
(
2
) shows that, up to
quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras, there is a unique

A_{\infty}
-structure on
H^{\bullet}(A)=H^{\bullet}(B)
with this property. Consequently,

A
and
B
are quasi-isomorphic DG algebras (compare with the proof of

Theorem

5.1.3
). This finishes the proof of
the theorem.

Uniqueness of enhancements.
Finally, we need to show that the triangulated categories that satisfy the
conditions in
Theorem

5.1.3
(
2
)
admit a unique enhancement. This follows from
Theorem

5.1.8
.
∎

5.2.
Proof of
B

Let
A
be a graded vector space. Essentially by definition, a minimal

A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
A
can be identified with a point in the
mapping space (a simplicial set)

\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)=\operatorname{Map}_{\operatorname{dgOp}}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)

of operadic maps from the
A_{\infty}
-operad
\mathtt{A}_{\infty}
to the endomorphism operad

\mathtt{End}(A)
of
A
viewed as a cochain complex with vanishing differential,
computed in the model category
\operatorname{dgOp}
of differential graded
(non-symmetric) operads

[
Lyu11
,
Mur11
]
.
By
[
Fre09
, Thm. 5.2.1]
or
[
Mur16
, Cor. 2.3]
,
two points in the space
\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)
lie in the same path-connected
component if and only if the corresponding minimal
A_{\infty}
-structures are related by
a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras
F
with
F_{0}=\mathbf{1}
and
F_{1}=\mathbf{1}

(compare with
B
). Moreover, the mapping space

\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)
is the homotopy limit of the tower of fibrations

\cdots\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{i+2},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{3},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{2},\mathtt{End}(A)\right),

where
\mathtt{A}_{n}
is the operad of
A_{n}
-algebra structures.
5
5

5

Recall that
an
A_{n}
-algebra has operations

m_{i}\colon A^{\otimes i}\longrightarrow A(2-i),\qquad 1\leq i\leq n,

that satisfy all the
A_{\infty}
-equations that involve only these operations;
in particular, an
A_{\infty}
-algebra yields an
A_{n}
-algebra by discarding the
operations
m_{i}
,
i>n
. Notice also that there is no condition of the
n
-ary
multiplicaton in an
A_{n}
-algebra when
m_{1}=0
, that is when
A
is minimal.
For example, a minimal
A_{3}
-algebra consists of an
associative
graded
algebra with an
arbitrary
ternary operation.

Thus, a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure
x_{\infty}\in\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)

on
A
yields minimal
A_{i+2}
-algebra structures

x_{i}\in\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{i+2},\mathtt{End}(A)\right),\qquad i\geq 0,

by restriction. To simplify the notation, we write

X_{i}\coloneqq\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{i+2},\mathtt{End}(A)\right),\qquad i\geq 0,
