so that we have a tower of
pointed
fibrations

\cdots\twoheadrightarrow X_{i+2}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{0}.

The above perspective on
A_{\infty}
-structures is advantageous in that it
enables us to apply robust techniques from homotopy theory to compute the set of
path-connected components of the homotopy limit
\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)
. Indeed,
suppose that
A
is a
d
-sparse graded algebra. In this case, the results in

[
Mur20a
]
provide an extension of the classical Bousfield–Kan (fringed)
spectral sequence
[
BK72
, Ch. IX, Sec. 4]
with useful computational
properties, of which we highlight the following:

•

The classical Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence is only defined on the
upper-half of the bisection of the first quadrant, that is
E_{r}^{s,t}
is
defined for
t\geq s\geq 0
. In contrast, the extension of the Bousfield–Kan
spectral sequence is defined in most of the right half-plane; however, the
extended region of definition ‘converges’ to that of the classical
Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence as
{r\to\infty}
, see

Figure

1
and
[
Mur20a
]
for details.

\scriptscriptstyle 2r-3
\scriptstyle s
\scriptstyle t

Figure 1.
Range of definition of the extended Bousfield–Kan spectral
sequence (
r=5
). The red region is where the classical Bousfield–Kan
spectral sequence is defined; the extended spectral sequence is defined
also in the blue region.

•

The extended Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence is partially-defined even if
we only have a minimal
A_{i+2}
-structure on
A
, so just the bottom part of the tower of fibrations is pointed. More precisely, the extended
spectral sequence is defined up to the terms of page

\lfloor\frac{i+3}{2}\rfloor
, see
[
Mur20a
, Def. 5.1]
.

•

Suppose given a minimal
A_{i+2}
-algebra structure on
A
, that is a base point

x_{i}\in X_{i}=\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{i+2},\mathtt{End}(A)\right).

The obstruction to the existence of a minimal
A_{i+3}
-algebra structure

x_{i+1}\in x_{i+1}=\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{i+3},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)

such that
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
have the same underlying minimal
A_{i-r+3}
-algebra
structure

x_{i-r+1}\in X_{i-r+1}=\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{i-r+3},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)

lies in the term
E_{r}^{i+1,i}
,
1\leq r\leq\lfloor\tfrac{i+3}{2}\rfloor
.
This obstruction vanishes precisely when such a minimal
A_{i+3}
-algebra structure
exists, see
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 5.4]
.

•

The
E_{2}^{\bullet,*}
-terms of the extended Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence are given
by
[
Mur20a
, Cor. 6.3]

E_{2}^{s,t}=\begin{cases}\operatorname{HH}^{s+2,-t}\!\left(A\right)&s>0,\ t\in\mathbb{Z};\\
\operatorname{Z}^{2,-t}(\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right))&s=0,\ t>0.\end{cases}

In particular, since the graded algebra
A
is
d
-sparse,
E_{2}^{s,t}=0
if

t\not\in d\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, the spectral sequence differentials
\partial_{i}=0
are necessarily trivial for
2\leq i\leq d
and
E_{d+1}^{s,t}=E_{2}^{s,t}
wherever they are defined.

5.2.1
.

By construction, the spectral sequence described above is related to the
homotopy groups of the space
\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(A)\right)
, which turn out to be computed in
terms of Hochschild cohomology of
A_{\infty}
-algebras,
see
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 6.9]
for a precise statement. In particular, notice that the spectral sequence
above is different from those usually considered in the context of deformation
problems, such as the one discussed in
[
Sei15
, Ch. 3]
. Seidel’s spectral
sequence is a particular case of the third sequence in
Elmendorf–Kriz–Mandell–May’s
[
EKMM07
, Thm. 1.6]
in the context of ‘brave new algebra.’ For the relation between these spectral sequences and ours, see
[
Ang11
, Sec. 2]
and
[
Mur20a
, Sec. 7]
.

A minimal
A_{i+2}
-algebra structure on
A
has an
m_{d+2}
operation for any
i\geq d
, but we need
i>d
to ensure that
m_{d+2}
is a cocycle, whose cohomology class

\{m_{d+2}\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(A\right)

also deserves to be called
universal Massey product of length
d+2
, and
i>2d
so that

\operatorname{Sq}(\{m_{d+2}\})=0.

5.2.2
.

Let
A
be a
d
-sparse graded algebra equipped with a minimal
A_{2d+3}
-algebra structure, so the spectral sequence is defined up to the
E_{d+2}^{\bullet,*}
terms. The spectral sequence differential

\partial_{d+1}\colon E_{d+1}^{s,t}\longrightarrow E_{d+1}^{s+d+1,t+d}

is given by

\partial_{d+1}(x)=\pm[\{m_{d+2}\},x]

wherever it is defined, except for
(s,t)=(d-1,d)
in
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})=2
, where it is given by

\partial_{d+1}(x)=[\{m_{d+2}\},x]+x^{2}.

5.2.3
.

The differential defined in
Proposition

5.2.2
is the first possibly
non-trivial spectral sequence differential beyond page
1
. Notice also that
x^{2}=0
in
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})\neq 2
by graded commutativity.

Proof of
Proposition

5.2.2
.

The general formula follows from
[
Mur20a
, Lem. 6.1]
by the same argument as in the proof of
[
Mur20a
, Thm. 6.5]
, which is the case
d=1
. Condition
m\leq s+1
in the statement of
[
Mur20a
, Lem. 6.1]
is actually unnecessary. Indeed, as indicated therein, this lemma follows directly from
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 3.5]
where no upper bound is required. In our current context, where
A
is
d
-sparse,
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 3.5]
is used for
r=s+1
,
s=d+1
and
t=1
and only the first and last summands (
p=2,d+2
) produce possibly non-vanishing elements since
m_{i+2}=0
for
0<i<d
. Hence the proof goes through as for
[
Mur20a
, Thm. 6.5]
.

In the special case
(s,t)=(d-1,d)
we must invoke
[
Mur20a
, Cors. 3.9,3.10]
instead, as in the proof of
[
Mur20a
, Thm. 6.5]
, here for
r=2d+1
and
s=1
.
∎

5.2.4
.

For
s=0
and
t>0
,
x\in E^{0,t}_{2}=\operatorname{Z}^{2,-t}(\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right))
is a cocycle. Hence, on the right hand side of the formula for
\partial_{d+1}(x)
in
Proposition

5.2.2
, we understand that
x
stands for its Hochschild cohomology class.

5.2.5
.

A
d
-sparse Massey algebra

(A,m)
is a pair given by a
d
-sparse graded algebra
A
and a Hochschild cohomology class

m\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(A\right)

with trivial Gerstenhaber square

\operatorname{Sq}(m)=0.

The
Hochschild complex
of a
d
-sparse Massey algebra is the bigraded vector space
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,m\right)
defined as
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
in horizontal degrees
\bullet\geq 2
and zero otherwise, with bidegree
(d+1,-d)
differential

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{s,t}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{s+d+1,t-d}\!\left(A\right),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto[m,x],

except in bidegree
(d+1,-d)
, where it is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{d+1,-d}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{2(d+1),-2d}\!\left(A\right),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto[m,x]+x^{2}.

The cohomology of this complex is the
Hochschild cohomology
of the
d
-sparse Massey algebra, and it is denoted by
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,m\right)
.

5.2.6
.

The Hochschild complex of a
d
-sparse Massey algebra is indeed a complex since

[m,[m,x]]=[\operatorname{Sq}(m),x]=0

by the Gerstenhaber algebra relations. Moreover, if
x
is in bidegree
(d+1,-d)

[m,[m,x]+x^{2}]=[m,[m,x]]+[m,x^{2}]=[\operatorname{Sq}(m),x]+[m,x]\cdot x-x\cdot[m,x]=0.

Notice also that
x^{2}=0
in
\operatorname{char}(k)\neq 2
by graded commutativity.

5.2.7
.

Let
A
be a
d
-sparse graded algebra equipped with a minimal
A_{2d+3}
-algebra structure.
The terms
E_{d+1}^{s,t}
of the (truncated) extended Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence constructed in
[
Mur20a
]
are defined for
t\geq s
and for
s\geq 2d-1
and
t\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, the differential

\partial_{d+1}\colon E_{d+1}^{s,t}\longrightarrow E_{d+1}^{s+d+1,t+d}

is defined except for
0\leq s=t\leq d
. Furthemore,
E_{d+2}^{s,t}
is given
by the cohomology of
(E_{d+1}^{\bullet,*},\partial_{d+1})
whenever the incoming and
outgoing differentials are defined on
E_{d+1}^{s,t}
. Notice that the
assumptions on
A
imply that
(A,\{m_{d+2}\})
is a
d
-sparse Massey algebra. Moreover,
Proposition

5.2.2
shows that

(E_{d+1}^{\bullet,*},\partial_{d+1})
surjects onto
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet+2,-*}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)
whenever the source is defined, and this surjection is actually an isomorphism for
\bullet>0
. Therefore, we deduce that

E^{s,t}_{d+2}\cong\operatorname{HH}^{s+2,-t}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)

in the following cases:

•

t>s
,

•

t=s>d
,

•

s\geq 3d
,
t\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We are ready to prove
B
.

Proof of
B
.

Let
A
be a
d
-sparse
graded algebra and

B=(A,m_{d+2}^{B},m_{2d+2}^{B},m_{3d+2}^{B},\dots)\qquad\text{and}\qquad C=(A,m_{d+2}^{C},m_{2d+2}^{C},m_{3d+2}^{C},\dots)

minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
A
, which we identify with points

x_{\infty},y_{\infty}\in X_{\infty}=\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(A)\right),

respectively. Suppose that there is an equality of universal Massey products

m=\left\{m_{d+2}^{B}\right\}=\left\{m_{d+2}^{C}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(A\right)

and that

\operatorname{HH}^{p+2,-p}\!\left(A,m\right)=0,\qquad p>d.

As explained at the beginning of this subsection, we need to prove that
x_{\infty}
and
y_{\infty}
lie in
the same connected component of
X_{\infty}
. We take
x_{\infty}\in X_{\infty}
as a base point for the definition of the spectral sequence in
[
Mur20a
]
. By
Remark

5.2.7

E^{p,p}_{d+2}=\operatorname{HH}^{p+2,-p}\!\left(A,m\right)=0,\qquad p>d,

therefore

E^{p,p}_{r}=0,\qquad p>d,\quad d+2\leq r\leq\infty.

Denote by
x_{i},y_{i}\in X_{i}
the images of
x_{\infty},y_{\infty}\in X_{\infty}
along the restriction map
X_{\infty}\to X_{i}
. We have chosen
x_{i}
as the base point of
X_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq\infty
, hence
\pi_{0}(X_{i})
is a set pointed by
[x_{i}]
. We will inductively show that
[x_{i}]=[y_{i}]\in\pi_{0}(X_{i})
for
0\leq i\leq\infty
.

(
i=0
) Since
A
has trivial differential, the set
\pi_{0}(X_{0})
is in
bijection with the set of degree
0
maps of graded vector spaces

A\otimes A\to A
, and the class
[x_{0}]
corresponds to the product
operation of

A
; the class
[y_{0}]
also corresponds to the product operation of
A

since
A
is the underlying graded algebra of both minimal

A_{\infty}
-algebra structures
B
and
C
. We distinguish between the
various natural cases:

(
0<i<d)
Since the graded algebra
A
is
d
-sparse, in this range the
set
\pi_{0}(X_{i})
is in bijection with the set of graded associative algebra structures on the underlying graded vector space of
A
, and both
[x_{i}]
and
[y_{i}]
correspond the product of
A
by the same reason as above.

(
i=d
) The previous case shows, in particular, that

[x_{d-1}]=[y_{d-1}]
. Therefore the classes
[x_{d}]
and
[y_{d}]
lie in

\ker(\pi_{0}X_{d}\to\pi_{0}X_{d-1})\cap\operatorname{im}(\pi_{0}X_{2d}\to\pi_{0}X_{d})

and, according to
[
Mur20a
, Eqn. 4.8]
, this intersection is in fact equal to

E^{d,d}_{d+1}=E^{d,d}_{2}=\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(A\right).

The elements in Hochschild cohomology corresponding to the classes
[x_{d}]

and
[y_{d}]
are the universal Massey products

\left\{m_{d+2}^{B}\right\}=\left\{m_{d+2}^{C}\right\}
, which are equal by hypothesis and
hence we obtain the required equality
[x_{d}]=[y_{d}]
.

(
d<i<\infty
) As in the previous case, the classes
[x_{i}]
and
[y_{i}]

lie in the intersection

\ker(\pi_{0}X_{i}\to\pi_{0}X_{i-1})\cap\operatorname{im}(\pi_{0}X_{2i}\to\pi_{0}X_{i})=E_{i+1}^{i,i}=0,

see
[
Mur20a
, Eqn. 4.8]
, therefore
[x_{i}]=[y_{i}]
.

(
i=\infty
) We deduce the desired equality
[x_{\infty}]=[y_{\infty}]
from the following two facts:

•

[
BK72
, Sec. IX.3.1]
There is a Milnor short
exact sequence of pointed sets

*\longrightarrow\varprojlim\nolimits^{1}\pi_{1}(X_{i})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(X_{\infty})\longrightarrow\varprojlim\pi_{0}(X_{i})\longrightarrow*

and, by the previous discussion, the classes
[x_{\infty}]
and
[y_{\infty}]
have
the same image under the canonical map

\pi_{0}(X_{\infty})\to\varprojlim\pi_{0}(X_{i})
, namely the base point of
\varprojlim\pi_{0}(X_{i})
.

•

[
BK72
, Sec. IX.5.4]
For every
s\geq 0
, the term

E_{r}^{s,s+1}
stabilises for
r\geq\max\{d+2,s+1\}
since the source of the incoming differential is
E_{r}^{s-r,s-r+1}=0
being in the left half-plane (
s-r\leq s-(s+1)<0
), and the target of the outgoing differential is
E_{r}^{s+r,s+r}=0
since
s+r\geq r\geq d+2>d
. Therefore
\varprojlim\nolimits^{1}\pi_{1}(X_{i})=0
. This implies that the inverse image of the base point along
the natural map of pointed sets

\pi_{0}(X_{\infty})\to\varprojlim\pi_{0}(X_{i})
consists just of the source’s base point, hence
[x_{\infty}]=[y_{\infty}]
by the last observation in the previous item.

This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

For the sake of completeness, we observe that
B

implies Kadeishvili’s intrinsic formality theorem, which we state in its version
for minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebras.

5.2.8
[
Kad88
]
, see also
[
ST01
, Thm. 4.7]
.

Let
A
be a graded algebra such that

\operatorname{HH}^{p+2,-p}\!\left(A,A\right)=0,\qquad p\geq 1.

If
B
and
C
are minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebras with
H^{\bullet}(B)=H^{\bullet}(C)=A
as
graded algebras, then
B
and
C
are
A_{\infty}
-isomorphic through an

A_{\infty}
-isomorphism with identity linear part. In particular, the graded
algebra
A
is intrinsically formal.

Proof.

Endow the graded algebra
A
with the trivial
1
-sparse Massey algebra
structure
(A,0)
and notice that, in fact, this is the only possible
such structure since
\operatorname{HH}^{3,-1}\!\left(A,A\right)=0
by assumption. We will apply

B
to
(A,0)
. By definition, the cochain
complex
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,0\right)
has vanishing differential and therefore the
Hochschild cohomology of the
1
-sparse Massey algebra
(A,0)

coincides with the (ordinary) Hochschild cohomology of the graded algebra
A
.
In particular,

\operatorname{HH}^{p+2,-p}\!\left(A,0\right)=\operatorname{HH}^{p+2,-p}\!\left(A\right)=0,\qquad p>1,

by assumption. Thus, we may apply
B
to
the
1
-sparse Massey algebra
(A,0)
and the first claim follows. The second
claim follows by taking
C=A
viewed as a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra with
vanishing higher operations.
∎

We record the following vanishing properties that are crucial to the proof of

Theorem

5.3.3
below, compare with the hypothesis
in
Remark

5.2.7
and the assumptions in
B
.

5.2.9
.

Let
A=\Lambda(\sigma,d)
as in
5.0.1
. Assume we have at least a minimal
A_{2d+3}
-algebra structure on
A
such that
\{m_{d+2}\}
is an edge unit. Then the terms
E_{d+2}^{s,t}
in the
extended Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence associated to the tower of
fibrations

\cdots\twoheadrightarrow X_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{0}

can be non-zero only in the following cases (
Figure

2
):

\scriptscriptstyle d
\scriptscriptstyle d
\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 7.3612pt3d-1
\scriptscriptstyle 2d+1\hskip 7.3612pt
\scriptstyle s
\scriptstyle t

Figure 2
.
The
E_{d+2}^{\bullet,*}
-page of the extended Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence
can be non-trivial only in the green part
(d=4)
, see

Proposition

5.2.9
.

•

s=0
and
t=0
.

•

t=kd
,
k\geq 1
, and
0\leq s\leq d
.

•

t=kd
,
k\leq 2
, and
2d+1\leq s\leq 3d-1
.

Proof.

We are going to prove that

\operatorname{HH}^{s,t}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)=0,\qquad s>d+2.

Hence, the proposition follows from the relation between
E_{d+2}^{\bullet,*}
and the Hochschild cohomology of the
d
-sparse Massey algebra
(A,\{m_{d+2}\})
in
Remark

5.2.7
, and from the fact that
\operatorname{HH}^{s,t}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)
is concentrated in
t\in d\mathbb{Z}
since
A
is
d
-sparse.

Consider the degree
(d+2,-d)
endomorphism of
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)
given by the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{s,t}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{s+d+2,t-d}\!\left(A\right),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x,

if
(s,t)\neq(d+1,-d)
,
see
Proposition

4.7.15
, and by

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{d+1,-d}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{2d+3,-2d}\!\left(A\right),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x+\{\delta_{/d}\}\cdot x^{2}.

The latter map is also bijective. This can be checked as in the proof of
[
Mur22
, Thm. 9.1]
. The remaining components of this endomorphism are maps from
0
to possibly non-trivial targets, so it not a point-wise isomorphism everywhere.

Indeed, the previous maps assemble to a cochain map because, by the Gerstenhaber algebra relations,

\displaystyle[\{m_{d+2}\},\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x]

\displaystyle=[\{m_{d+2}\},\{m_{d+2}\}]\cdot x+\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot[\{m_{d+2}\},x]

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Sq}(\{m_{d+2}\})\cdot x+\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot[\{m_{d+2}\},x]

\displaystyle=\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot[\{m_{d+2}\},x],

and if
x
has bidegree
(d+1,-1)
then

\displaystyle[\{m_{d+2}\},\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x+\{\delta_{/d}\}\cdot x^{2}]

\displaystyle=[\{m_{d+2}\},\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x]+[\{m_{d+2}\},\{\delta_{/d}\}\cdot x^{2}]

\displaystyle=\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot[\{m_{d+2}\},x]+[\{m_{d+2}\},\{\delta_{/d}\}]\cdot x^{2}

\displaystyle=\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot[\{m_{d+2}\},x]+\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x^{2}

\displaystyle=\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot\left([\{m_{d+2}\},x]+x^{2}\right),

see
Proposition

4.7.4
.

Since the previous cochain endomorphism is mostly given by isomorphisms, it induces isomorphisms in most of the cohomology. Namely,

\operatorname{HH}^{s,t}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)\cong\operatorname{HH}^{s+d+2,t-d}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right),\qquad s>d+2,

because in these bidegrees the endomorphism of
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)
is an isomorphism on the source and target of the incoming and outgoing differentials.

Strikingly, the previous endomorphism of the complex
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)
is null-homotopic. The chain homotopy is given by the bidegree
(1,0)
maps

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{s,t}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{s+1,t}\!\left(A\right),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\{\delta_{/d}\}\cdot x.

Indeed, by
Proposition

4.7.8
,

\displaystyle\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x

\displaystyle=[\{m_{d+2}\},\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x]+\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot[\{m_{d+2}\},x],

in particular if
x
has bidegree
(d+1,-d)
,

\displaystyle\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x+\{\delta_{/d}\}\cdot x^{2}

\displaystyle=[\{m_{d+2}\},\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x]+\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot\left([\{m_{d+2}\},x]+x^{2}\right),

and if
d>1
and
x
has bidegree
(d,-d)
,

\displaystyle\{m_{d+2}\}\cdot x

\displaystyle=[\{m_{d+2}\},\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x]+\left(\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x\right)^{2}+\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot[\{m_{d+2}\},x]

\displaystyle=[\{m_{d+2}\},\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot x]+\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot[\{m_{d+2}\},x],

since the product is graded commutative and
\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}^{2}=0
by
Proposition

4.7.6
.

Therefore, the previous isomorphisms on the Hochschild cohomology of the
d
-sparse Massey algebra
(A,\{m_{d+2}\})
are trivial maps. This implies that

\operatorname{HH}^{s,t}\!\left(A,\{m_{d+2}\}\right)=0,\qquad s>d+2,

as desired.
∎

5.3.
Proof of
Theorem

5.1.2

In this subsection we complete the proof of

A
by providing a proof of

Theorem

5.1.2
. We remind the reader of our standing assumptions
for this section (
5.0.1
).

5.3.1
.

We denote by
\operatorname{S}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)
the set of (minimal)
A_{\infty}
-algebra structures

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},m_{3d+2},\dots)

on the
d
-sparse graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
whose restricted universal Massey
product

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\right)

is an edge unit. Similarly, we denote by
\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)
the quotient
of
\operatorname{S}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)
by the equivalence relation given by the existence
of a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras
F
with

F_{1}=\mathbf{1}
.

5.3.2
.

The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
of graded algebra automorphisms of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
acts
on the right on the set
\operatorname{S}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)
via the formula in

5.1.1
, and this action descends to the quotient

\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)
(compare with
Remarks

4.4.1
and
4.7.10
). Moreover, the set of
path-connected components of the space
\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\right)
of
minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
contains the quotient set

\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)
, see
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 6.9]
.

We shall prove the following theorem.

5.3.3
.

Suppose that
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable. The following statements hold:

(1)

The
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
-equivariant map

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

\displaystyle(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)

\displaystyle\longmapsto j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}

is injective.

(2)

The image of
\Phi
consists precisely of
the edge units in
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
.

(3)

The set of orbits

\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)/\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))

is a singleton.

Proof.

The map

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

\displaystyle(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)

\displaystyle\longmapsto j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}

is clearly
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
-equivariant and, by definition, its image
consists of units in the Hochschild–Tate cohomology

\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
(the set of Hochschild–Tate units in bidegree

(d+2,-d)
is non-empty since the algebra
\Lambda
is twisted

(d+2)
-periodic with respect to the algebra automorphism

\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\Lambda
, see

5.0.1
and
Remark

4.5.16
).

Notice also that statement (
3
) follows immediately from
statements (
1
) and (
2
)
and
Proposition

4.7.16
: statements (
1
) and
(
2
) imply that we may identify

\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)/\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
with the set of orbits of edge units
in
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
, and the latter set is a singleton by

Proposition

4.7.16
. Recall that

X_{i}=\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{i+2},\mathtt{End}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\right),\qquad i\geq 0,

is the space of minimal
A_{i+2}
-algebra structures on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
, and that
we have a tower of fibrations

\cdots\twoheadrightarrow X_{i+2}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{0}

as well as a distinguished base point
x_{0}\in X_{0}
given by multiplication on

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
. As in
Section

5.2
, we identify

X_{\infty}=\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\right),

with the (homotopy) limit of the above tower. In particular, we have
restriction/truncation maps
X_{\infty}\to X_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
.

Proof of statement (
2
):
We have to prove that
every edge unit is in the image of
\Phi
. Let

x\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
be an edge unit. We need to prove that
there exists an
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure

x_{\infty}\in X_{\infty}=\operatorname{Map}\!\left(\mathtt{A}_{\infty},\mathtt{End}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\right),\qquad x_{\infty}\longmapsto x_{0},

whose underlying graded algebra structure is the given one
(
Remark

4.5.3
) and whose restricted universal Massey product of
length
d+2
is
x
. We make the following observations:

•

The class
x
lies in the image of the restriction map

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

induced by the inclusion
j\colon\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Lambda(\sigma,d)
of the
degree
0
part. Indeed, in view of the long exact sequence described in

Proposition

4.7.11
, it is enough to check that

\sigma_{*}^{-1}\sigma^{*}(x)=x
; this can be shown exactly as in the proof of

[
Mur22
, Cor. 7.7]
, that is using
[
Mur22
, Prop. 7.6]
for
n=d+2
,
replacing
\sigma
by
\sigma^{-1}
and keeping in mind that the extension
of
\sigma^{-1}
acts on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{-d}={}_{\sigma^{-1}}{\Lambda}_{1}
by

(-1)^{-d}\sigma^{-1}=(-1)^{(d+2)^{2}}\sigma^{-1}.

•

There exists a unique
u\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
such that

j^{*}(u)=x
and
\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(u\right)=0
. To prove the existence of such a class
u
,
notice that if
j^{*}(u)=x
, then also

j^{*}(u+\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot y)=x

for arbitrary
y\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+1,-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
; this follows from the
fact that
\Lambda
is concentrated in degree
0
and therefore

j^{*}\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}=0
. Arguing as in the proof of

[
Mur22
, Prop. 7.9]
, one shows that one can choose an appropriate
y

such that
\operatorname{Sq}\!\left(u+\left\{\delta_{/d}\right\}\cdot y\right)=0
(in the argument, one must
replace
[
Mur22
, Props. 3.6, 3.9 and 3.10]
by

Proposition

4.7.8
and
[
Mur22
, Lemma 6.8]
by

Proposition

4.7.15
). The proof that such a pre-image
u
is unique
is completely analogous to that of
[
Mur22
, Prop. 7.10]
, replacing

[
Mur22
, Prop. 3.6]
by the last equation in
Proposition

4.7.8

and
[
Mur22
, Lemma 6.8]
by
Proposition

4.7.15
.

•

Since the graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is
d
-sparse, tautologically, a
minimal
A_{d+2}
-algebra structure
x_{d}\in X_{d}
is simply a Hochschild
cochain

m_{d+2}\in\operatorname{C}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),

which we choose to be a representing cocycle of the unique class

u\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\quad\text{with}\quad\operatorname{Sq}(u)=0\quad\text{and}\quad j^{*}(u)=x

(compare with
Proposition

4.4.4
). The fact that
m_{d+2}

is a cocycle implies that it extends to an
A_{d+3}
-algebra structure on

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
choosing
m_{d+3}
arbitrarily, e.g.
m_{d+3}=0
(this is
actually the only possible choice if
d>1
). This is immediate from the
definition of an
A_{\infty}
-algebra, see
[
Kel01
, Sec. 3.1]
. Thus, there
exists a point
x_{d+1}\in X_{d+1}
that maps to
x_{d}
under the fibration

X_{d+1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{d}
. Moreover, the same argument shows that we
can extend the
A_{d+3}
-algebra structure to an
A_{2d+2}
-algebra
structure, necessarily setting
m_{i+2}=0
for
d<i<2d
and choosing

m_{2d+2}
arbitrarily, for example
m_{2d+2}=0
. Hence we have
x_{2d}\in X_{2d}

that maps to
x_{d}
under the fibration
X_{2d}\twoheadrightarrow X_{d}
. In
other words, the Hochschild cocycle
m_{d+2}
with
\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}=u
gives
rise to a
d
-sparse minimal
A_{d+2}
-algebra
(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2})
which
can be extended to a
d
-sparse minimal
A_{2d+2}
-algebra

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},0)
, with all other operations being trivial due to the
fact that
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is
d
-sparse. Notice, however, that

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},0,0,\ldots)

need not
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
since, although
\operatorname{Sq}(\left\{m_{d+2}\right\})=\operatorname{Sq}(u)=0
, the Gerstenhaber square of

m_{d+2}
need not vanish at the cochain level.

•

The condition
\operatorname{Sq}(u)=0
is equivalent to the existence of an

A_{2d+3}
-algebra structure on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
with the same underlying

A_{d+2}
-algebra as above. In other words,
\operatorname{Sq}(u)=0
if and only if there
exists a point
y_{2d+1}\in X_{2d+1}
such that
y_{2d+1}\mapsto x_{d}
. This
can be shown exactly as in the proof of
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 6.7]
, since the
relevant results
[
Mur20a
, Props. 3.4 and 5.4]
are not particular to the
case
d=1
. Indeed, in the notation of
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 5.4]
we have

k=2d
and
l=2d-1
, the recipient of the obstruction is

E^{k+1,k}_{k+1-l}=E^{2d+1,2d}_{2}=\operatorname{HH}^{2d+3,-2d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
, and the
obstruction is
\operatorname{Sq}(u)
by
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 3.4]
(here, only the last
summand counts, since the other ones correspond to the elements
m_{i+2}=0
,

d<i<2d
, which vanish since
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is
d
-sparse, as remarked in the
previous item). In other words, we have obtained a
d
-sparse minimal

A_{2d+3}
-algebra
(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2})
with
\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}=u

and whose operation of top arity
2d+3
must vanish since
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is

d
-sparse.

Let
j\geq d
and suppose that we have constructed points
x_{i}\in X_{i}
,
d\leq i\leq j
, as well as an auxiliary point
y_{j+d+1}\in X_{j+d+1}
that map to
one another under the bonding maps in the tower:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j+d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j+d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists y_{j+d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{j+d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}

(the base of the induction
j=d
was discussed above). Since

E_{d+2}^{j+d+2,j+d+1}=0
by
Proposition

5.2.9
, the obstruction for
the existence of a point

y_{j+d+2}\in X_{j+d+2},\qquad y_{j+d+2}\longmapsto x_{j},

vanishes; therefore such a
y_{j+d+2}
exists and we define
x_{j+1}\in X_{j+1}
as its image. Here we use
[
Mur20a
, Prop. 5.4]
for
k=j+d+1
and

l=j
. This process results in a sequence
\left\{x_{i}\in X_{i}\right|i\geq d\}
that
defines a point
x_{\infty}\in X_{\infty}
in the (homotopy) limit with

x_{\infty}\mapsto x_{d}
, that is a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the
graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
whose restricted universal Massey product is

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}\right\}=x
. This finishes the proof of statement
(
2
).

Proof of statement (
1
):
Let

x_{\infty}
and
y_{\infty}
be
A_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
with
universal Massey products
\left\{m_{d+2}^{x_{\infty}}\right\}
and

\left\{m_{d+2}^{y_{\infty}}\right\}
, respectively. We need to prove that, if there
is an equality of restricted universal Massey products

j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}^{y_{\infty}}\right\}=j^{*}\left\{m_{d+2}^{x_{\infty}}\right\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),

then
x_{\infty}
and
y_{\infty}
lie in the same connected component of

X_{\infty}
. The claim follows from
B
since we
have

\operatorname{Sq}(\left\{m_{d+2}^{x_{\infty}}\right\})=0=\operatorname{Sq}(\left\{m_{d+2}^{y_{\infty}}\right\})

and therefore
\left\{m_{d+2}^{y_{\infty}}\right\}=\left\{m_{d+2}^{x_{\infty}}\right\}
(as
explained in the proof of statement (
2
)). This
finishes the proof of statement (
1
).
∎

We are finally ready to prove
Theorem

5.1.2
, the last logical step
for establishing
A
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.1.2
.

The theorem is a direct
consequence of
Theorem

5.3.3
(
3
). Indeed,

Theorem

5.1.2
(
1
), that is the existence of an

A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the
d
-sparse graded algebra
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
whose
restricted universal Massey product is a unit in Hochschild–Tate cohomology,
follows immediately from the fact that the quotient

\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)/\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
is non-empty. Finally,

Theorem

5.1.2
(
2
), that is the essential
uniqueness of such
A_{\infty}
-algebra structures, is readily seen to be
equivalent to the fact that the quotient

\operatorname{S}^{\simeq}_{d}(\Lambda,\sigma)/\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
has exactly one element.
∎

5.4.
Strong differential graded enhancements

The following definition extends that of a ‘strong enhancement’ of a
triangulated category (the case
d=1
) introduced by Lunts and Orlov
in
[
LO10
]
to the setting of
(d+2)
-angulated categories.

5.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
(d+2)
-angulated category.

(1)

A
strong (DG) enhancement of
\mathcal{F}

is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
consisting of a pre-
(d+2)
-angulated DG category
\mathcal{A}
and a

\mathbf{k}
-linear equivalence of
(d+2)
-angulated categories

\varphi\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}.

(2)

Two strong enhancements
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
and
(\mathcal{B},\psi)
of
\mathcal{F}
are

equivalent
if there exists a quasi-equivalence of DG categories

F\colon\mathcal{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{B}
such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

commutes up to (unspecified) natural isomorphism.

We say that
\mathcal{F}

admits a unique strong (DG)
enhancement
6
6

6

In this case one also says that
\mathcal{T}
‘admits a strongly unique enhancement.’
if any two
strong enhancements of
\mathcal{F}
are equivalent.

In the case
d=1
of triangulated categories (with split idempotents),
Definition

5.4.1
admits the
following equivalent formulation.

5.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category with split idempotents.

(1)

A
strong (DG) enhancement of
\mathcal{T}

is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
consisting of a DG category
\mathcal{A}
and a

\mathbf{k}
-linear equivalence of triangulated categories

\varphi\colon\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{T}.

(2)

Two strong enhancements
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
and
(\mathcal{B},\psi)
of
\mathcal{T}
are

equivalent
if there exists a Morita equivalence of DG categories

F\colon\mathcal{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{B}
such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}F_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

commutes up to (unspecified) natural isomorphism.

In order to investigate strong enhancements of
(d+2)
-angulated categories, we extend the notion to pairs.

5.4.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)
be a pair consisting of an additive category and a
self-equivalence, that we often omit from the notation.

(1)

A
strong
(d+2)
-angulated (DG) enhancement of
\mathcal{C}

is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
consisting of a pre-
(d+2)
-angulated DG category
\mathcal{A}
and a

\mathbf{k}
-linear equivalence of pairs

\varphi\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}.

(2)

Two strong enhancements
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
and
(\mathcal{B},\psi)
of
\mathcal{C}
are

equivalent
if there exists a quasi-equivalence of DG categories

F\colon\mathcal{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{B}
such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

commutes up to (unspecified) natural isomorphism.

5.4.4
.

For
d=1
,
Definition

5.4.3
also admits an equivalent formulation in
terms of perfect derived categories, compare

Definitions

5.4.1
and
5.4.2
.

5.4.5
.

A strong
(d+2)
-angulated enhancement of
(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)
induces a

(d+2)
-angulated structure and two equivalent enhancements induce

identical

(d+2)
-angulated structures, not just equivalent ones.
Therefore, the set of equivalence classes of strong enhancements of a

(d+2)
-angulated category
(\mathcal{C},\Sigma,{\pentagon})
is a subset of the set of
equivalence classes of strong
(d+2)
-angulated enhancements of
(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)
. Moreover, the latter can be partitioned by subsets of the former form as
{\pentagon}
runs over the set of
(d+2)
-angulated structures on the pair
(\mathcal{C},\Sigma)
.

Recall the standing assumptions of this section,

5.0.1
. We endow
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
with any of
the equivalent algebraic, that is AL
(d+2)
-angulated structures
{\pentagon}
with suspension
functor
\Sigma=-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}
, which we fix, see

Definition

2.2.17
,
Proposition

2.2.18
and
Remark

5.1.4
. Our aim
is to compute the set of equivalence classes of strong enhancements of the

(d+2)
-angulated category
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma,{\pentagon})
. For this, we
need some auxiliary definitions.

5.4.6
.

The
stable centre

\underline{Z}(\Lambda)
of
\Lambda
is the quotient
of the centre
Z(\Lambda)
by the ideal formed by the elements
a\in Z(\Lambda)
such that the
\Lambda
-bimodule morphism

\Lambda\to\Lambda,\quad x\mapsto xa,

factors through a projective-injective
\Lambda
-bimodule.

5.4.7
.

In the same way that the centre of
\Lambda
can be interpreted in terms of
Hochschild cohomology as
Z(\Lambda)=\operatorname{HH}^{0}\!\left(\Lambda\right)
, the stable centre is
interpreted in terms of Hochschild–Tate cohomology as

\underline{Z}(\Lambda)=\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{0}\!\left(\Lambda\right)=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\Lambda^{e}}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda\right).

We recall the following classical definition.

5.4.8
.

The
centre

Z(\mathcal{D})
of a
\mathbf{k}
-linear category
\mathcal{D}
is the endomorphism
algebra of the identity functor
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

5.4.9
.

There is a commutative square of algebra maps
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Z}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\operatorname{\underline{mod}}\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

where the top arrow is the well-known isomorphism that sends
a\in Z(\Lambda)

to the natural transformation with components

M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M,\quad x\mapsto xa,\qquad M\in\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda),

the left vertical arrow is the quotient map, and the right vertical arrow is
induced by the ideal quotient

\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)=\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda)/[\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)].

We denote by

\zeta^{\times}\colon\underline{Z}(\Lambda)^{\times}\longrightarrow Z(\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda))^{\times}

the morphism induced by
\zeta
between the corresponding groups of units.

5.4.10
.

Suppose that the algebra
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable and endow the pair
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
with an algebraic
(d+2)
-angulation
{\pentagon}
. The following
statements hold:

(1)

The set of equivalence classes of strong
(d+2)
-angulated enhancements
of the pair
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
is in bijection with the set

\underline{Z}(\Lambda)^{\times}
of units in the stable centre of
\Lambda
.

(2)

The subset of equivalence classes of strong enhancements of the

(d+2)
-angulated category
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma,{\pentagon})
is in
bijection with
\ker\zeta^{\times}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
be a strong enhancement of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma,{\pentagon})
, which is in particular a strong
(d+2)
-angulated enhancement of

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
. If
(\mathcal{B},\psi)
is another strong
(d+2)
-angulated enhancement of

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
, then
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are quasi-equivalent, were we use

Proposition

2.2.18
,

Theorem

5.1.3
and the separability
hypothesis. Therefore, without loss of generality, we may replace
\mathcal{B}
by
\mathcal{A}

and consider only strong enhancements of the form
(\mathcal{A},\psi)
.

A strong
(d+2)
-angulated enhancement of the form
(\mathcal{A},\psi)
is equivalent
to
(\mathcal{A},\xi\varphi)
for
\xi=\psi\varphi^{-1}
, where
\varphi^{-1}
is a
choice of quasi-inverse of the equivalence of triangulated categories

\varphi
; indeed, the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}=H^{0}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

commutes up to natural isomorphism. Moreover, given two self-equivalences

\xi_{1},\ \xi_{2}
of
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
, the strong
(d+2)
-angulated
enhancements
(\mathcal{A},\xi_{1}\varphi)
and
(\mathcal{A},\xi_{2}\varphi)
are equivalent if and
only if there exists an automorphism
F
of
\mathcal{A}
in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
such that
the following square commutes up to natural isomorphism
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{2}^{-1}\xi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

In this context, talking about the self-equivalence of
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
induced by an automorphism
F
of
\mathcal{A}
in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
makes sense because any such automorphism is represented by a zig-zag of quasi-equivalences

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{(0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(n-1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}^{(n)}=\mathcal{A}.

Here, taking cohomology we obtain a zig-zag of equivalences of pairs

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})=H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{(0)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{(1)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{(n-1)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)})=H^{0}(\mathcal{A}).

We can then take quasi-inverses of arrows pointing backwards

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})=H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{(0)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{(1)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{(n-1)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)})=H^{0}(\mathcal{A})

and the composite is well defined up to natural isomorphism.
We conclude that the set of equivalence classes of strong
(d+2)
-angulated
enhancements of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
is in bijection with the set of left
cosets of the group of natural isomorphism classes of self-equivalences of

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
modulo the subgroup defined by the
self-equivalences induced by an automorphism of
\mathcal{A}
in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
, see

Section

3.1.2
.

Recall that the group of self-equivalences of the category
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)

modulo natural isomorphisms—the Picard group of
\Lambda
—identifies with
the outer automorphism group
\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda)
, see

Section

2.2.2
. Similarly, the group of self-equivalences of the
pair
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
modulo natural isomorphisms identifies with
the outer automorphism group
\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
of the graded algebra

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
. We now investigate the self-equivalences of
\mathcal{A}
. We use
homotopical techniques that have also been used in
[
Mur22
, Sec. 8]
with
similar purposes.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{A}
be the full DG subcategory spanned by the objects which are
basic additive generators of
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\simeq\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
. Denote by

\operatorname{dgcat}_{\mathcal{A}},\operatorname{dgcat}_{\mathcal{B}}\subset\operatorname{dgcat}
the non-full (!) subcategories whose objects are the DG categories which are quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively, and whose morphisms are quasi-equivalences.

The following argument is based in the proof of
[
Mur22
, Prop. 8.5]
.
Consider the functors
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dgcat}_{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dgcat}_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

defined as follows:

•

The functor
R
is the homotopy Karoubi envelope (defined for example in

[
GHW21
, Sec. 4]
), which is an endofunctor of
\operatorname{dgcat}
.

•

The functor
j
takes a DG category
\mathcal{D}
quasi-equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
to
its full DG subcategory spanned by the objects that are basic additive
generators in
H^{0}(\mathcal{D})\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\simeq\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
.

For an arbitrary DG category
\mathcal{D}
, there is a natural functor
\mathcal{D}\to R(\mathcal{D})

that is a quasi-equivalence if and only if
H^{0}(\mathcal{D})
is idempotent
complete. If
\mathcal{D}
is quasi-equivalent to
\mathcal{B}
, then the previous canonical
functor corestricts to a natural quasi-equivalence
\mathcal{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}jR(\mathcal{D})
.
If
\mathcal{D}
is quasi-equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
, then there is a zig-zag of natural quasi-equivalences

Rj(\mathcal{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R(\mathcal{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{D}.

This proves that
R
and
j
induce inverse homotopy equivalences between the
classifying spaces of the categories
\operatorname{dgcat}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\operatorname{dgcat}_{\mathcal{B}}
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\operatorname{dgcat}_{\mathcal{B}}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\operatorname{dgcat}_{\mathcal{A}}|.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|R|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|j|}

Taking fundamental groups (these spaces are connected by construction) we obtain an
isomorphism between the automorphism groups of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{A}
be an object such that
\varphi(c)\cong\Lambda
in

\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
; in particular,
c\in\mathcal{B}
. Since all objects in

H^{0}(\mathcal{B})
are isomorphic, the homotopy fiber sequence in the proof of

[
TV08
, Prop. 2.3.3.5]
gives an isomorphism between the automorphism group
of
\mathcal{B}
in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
and the automorphism group of
\mathcal{B}(c,c)
in

\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgalg}\right)
, the homotopy category of the model category of DG algebras with
quasi-isomorphisms as weak equivalences and surjections as fibrations.

A choice of isomorphism
\varphi(c)\cong\Lambda
in
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
induces
an isomorphism of graded algebras
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}(c,c))\cong\Lambda(\sigma,d)
. Fix
a minimal model

(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)

of the DG algebra
\mathcal{B}(c,c)
. The automorphism group of
\mathcal{B}(c,c)
in

\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgalg}\right)
is isomorphic to the group of homotopy classes of

A_{\infty}
-automorphisms of this minimal model (an
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism of
minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebras is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism
F
such that
F_{1}
is
an isomorphism); this follows from
[
LH
, Cor. 1.3.1.3]
(despite the
fact that only non-unital DG algebras are considered in
[
LH
]
, the
claim is still valid by
[
Mur14
, Prop. 6.2]
).

An
A_{\infty}
-automorphism
F
of
(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)
is the same as an
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism

\displaystyle G\colon(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2},m_{2d+2},\dots)*F_{1}

\displaystyle=(\Lambda(\sigma,d),m_{d+2}^{F_{1}},m_{2d+2}^{F_{1}},\dots)

with
G_{1}=\mathbf{1}
and
G_{n}=F_{1}^{-1}F_{n}
, see
5.1.1
and compare with
the proof of
Theorem

5.1.3
.
By
Theorem

5.3.3
(
1
), such an

A_{\infty}
-isomorphism exists if and only if there is an equality of
restricted universal Massey products

j^{*}\{m_{d+2}\}=j^{*}\{m_{d+2}^{F_{1}}\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right).

Moreover, using the right conjugation action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
on
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
that appeared in
Remark

4.7.10
,

j^{*}\{m_{d+2}^{F_{1}}\}=j^{*}\{m_{d+2}\}^{F_{1}}.

Hence, if
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
is now the isotropy group of this action at

j^{*}\{m_{d+2}\}
and
H\subset\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
is the image of
G
along the
quotient map

\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)),

the set of cosets we
wish to describe is
\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))/H
.

The right conjugation action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)

factors through
\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
(
Proposition

4.4.2
).
Since
j^{*}\{m_{d+2}\}
is in the image of the
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
-equivariant
morphism

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda(\sigma,d),\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right),

we deduce that
G
contains all inner automorphisms of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
and, therefore,
\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))/H
is in bijection with
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))/G
.

By
Proposition

4.7.16
,
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))/G
is in
bijection with the edge units of
\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
, that is
with the Hochschild cohomology classes in bidegree
(d+2,-d)
that become
units in the Hochschild–Tate cohomology
\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)
. These
clases are in (non-canonical) bijection with the units in

\underline{\operatorname{HH}}^{0,0}\!\left(\Lambda,\Lambda(\sigma,d)\right)=\underline{Z}(\Lambda),

since the latter
group of units acts freely and transitively on the set of edge units of
bidegree
(d+2,-d)
. An explicit bijection between
\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))/G
and

\underline{Z}(\Lambda)^{\times}
is induced by the principal crossed
homomorphism

\displaystyle\chi\colon\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\underline{Z}(\Lambda)^{\times},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\longmapsto j^{*}\{m_{d+2}\}^{g}j^{*}\{m_{d+2}\}^{-1}

whose kernel is
\ker\chi=G
.
This proves statement (
1
).

To prove statement (
2
) we have to identify the image along
\chi
of the subgroup
K\subset\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
corresponding to the self-equivalences of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
which preserve the
(d+2)
-angulated structure
{\pentagon}
.

The subgroup
K
contains
G
since the latter is the subgroup of

\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
formed by the automorphisms of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
induced by an
automorphism of the pre-
(d+2)
-angulated DG category
\mathcal{A}
in
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\operatorname{dgcat}\right)
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is an enhancement of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma,{\pentagon})
, the
self-equivalences of the pair
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\simeq(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)

induced by the automorphisms of
\mathcal{A}
also preserve the
(d+2)
-angulated
structure
{\pentagon}
, that is
G\subset K
. Since
G=\ker\chi
we deduce that
K=\chi^{-1}(\chi(K))
(this is well known for group homomorphisms and easy to deduce for crossed homomorphisms).

Recall that the automorphisms of
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
introduced in
Remark

4.7.10
define a group homomorphism

\displaystyle\gamma\colon Z(\Lambda)^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)),

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\longmapsto g_{u}.

The composite
\chi\gamma
is the surjective group homomorphism

Z(\Lambda)^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow\underline{Z}(\Lambda)^{\times}
induced by
the natural projection
Z(\Lambda)\twoheadrightarrow\underline{Z}(\Lambda)

(the former homomorphism is surjective by
[
Che21
, Cor. 2.3]
). Since
K=\chi^{-1}(\chi(K))
and
\chi\gamma
is surjective,

\chi(K\cap\gamma(Z(\Lambda)^{\times}))=\chi(K)\cap\chi\gamma(Z(\Lambda)^{\times})=\chi(K).

Hence,

\chi(K)\subset\underline{Z}(\Lambda)^{\times}
consists of the classes of
those units
u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
such that
g_{u}
preserves the

(d+2)
-angulation
{\pentagon}
. The self-equivalence of

(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)
induced by
g_{u}
,
u\in Z(\Lambda)^{\times}
, is

(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},u)
(considered already in the proof of
Proposition

2.2.18
). Therefore, in order to finish the proof we have to check that
(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},u)
preserves
{\pentagon}
if and only if
[u]\in\ker\zeta^{\times}
.

First, assume
[u]\in\ker\zeta^{\times}
. We have to show that, for every
(d+2)
-angle

x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{3}\to\cdots\to x_{d+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{d+2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma(x_{1})

in
{\pentagon}
,
the sequence

x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{3}\to\cdots\to x_{d+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle uf_{d+2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma(x_{1})

is also in
{\pentagon}
; notice that we can in fact place the multiplication
by
u
in any given arrow, since the resulting sequences are isomorphic. We
therefore consider the sequence

x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle uf_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{3}\to\cdots\to x_{d+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{d+2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma(x_{1})

and show that it is isomorphic to the first diagram. This will suffice by (

d

-TR1
). We factor
f_{1}
in
\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda)
as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

and consider the following commutative square of solid arrows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

Since
[u]\in\ker\zeta^{\times}
, multiplication by
u
induces the identity in the stable module category
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)
for evey finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-module. This implies the existence of a dashed arrow
\alpha
such that

\alpha i=u-\mathbf{1}_{M}.

An easy computation shows that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(x_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(x_{1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{x_{2}}+i\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uf_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d+2}}

The arrow
\mathbf{1}_{x_{2}}+i\alpha
is an isomorphism by the five lemma, which is
valid in the setting of
(d+2)
-angulated categories by (

d

-TR2
) and
[
GKO13
, Prop. 2.5(a)]
.

Assume now that
(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},u)
preserves the
(d+2)
-angulation

{\pentagon}
. Let
M
be a finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-module. Recall that
\Sigma\coloneqq-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}
. There exists a
(d+2)
-angle in
{\pentagon}

x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{3}\to\cdots\to x_{d+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{d+2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma(x_{1})

such that
f_{d+2}
factors as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(x_{1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(i)}

in
\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda)
.
Indeed, choose an epimorphism
p\colon x_{d+2}\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma M
with projective source and a monomorphism
i\colon M\hookrightarrow x_{1}
with injective target; define
f_{d+2}=\Sigma(i)p
and then apply (

d

-TR1
) and (

d

-TR2
).
The previous factorisation and
(d+2)
-angle induce an isomorphism in
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)
,

\omega\colon\Sigma M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega^{-(d+2)}M.

Since
(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},u)
preserves
{\pentagon}
, the following diagram is also in
{\pentagon}

x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}x_{3}\to\cdots\to x_{d+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle uf_{d+2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma(x_{1}).

We can factor
f_{d+2}
as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{d+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(x_{1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces up}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(i)}

The natural isomorphism in
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)
induced by this factorisation and the previous diagram is

u\omega\colon\Sigma M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega^{-(d+2)}M.

By
Proposition

2.1.8
(
3
) both isomorphisms in
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)

must coincide:

\omega=u\omega.

Therefore, the isomorphism
u\colon M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M
in

\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)
represented by multiplication by
u
must be the identity
for every
M\in\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda)
. This shows that
[u]\in\ker\zeta^{\times}
, as
required. This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.4.11
.

It is an interesting problem to study the set of equivalence classes of strong
enhancements of an algebraic triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
with
finite-dimensional morphism spaces, split idempotents, and a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster
tilting object
c\in\mathcal{T}
.
Theorem

5.4.10
provides a complete
answer in the case
d=1
when
\operatorname{add}(c)=\mathcal{T}
. In general, there is an apparent
map (given by restriction) from the set of equivalence classes of strong
enhancements of the ambient triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
to the set of
equivalence classes of strong enhancements of the standard
(d+2)
-angulated
category
(\operatorname{add}(c),[d],{\pentagon})
. We do not know, however, whether this map
is injective or
surjective.

The following result provides, in particular, the simplest example of an
algebraic triangulated category with a unique enhancement but with non-unique
strong enhancements.

5.4.12
.

Let
\Lambda=\mathbf{k}[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{2})
be the algebra of dual numbers. The following statements hold:

(1)

The algebra
\Lambda
is connected and twisted
(d+2)
-periodic for every
d\geq-1
and
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable, hence
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
is algebraic
(d+2)
-angulated for every
d\geq 1
and there is only one choice of suspension functor.

(2)

If
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})\neq 2
and
d\geq 1
, then every algebraic
(d+2)
-angulated structure on

\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
admits a unique strong enhancement.

(3)

If
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})=2
and
d\geq 1
, then for every algebraic
(d+2)
-angulated structure on

\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
the set of equivalence classes of strong
enhancements is in bijection with the elements of the ground field
\mathbf{k}
,
and therefore
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
does not admit a unique strong enhancement.

Proof.

(
1
) The algebra
\Lambda
is obviously connected and
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}=\Lambda/(\varepsilon)=\mathbf{k}
is separable. The algebra
\Lambda
is also twisted
1
-periodic, as witnessed by the short exact sequence
of
\Lambda
-bimodules

0\longrightarrow{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda\otimes\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mu
is the multiplication map, the automorphism
\sigma
is given by

\sigma(\varepsilon)=-\varepsilon
, and

\nu(1)=1\otimes\varepsilon-\varepsilon\otimes 1.

Therefore,
\Lambda
is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic for every
d\geq-1
. By

Theorem

2.2.20
, the category
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
is
(d+2)
-angulated for any
d\geq 1
. Indeed, by
Theorem

2.2.15
,
Proposition

2.2.6
and
Corollaries

4.5.17
and
4.5.20
it has a unique AL
(d+2)
-angulated structure, up to equivalence, and the only choice of suspension functor is
\Sigma=-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{\sigma}{\Lambda}_{1}
. Algebraic and AL
(d+2)
-angulated structures are the same thing (
Remark

5.1.4
). Now, the first claim follows.

(
2
) and
(
3
)
In view of
Theorem

5.4.10
, we need a set-theoretic description
of the kernel of the canonical morphism

\zeta^{\times}\colon\underline{Z}(\Lambda)^{\times}\longrightarrow Z(\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda))^{\times}.

Clearly
Z(\Lambda)=\Lambda
and the stable category

\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)\simeq\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})

has centre isomorphic to the ground field
\mathbf{k}
. Notice that all

\Lambda
-bimodule morphisms
\Lambda\to\Lambda\otimes\Lambda
are of the form

1\mapsto\alpha(1\otimes\varepsilon+\varepsilon\otimes 1)+\beta(\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon),\qquad\alpha,\ \beta\in\mathbf{k}.

The composition of such a
\Lambda
-bimodule morphism with
\mu
satisfies

1\mapsto 2\alpha\varepsilon.

Since
\mu
is a surjection from a projective
\Lambda
-bimodule, the stable
centre is the quotient of
Z(\Lambda)
by the ideal
(2\varepsilon)
. Hence,
if
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})\neq 2
, then
\zeta
is an isomorphism. On the other hand, if

\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})=2
then
\zeta\colon\Lambda\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{k}
is the natural
projection and

\ker\zeta^{\times}=1+(\varepsilon)=\left\{1+\alpha\varepsilon\right|\alpha\in\mathbf{k}\}.

The two last claims now follow from
Theorem

5.4.10
.
∎

5.4.13
.

Let
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})=2
and
\Lambda=\mathbf{k}[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{2})
the algebra
of dual numbers. Following the proof of
Theorem

5.4.10
and
Corollary

5.4.12
, we give an explicit description of the strong enhancements of
the triangulated category
\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)
(for any triangulated structure, all of them are algebraic).

Since
\operatorname{char}(\mathbf{k})=2

the suspension functor must be
\Sigma=\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)}
(see the
proof of
Corollary

5.4.12
). Moreover, the
stable category
\operatorname{\underline{mod}}(\Lambda)
is equivalent to
\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})
and

\Omega=\Sigma=\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})}
.
Proposition

2.1.8
establishes a
bijection between the triangulated structures on
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma)

and the set
\mathbf{k}^{\times}
of units of the ground field. It is straightforward
to verify that the triangulated structure
{\pentagon}_{\alpha}
corresponding to

\alpha\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
is characterised by the fact that the triangle

\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda

belongs to
{\pentagon}_{\alpha}
, compare with
[
MSS07
, Rmk. 8]
.

Consider the DG algebra with underlying graded algebra

A=\frac{k[u,v^{\pm 1}]\langle a\rangle}{(a^{2},av+va,au+ua+1)},\qquad|u|=0,\quad|v|=1,\quad|a|=0,

and differential

\displaystyle d(u)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle d(v)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle d(a)

\displaystyle=u^{2}v.

It is proved
in
[
MSS07
, Rmk. 8]
that there is an equivalence of pairs

\displaystyle\varphi\colon\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(A)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\longmapsto H^{0}(M),

which is a triangulated equivalence if we endow the target with the triangulated structure
{\pentagon}_{1}
. For any
\alpha\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
, we have a triangulated equivalence

(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},\alpha)\colon(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma,{\pentagon}_{1})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma,{\pentagon}_{\alpha})

of the kind considered in the proofs of
Proposition

2.2.18
and
Corollary

5.4.12
.
Given
\beta\in\mathbf{k}
we consider the self-equivalence
(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},1+\beta\varepsilon)
and define

\varphi_{\alpha,\beta}=(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},1+\beta\varepsilon)(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},\alpha)\varphi=(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda)},(1+\beta\varepsilon)\alpha)\varphi.

The map

\beta\longmapsto(A,\varphi_{\alpha,\beta})

realises the bijection between
\mathbf{k}
and the set of equivalence classes of
strong enhancements of
(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),\Sigma,{\pentagon}_{\alpha})
given by

Corollary

5.4.12
.

5.5.
Comments on the (non-)uniqueness of enhancements

We wish to illustrate the necessity of the assumptions in

Theorems

5.1.3
,
5.1.8
and
5.4.10
by analysing the case when

\Lambda=\mathbf{k}
is an arbitrary field, which is the most basic example of a
periodic algebra of any period
d\geq 1
. Thus, let
d\geq 1
and
\sigma=\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}
so that

\Lambda(\sigma,d)=\mathbf{k}\langle\imath^{\pm}\rangle,\qquad|\imath|=-d,

is the algebra of Laurent polynomials in a single variable of degree
-d
, which
we view as a DG algebra with vanishing differential (this algebra is considered
implicitly in
[
Lad
, Ex. 3.30]
and explicitly in
[
Lor
, Ex. 6.7]
,
see also
Remark

5.5.2
).

By definition, a DG
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-module
M=(V,\varphi)
consists of a cochain
complex of vector spaces
V
equipped with an invertible degree
-d
cocycle

\varphi\in\operatorname{Z}^{-d}(\operatorname{hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,V\right))
that we interpret as an isomorphism

\varphi\colon V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}V[-d]
. Similarly, a morphism of DG

\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-modules
f\colon(V,\varphi)\to(W,\psi)
is a morphism of cochain
complexes of vector spaces
f\colon V\to W
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

commutes. In particular, such an
f
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if the
induced linear map

H^{i}(f)\colon H^{i}(V)\longrightarrow H^{i}(W)

is an isomorphism for all
0\leq i<d
. We claim that functor

\operatorname{D}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\longrightarrow\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{Mod}(\mathbf{k}),\qquad M\longmapsto(H^{0}(M),H^{1}(M),\dots,H^{d-1}(M)),

is an equivalence of (ordinary) categories (although this fact is well known to
experts, we give a proof for the convenience of the reader). For this, notice
that there is an apparent fully faithful functor

\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{Mod}(\mathbf{k})\longrightarrow\operatorname{dgMod}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))

which sends a tuple
(V^{0},V^{1},\dots,V^{d-1})
to the DG
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-module with trivial differential

(V,\mathbf{1}_{V})
where

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{d-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{d-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Notice that the composite

\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{Mod}(\mathbf{k})\longrightarrow\operatorname{dgMod}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\longrightarrow H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)))

is also fully faithful. To complete the proof, it is enough to observe the following:

•

Every DG
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-module is homotopy equivalent to its cohomology
(viewed as a DG
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-module in the obvious way).

•

A morphism of DG
\Lambda(\sigma,d)
-modules is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
it is a homotopy equivalence and therefore

H^{0}(\operatorname{dgMod}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)))=\operatorname{D}(\Lambda(\sigma,d)).

•

The composite

\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{Mod}(\mathbf{k})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\longrightarrow\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{Mod}(\mathbf{k})

is isomorphic to the identity functor.

The first claim is obvious and the second and third claims can be shown exactly
as in the case of cochain complexes of vector spaces, observing that the
category
\operatorname{dgMod}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
is equivalent to the category of
d
-periodic
cochain complexes of vector spaces. Under the above equivalence, the shift
functor on
\operatorname{D}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
corresponds to the automorphism

(V^{0},V^{1},\dots,V^{d-1})\longmapsto(V^{1},\dots,V^{d-1},V^{0}).

of
\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{Mod}(\mathbf{k})
. Since the graded algebra

H^{\bullet}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))=\Lambda(\sigma,d)
is finite-dimensional in each degree and

H^{i}(\Lambda(\sigma,d){[-j]})=\Lambda(\sigma,d)^{i-j}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{k}&i=j,\\
0&i\neq j,\end{cases}\qquad 0\leq i,j<d,

it is clear that the functor

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\longrightarrow\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k}),\qquad M\longmapsto(H^{0}(M),H^{1}(M),\dots,H^{d-1}(M)),

is also an equivalence of (ordinary) categories. Finally, the free DG module

{\Lambda(\sigma,d)\in\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))}
is a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object, since
this is obviously the case for its image

(\mathbf{k},0,\dots,0)\in\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k}).

5.5.1
.

According to
Theorem

2.2.1
, the additive category

\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})\simeq\operatorname{add}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\subseteq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))

admits a
(d+2)
-angulated structure with suspension functor
\Sigma=\mathbf{1}
(up
to natural isomorphism, the only autoequivalence of
\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})
) whose

(d+2)
-angles are induced by the triangles in
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
. In
fact,
\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})
admits a unique
(d+2)
-angulation: A
(d+2)
-angle

V_{1}\to V_{2}\to\cdots\to V_{d+2}\to V_{1}

in
\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})
must yield a
(d+2)
-periodic exact sequence

\cdots\to V_{d+2}\to V_{1}\to V_{2}\to\cdots\to V_{d+2}\to V_{1}\to\cdots

(see
[
GKO13
, Prop. 2.5]
) and, conversely, every
(d+2)
-angulation must
contain all such sequences for these are isomorphic to a finite direct sum of
rotations of the trivial sequence

\mathbf{k}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{k}\to 0\to\cdots\to 0\to\mathbf{k}

with
d
zeroes. Moreover,
\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})
has a unique DG-enhancement, namely the graded category
\operatorname{add}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
endowed with the trivial differential.

5.5.2
.

The above discussion and
Theorem

5.1.6
show that the triangulated category
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
is
equivalent to the
d
-cluster category of
\mathbf{k}
, that is the orbit
category
[
Kel05a
]

\mathcal{C}_{d}(\mathbf{k})\coloneqq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k}))/[d].

Indeed,
\mathcal{C}_{d}(\mathbf{k})
is an algebraic triangulated category with
[d]=\mathbf{1}
,
finite-dimensional morphism spaces, split idempotents and
\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{d}(\mathbf{k})
is a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster
tilting object with endomorphism algebra isomorphic to
\mathbf{k}
(alternatively,
one can also invoke the Recognition Theorem of Keller and Reiten
[
KR08
]
).

5.5.3
.

We record the following observation for the sake of completeness. The
triangulated category
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
admits an additive generator
that, under the identification

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))\simeq\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{mod}(\mathbf{k})
, is given by the
direct sum
\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d-1}\mathbf{k}(i)
, where

\mathbf{k}(i)\coloneqq(0,\dots,0,\mathbf{k},0,\dots,0)

with
\mathbf{k}
placed in the
i
-th coordinate. It is straightforward to verify
that the derived endomorphism algebra of this additive generator is
quasi-isomorphic to the graded algebra
\mathbf{k}Q/I
where
Q
is the graded
quiver
0
1
2
\cdots
d-1

with arrows
u\colon i\to i+1
of degree
-1
and
v\colon i+1\to i
of degree

1
, and
I
is the graded ideal generated by the relations
vu=e_{i}
and

uv=e_{i+1}
(with all indices modulo
d
). We leave the details to the
reader.

5.5.1.
Non-unique enhancements in the
\operatorname{Hom}
-infinite
case

Let
\ell
be a field and
\mathbf{k}=\ell(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{d-1})
; notice that
\mathbf{k}

is perfect if
\ell
has characteristic
0
. Rizzardo and Van den
Bergh
[
RVdB19
]
show that
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
, viewed as an
\ell
-linear
(!) algebraic triangulated category, admits two inequivalent enhancements. This
does not contradict
A
,
Theorem

5.1.8
or
Theorem

5.4.10
: as an
\ell
-linear
category, the morphism spaces in
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
are infinite dimensional
and therefore the hypotheses in
A
and
Theorems

5.1.8
and
5.4.10
are not
satisfied. Thus, this example explains why it is necessary to restrict to
triangulated categories with finite-dimensional morphism spaces.

5.5.2.
Non-unique enhancements for non-separable algebras

Although
A
requires the ground field
\mathbf{k}

to be perfect, in
Theorems

5.1.3
,
5.1.6
and
5.4.10
it is enough to
assume that
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable. We shall see that the latter
assumption is essential. Indeed, let
p>0
be a prime number and
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{F}_{p}(x)
;
thus,
\mathbf{k}
is not perfect. Let

\Lambda\coloneqq\mathbb{F}_{p}(x^{\frac{1}{p}})=\frac{\mathbf{k}[X]}{(X^{p}-x)},

which is a purely inseparable field extension of
\mathbf{k}
of degree
p
with
trivial Galois group; thus, in particular,
\Lambda=\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is not
separable over
\mathbf{k}
. Notice also that the algebras
\mathbf{k}
and
\Lambda
are
isomorphic over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, but not over
\mathbf{k}
. Moreover, there is an isomorphism
of
\mathbf{k}
-algebras

\Lambda^{e}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbf{k}}\Lambda^{\mathrm{op}}\cong\frac{\mathbf{k}[X,Y]}{(X^{p}-x,Y^{p}-x)}\cong\frac{\Lambda[Y]}{(Y-X)^{p}};

we treat the above isomorphism as an identification. Over
\Lambda
, we have
seen that the semisimple category
\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda)
admits a unique

(d+2)
-angulated structure (
Remark

5.5.1
), and the latter admits
a unique enhancement, represented by the graded category
\operatorname{add}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
with trivial differential. We shall see
that
\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda)
admits two inequivalent enhancements over the field

\mathbf{k}
. Indeed, notice that the diagonal
\Lambda
-bimodule has infinite
projective dimension and admits a
2
-periodic projective resolution

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y-X)^{p-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y-X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y-X)^{p-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y-X}

that is in fact
1
-periodic if
p=2
. Thus, for even
d
(or every
d\geq 1
if

p=2
), we obtain a non-trivial extension of length
d+2

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y-X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y-X)^{p-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y-X}

and we may consider the AL
(d+2)
-angulation associated to this extension
(
Theorem

2.2.15
). By
Theorem

5.1.3
, this
AL
(d+2)
-angulation admits an enhancement
\mathcal{B}
whose restricted universal
Massey product is represented, up to sign, by the class of the above non-trivial extension;
in particular,
\mathcal{B}
cannot be quasi-isomorphic to
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})
as DG categories
over
\mathbf{k}
and, therefore,
\mathcal{B}
cannot be quasi-equivalent to the enhancement
\operatorname{add}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
of

\operatorname{mod}(\Lambda)
. This does not
contradict
Theorem

5.1.3
: the algebra

\Lambda=\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is not separable as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra, and hence the
hypotheses in
Theorem

5.1.3
are not satisfied.

Moreover, just as in
[
RVdB20
]
,
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{B})
is equivalent to
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
as triangulated categories, but the DG enhancements
\mathcal{B}
and
\operatorname{add}(\Lambda(\sigma,d))
cannot be Morita equivalent. Indeed, if they were, the arguments in the proof of
Theorem

5.4.10
would show that they also have to be quasi-equivalent, and we have already argued that they are not. This proves that the separability hypothesis is necessary in
Theorems

5.1.8
and
5.4.10
.

5.5.3.
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting does not guarantee twisted

(d+2)
-periodicity

According to
Proposition

2.2.10
, the endomorphism algebra

\Lambda
of a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object in a triangulated category with
finite-dimensional morphism spaces is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic, provided that

\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable. This need not be the case if the algebra

\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is not separable, even if the ambient triangulated category
is algebraic. Indeed, as in
Section

5.5.2
above, let

p>0
be a prime number,
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{F}_{p}(x)
and

\Lambda=\mathbb{F}_{p}(x^{\frac{1}{p}})
. As explained in

Section

5.5
, the triangulated category

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda\langle\imath^{\pm}\rangle),\qquad|\imath|=-d,

is algebraic and admits a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object with endomorphism
algebra isomorphic to
\Lambda
(since
\Lambda
is itself a field). However, if

p\neq 2
and
d
is odd (in particular if
d=1
), then
\Lambda
is not twisted

(d+2)
-periodic as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra and, therefore, the restricted universal
Massey product associated to the canonical
\mathbf{k}
-linear (!) enhancement of

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda\langle\imath^{\pm}\rangle)
cannot be represented by a unit
in the Hochschild–Tate cohomology of
\Lambda
(viewed as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra).
This does not contradict
Proposition

2.2.10
nor the implication
(
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
) in

Corollary

4.5.17
, since
\Lambda=\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is not separable
as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra.

5.5.4.
Differential graded enhancements vs topological enhancements

Our results do not give any information on the (non-)existence of exotic

topoplogical
enhancements, that is enhancements given in terms of
spectral categories, say. We illustrate this point with the following example
